# Detailed curriculum: 1.6 Statistics and 2.2 Time series

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 1.6 Statistics (120 h) and item 2.2 Time series (60 h) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, topic exams, the rebuilt exit tests, reading and verification, and writes one lesson in full. The two items share one file because 2.2 is statistics on dependent data: its bands, tests, likelihoods and regressions are 1.6's tools with the independence assumption removed, and the sample lesson (1.6 lesson 2, the standard error) is the root of both.

## 1. Item overview

### 1.1 Decisions shared by both items

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, as in Stage 0, 1.1, 1.3 and 1.4 | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the app keeps hours tables comparable. Statistics screens carry simulations and tables that take longer to read than a tap |
| 2 | Display codes | **Two letters**: 1.6 uses ES, IT, RG, BY; 2.2 uses SM, VL, CO. Level RG3 is the third level of course RG | One letter, as the earlier files | Single letters are exhausted: Stage 0, 1.1, 1.3 and 1.4 use 25 of 26 (only J is free), and 1.1 already repeats Stage 0's Y, B and A. Two letters cannot run out. Ids, which learner progress refers to, do not depend on display codes (open item 1) |
| 3 | Simulation and grading | **A simulated number is never graded** (widget catalogue decision 3). Screens grade predictions made before a run, exact values from formulas, or computations on fixed data shown on screen | Grading a simulated coverage or p-value within a tolerance | A tolerance on a random estimate either fails honest learners on unlucky seeds or is too wide to teach |
| 4 | Data | **Seeded synthetic data by default**: returns, prices, pairs and regressions come from generators that know the truth; learner CSVs on the web app; no bundled market data (catalogue decision 11) | Bundling Yahoo or exchange data | Redistribution rights are unclear, and items must know the true parameter to say whether an interval covered it |
| 5 | Code and runtimes | **Every lesson screen runs on both targets** (authored code runs at build time). Drills carry `micropython-ok` (standard library plus the shipped `zqrandom` port of the engine's RNG and `math`), `ulab-ok` or `web` (NumPy, SciPy, statsmodels). Anything that must match statsmodels is `web`; the Artifact shows CI fixtures (catalogue decisions 5, 6, 7 and 10) | statsmodels everywhere; no code before the web app | Pyodide cannot run in the Artifact. A learner on a phone finishes every lesson; matching statsmodels waits for the web app, which is where the exits are run anyway |
| 6 | Transfer screens | **A quant or ML context wherever an honest one exists**, at least one per level: Sharpe ratios, alphas, betas, backtests, VaR, pairs, fills, order sizes | A finance story forced into every lesson | As 1.4 decision 11. Statistics has honest contexts everywhere; forced ones teach nothing |
| 7 | Proof standard | **DeGroot and Schervish's standard**: every level's homework has a long-form proof, derivation or written explanation with a 10-point rubric; exams give up to 30% of marks to long-form (the `topic` kind's ceiling) | No proofs (a recipe course); Casella and Berger's measure-free rigour throughout | Interviews and research ask why a formula holds (why $n - 1$, why the $t$, why HAC). Casella and Berger is the stretch text |
| 8 | Notation | $\theta$ a parameter, $\hat\theta$ an estimator; $\bar X$ random, $\bar x$ observed; $S^2$ with divisor $n - 1$, $\hat\sigma^2$ the MLE with divisor $n$; $p$ counts regression columns **including** the intercept, so residual degrees of freedom are $n - p$ and statsmodels' "Df Model" is $p - 1$; kurtosis is **Pearson's** (normal = 3) because statsmodels prints that, and excess kurtosis is always named as such; $\varphi$ for AR and $\theta$ for MA coefficients as in Tsay and FPP; $r_t$ log returns unless "simple" is said | Each source's own notation | One symbol, one meaning inside the app; lessons say once where DeGroot ($\bar X_n$), Hyndman ($\phi$, $\theta$ with FPP's sign convention) or statsmodels differ |
| 9 | Sample lesson | **1.6 lesson 2, "The standard error and the square-root law"** | 1.6 lesson 1 (a statistic is a random variable); 2.2 lesson 2 (stationarity) | It is the root of both items' dependency graphs: every interval, test statistic and "std err" column in 1.6, and the $\pm 1.96/\sqrt T$ bands, long-run variance and Newey-West errors of 2.2, are this lesson's $\sigma/\sqrt n$ generalised. Lesson 1 is its qualitative first half and has no derivation. It also carries the subject's most expensive trap (SD for SE) and its most important quant fact (a year of returns cannot pin down a mean) |

### 1.2 1.6 Statistics: scope, boundaries and decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | Spine | **DeGroot and Schervish, Probability and Statistics, 4th edition (2012)**, chapters 7 to 11 and section 12.6, as the curriculum names; **OpenIntro Statistics, 4th edition (free)** as the bridge for a learner who finds DeGroot steep; **MIT 18.650 Statistics for Applications (Fall 2016, Rigollet)** as the lecture parallel; **ISLP (the Python edition of ISLR, 2023) chapter 3** (free, 2.1's core text) for regression practice | Casella and Berger as spine; Wasserman as spine; OpenIntro only | The curriculum's pick; canon-gap keeps it. Casella and Berger assumes fluency the learner does not yet have; Wasserman is terse and is P5's spine. OpenIntro alone stops short of maximum likelihood and the theory the exit's "explain every number" needs |
| S2 | Number of courses | **Four**: ES Estimators and Their Errors (20 lessons), IT Intervals and Tests (22), RG Linear Regression (27), BY Bayesian Inference (11) | Six courses, one per catalogue outline section (1.6.1 to 1.6.6); three large courses | Four stories, each with a capstone level. Likelihood is the estimation story's second half, not a course of its own. BY at 11 lessons is under design research 8.2's 20-lesson guideline (open item 3) |
| S3 | Order | **Estimation, then intervals and tests, then regression, then Bayesian inference** | Bayesian first (MIT 18.05's order); Bayes interleaved with each topic | Credible against confidence intervals needs confidence intervals; Bayesian regression is ridge and needs regression; empirical-Bayes shrinkage closes the item and opens 2.1. The learner is not Bayes-naive: 1.5 taught Bayes' rule |
| S4 | Beyond the cover list | **A top-programme layer**, each one lesson: the empirical CDF and plug-in estimates (4), resistant estimators (8), method of moments (9), German tank (10), Fisher information (16), Cramér-Rao (17), MLE asymptotics (18), delta method and the Sharpe ratio's standard error (19), sufficiency (20), variance intervals (24), the bootstrap (25), Neyman-Pearson (36), likelihood-ratio tests (37), permutation tests (35), $\chi^2$ and KS tests (33, 34), FDR (40), forking paths (41), backtest overfitting (42), Gauss-Markov (47), HC and HAC errors (55, 56), influence (58), omitted-variable bias (61), VIF (62), logistic regression (65), model selection (64), posterior predictive (74), normal-normal (76), empirical Bayes (77), Bayesian ridge (78), Bayes factors (79), Metropolis (80) | The cover list only (estimators, bias and variance, maximum likelihood, confidence intervals, hypothesis tests, p-values, multiple testing, linear regression and its assumptions, Bayesian updating) | MIT 18.650 and DeGroot chapters 7 to 11 contain most of these; the rest are what a quant uses in the first month (the Sharpe ratio's standard error, HAC errors for overlapping returns, FDR for signal screens, the deflated Sharpe ratio). The exit itself demands Omnibus, Jarque-Bera, Durbin-Watson, AIC, BIC and the condition number (catalogue finding 0.2) |
| S5 | Canon-gap P5 (+25 h in canon-gap revision 2, which counts this file's in-budget core; +40 h in revision 1; pending the owner) | **Its core is inside 1.6's 120 hours**: delta method (19), bootstrap (25), permutation (35), FDR (40), logistic regression as maximum likelihood (65), resistant estimators (8). What waits for P5: GLMs beyond logistic, block bootstrap in depth, heavy-tail theory, Ledoit-Wolf beyond 1.4 lesson 95 | All of P5 inside 1.6; none of it | These six are one lesson each and are tested at interview; the rest needs P5's hours |
| S6 | The exit | **A whole level (RG5, 4 lessons) on the statsmodels summary**, every field recomputed and explained, plus the capstone drill and an exam item | One lesson on the table | The curriculum's exit is "explain every number"; the table has 25 numbers, 9 of them beyond the cover list |
| S7 | Exam | **`exm.stats`, topic kind**: 90 minutes, 20 items, 80 marks, long-form 18 marks (22.5%), the exit item inside | Finals kind (180 minutes) | ASSESSMENT.md assigns finals papers to 1.3 to 1.5 only; the 1.6 exit is a drill and an explanation, not a university final |
| S8 | Exit rebuild | **Gate**: `exm.stats` at 70%, the capstone `drl.stats.exit-ols`, and exam item Q19 (explain the table) at 7 of 10. **Confirm**: none (ASSESSMENT.md row `1.6.exit`); the capstone also runs locally as an optional repository task | A real university paper as confirm | No public paper tests "match statsmodels"; the drill is the honest instrument |

### 1.3 1.6 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Every number a desk or lab reports is an estimate with an error bar, stated or not.** A Sharpe ratio of 1.0 over three years has a standard error of about 0.58 (lesson 19). A researcher who cannot attach that error cannot tell a strategy from luck.
- **The expensive mistakes are statistical.** Reading a p-value as the chance the null is true; choosing the best of 200 backtests and believing it; ordinary standard errors on overlapping returns (too small by a factor of about $\sqrt{12}$ for monthly-sampled annual returns, homework RG3); a beta driven by one crash day; regressing one price on another (2.2 lesson 35). Each is a trap screen here.
- **Interviews test it directly.** Research interviews ask for the MLE of a distribution, the meaning of a confidence interval, why $n - 1$, power and sample size, multiple testing, and a regression read-out; the researcher track's "data take-home" is mostly this item.
- **ML labs run on it.** Maximum likelihood is the loss function of most models (cross-entropy is a negative log-likelihood, lesson 65); priors are regularisers (lesson 78); evaluation is hypothesis testing with multiple comparisons.
- **It makes 2.1, 2.2 and the Stage 3 papers possible.** Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016) is a multiple-testing paper; Fama and French (1993) is a regression table; 2.2 is this item on dependent data.

### 1.4 1.6 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| ST-O1 | Distinguish parameter, estimator and estimate; compute standard errors (iid, estimated, correlated) and size a sample for a target error | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; exam Q1 |
| ST-O2 | Compute and compare bias, variance and MSE; prove $E[S^2] = \sigma^2$ and $\text{MSE} = \text{bias}^2 + \text{variance}$; choose resistant estimators for heavy tails | Homework ES1 and ES2 long-form at 7 of 10; exam Q2 |
| ST-O3 | Derive MLEs by the score equation, at boundaries and numerically; compute Fisher information, standard errors from curvature and the Cramér-Rao bound; apply the delta method (including the Sharpe ratio's standard error) | Exam Q3, Q4; homework ES3, ES4 |
| ST-O4 | Construct and interpret $z$, $t$, Wilson, $\chi^2$-variance and bootstrap intervals, and state what "95%" claims | Exam Q5, Q6; homework IT1 |
| ST-O5 | Run and interpret $z$, $t$ (one-sample, paired, Welch), $\chi^2$, KS, permutation and likelihood-ratio tests; compute p-values, power and sample sizes; prove the Neyman-Pearson lemma and the uniformity of null p-values | Exam Q7 to Q10, Q20 pool; homework IT2, IT3 |
| ST-O6 | Control family-wise error (Bonferroni, Holm) and false discovery rate (Benjamini-Hochberg); quantify forking paths and backtest overfitting | Exam Q11, Q12; homework IT4 |
| ST-O7 | State the linear model's assumptions and what each buys; derive $\text{Cov}(\hat\beta) = \sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}$, $E[\text{SSE}] = (n - p)\sigma^2$ and Gauss-Markov | Homework RG1, RG2 long-form; exam Q20 pool |
| ST-O8 | Compute and interpret coefficient standard errors, $t$, $F$, $R^2$, adjusted $R^2$, confidence and prediction intervals; diagnose non-linearity, heteroscedasticity, autocorrelation, non-normality and influence; use HC and HAC errors | Exam Q13 to Q16; homework RG2 to RG4 |
| ST-O9 | Fit a regression in NumPy, match every number in the statsmodels summary, and explain every number | `1.6.exit`: capstone and exam Q19 at 7 of 10 |
| ST-O10 | Update priors on a grid and by conjugacy (Beta-binomial, normal-normal), compute credible intervals and predictive probabilities, shrink many estimates (empirical Bayes), read a prior as a penalty, and sample a posterior by Metropolis | Exam Q17, Q18; homework BY1, BY2 |
| ST-O11 | Apply all of the above to returns: Sharpe ratio inference, alpha shrinkage, multiple-strategy screens, factor regressions with HAC errors | Project `prj.stats.skill-or-luck`; transfer screens |

### 1.5 1.6 Prerequisites

- **0.3** level C4 (probability, conditional probability, trees) and level C5 (averages, spread, box plots, histograms, scatter and correlation, sampling bias; lessons 112 to 123 of that file).
- **1.1** level A2 lesson 63 (`ddof` in NumPy and pandas), level A3 (seeded simulation, lesson 69), level D4 (returns, lesson 93; look-ahead, lesson 94), lesson 101 (return histograms and fat tails). Every `web` drill assumes NumPy and pandas.
- **1.2** Git and pytest: the capstone and the project live in the learner's repository with tests.
- **1.3** lessons 30 to 32 (linear and quadratic approximation, error propagation: the delta method), 35 and 40 (critical points, second derivative test), 47 (fitting by optimisation, including a first MLE), 72 and 73 (densities and expectations as integrals), 99 and 100 (Taylor), 118 to 123 (multivariable optimisation, the Hessian, least squares as minimisation, two-parameter MLE), 133 (the Gaussian integral).
- **1.4** lessons 13 ($X^\top X$ is symmetric), 39 to 47 (projections, normal equations, CAPM beta, FWL, collinearity, weighted least squares, ridge), 51 (QR), 88 (condition number).
- **1.5** all of it, and specifically: expectation and its linearity, variance of sums and covariance, the named distributions (binomial, Poisson, exponential, normal, Gamma, Beta, $t$, $\chi^2$), the LLN and CLT, Chebyshev, Cauchy-Schwarz, conditional expectation, the bivariate normal and regression to the mean, Bayes' rule. Lesson numbers are those of the 1.5 file being written in parallel.
- **1.7** runs alongside 1.3 to 1.6: Sharpe ratios and excess returns are defined there; lessons 19 and 42 define the Sharpe ratio again in one line for a learner who meets them first here.

### 1.6 1.6 Hours budget (120 h)

| Block | What | Hours | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| In-app lessons | 80 lessons at 15 minutes, quiz included | 20.0 | 16.7% |
| In-app practice and redo | 15 level reviews at about 15 minutes (3.75 h); generated practice and the Redo queue at 7 and 30 days (14.25 h) | 18.0 | 15.0% |
| Homework | 15 sets at about 2 hours | 30.0 | 25.0% |
| Exams | One practice paper (1.5 h), `exm.stats` (1.5 h), reviewing both (1.0 h) | 4.0 | 3.3% |
| External reading and lectures | Watching 15.0 h: 10 core MIT 18.650 lectures budgeted at 1.35 h each (about 80 minutes recorded, with pauses: 13.5 h) and MIT 18.S096 lecture 6, "Regression Analysis" (1.5 h; section 5.0). Reading 5.0 h: DeGroot sections and five short papers. External problems 8.0 h: DeGroot exercises, 18.650 problem sets, ISLP chapter 3 and 13 exercises | 28.0 | 23.3% |
| Projects and drills | Exit capstone `drl.stats.exit-ols` (4 h); Statistics Lab, 14 drills of 30 minutes (7 h); project `prj.stats.skill-or-luck` (9 h) | 20.0 | 16.7% |
| **Total** | | **120.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except the 20 hours of watching and reading) is 100 hours, 83%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2).

**Pace.** 120 hours is 8 weeks at the curriculum's 15-hour week, but the blocks do not divide evenly: main subject (9 h a week) carries lessons, homework, reading and exams (82 h, 9 weeks); the Build block (3 h) carries the capstone, the Lab and the project (20 h, 7 weeks); the Review block (2 h) carries practice and redo (18 h). The main block sets the pace: about 9 weeks.

### 1.7 2.2 Time series: scope, boundaries and decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T1 | Spine | **Hyndman, Athanasopoulos and co-authors, Forecasting: Principles and Practice, the Pythonic Way (free)** for stationarity, decomposition, ARIMA, exponential smoothing and evaluation; **Tsay, Analysis of Financial Time Series, 3rd edition (2010)** chapters 1 to 3, 7 and 8 for returns, volatility, VaR and cointegration, as the curriculum names; **MIT 18.S096 Topics in Mathematics with Applications in Finance (Fall 2013)** time-series and volatility lectures (8, 9, 11 and 12, confirmed by search) as the lecture parallel | Hamilton as spine; Tsay only; FPP only | The curriculum's two picks cover complementary halves: FPP has no GARCH or cointegration; Tsay has little on evaluation. Hamilton is the theory reference (stretch) |
| T2 | Number of courses | **Three**: SM Stationary Models (21 lessons: stationarity, autocorrelation, ARMA, forecasting), VL Volatility (10), CO Unit Roots and Cointegration (12) | Five courses, one per cover item; two courses | Three stories: the mean of a series, its variance, and series without a mean to return to. VL and CO sit under the 20-lesson guideline (open item 3) |
| T3 | Order | **Stationary models, then volatility, then unit roots and cointegration** | Unit-root tests inside course SM (the catalogue's working outline 2.2.1 L6 to L9) | Dickey-Fuller and Engle-Granger are one idea (a unit-root test whose null distribution is not $t$, then the same test on fitted residuals); both need AR(1) estimation and regression inference. The random walk and "prices wander, returns hover" are taught on day one (lessons 1 to 3), so the exit's second half is built from the start |
| T4 | FPP topics outside the cover list | **One lesson each**: decomposition (4), benchmark forecasts (17), exponential smoothing (19), evaluation (20), leakage (21) | Skip them (the cover list names none); FPP's full depth | FPP is the curriculum's spine and spends most of its pages on them, and the 2.1 exit needs time-ordered validation. Seasonal ARIMA, hierarchical forecasting and dynamic regression stay in FPP as reading |
| T5 | Beyond the cover list | **One lesson each**: long-run variance and effective sample size (10), the Roll model as MA(1) (12), Wold's theorem (14), volatility asymmetry and GJR (26), realised volatility (29), VaR backtesting (30), volatility targeting (31), KPSS (34), ARIMA (36), VAR, Granger causality and Johansen as a preview (41), pairs trading without look-ahead (42) | Kalman filters, state-space models, regime switching, long memory, multivariate GARCH | The eleven are used daily on a desk; the rest belong to canon-gap P11 (2.2b) or Hamilton and Durbin-Koopman as stretch |
| T6 | GARCH fitting | **The learner's own likelihood, optimised with `scipy.optimize.minimize`** (catalogue decision 12) | The `arch` package | `arch` is not on the confirmed Pyodide package list [unverified]; writing the likelihood is the lesson |
| T7 | Exam | **`exm.ts`, topic kind**: 75 minutes, 15 items, 60 marks, long-form 16 marks (26.7%) | 90 minutes | The paper has fewer computational items than 1.6's; 75 minutes is about 1.5 times the summed item estimates (ASSESSMENT.md 3.1) |
| T8 | Exit rebuild | **Gate**: `exm.ts` at 70%, the capstone `drl.ts.exit-coint`, and exam item Q15 (why returns, not prices) at 7 of 10. **Confirm**: none (ASSESSMENT.md row `2.2.exit`) | Real data only | The drill's pairs come from generators that know whether each pair is cointegrated; real pairs are offered as an optional extension |

**What 2.2 leaves to other items.**

| Topic | Where | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Returns arithmetic, `shift`, `pct_change`, look-ahead in pandas | 1.1 lessons 93, 94 | 2.2 assumes the code; lesson 1 adds the statistics |
| Linear first-order equations and mean reversion; geometric series | 1.3 lessons 85, 87 | AR(1) as the discrete twin of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation (lesson 40) |
| Eigenvalues and the stability of $\mathbf u_{k+1} = A\mathbf u_k$ | 1.4 lessons 64, 70 | Lesson 41 applies them to VAR(1) |
| Regression inference, HAC errors, MLE, tests | 1.6 | Every course here assumes them |
| Time-ordered cross-validation inside an ML pipeline, purging and embargo | 2.1 | Lesson 21 is the time-series half; if the track puts 2.1 first, lesson 21 becomes a recap (open item 4) |
| Kalman filter, state space, regime switching, Fama-MacBeth, Newey-West in panels | Canon-gap P11 (2.2b) | |
| Stochastic differential equations, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck in continuous time | 3.2 (optional) | Lesson 40 uses the discrete form only |
| Pairs-trading replication (Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst) | 3.1 | Lesson 42 and the drill prepare it |

### 1.8 2.2 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Market data are time series, and independence is the assumption they break.** Every 1.6 standard error assumed independent draws; returns are close to uncorrelated but not independent, and prices are not stationary at all. 2.2 is where the learner finds out which 1.6 results survive.
- **Volatility is the most forecastable thing in finance.** Means are nearly unpredictable (1.6 lesson 2); variance clusters and mean-reverts, and GARCH-type forecasts drive risk limits, VaR, option desks and position sizing.
- **Fitting to prices manufactures relationships.** Two independent random walks regressed on each other give $\lvert t\rvert > 1.96$ about 76% of the time at $T = 100$ (lesson 35). The exit's "explain why models are fitted to returns and not prices" is the single most common error in amateur quant work.
- **Cointegration is the statistics of relative value.** Pairs, ETF against basket, futures against spot, dual listings: all are bets that a spread is stationary.
- **Evaluation discipline transfers to all of ML.** Rolling-origin evaluation and leakage (lessons 20, 21) are the time-series half of 2.1's exit.

### 1.9 2.2 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| TS-O1 | Convert prices to returns, aggregate them over time and across assets, scale volatility with $\sqrt h$ and say when that rule fails | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; exam Q1 |
| TS-O2 | Define weak stationarity, classify series, choose transforms, and decompose trend and season | Exam Q2; homework SM1 |
| TS-O3 | Compute and read the ACF, PACF, white-noise bands and Ljung-Box statistic; compute the long-run variance and effective sample size of a mean | Exam Q3, Q4; homework SM2 |
| TS-O4 | Derive the moments, ACF, stationarity and invertibility conditions of AR(1), MA(1), AR(2) and ARMA; identify, fit and check ARMA models | Exam Q5 to Q7; homework SM3 |
| TS-O5 | Forecast with benchmarks, ARMA and exponential smoothing, give interval forecasts, and evaluate by rolling origin without leakage | Exam Q8; homework SM4 |
| TS-O6 | Model volatility with EWMA, ARCH, GARCH and GJR; compute persistence, long-run variance, half-lives and term structures; fit GARCH by maximum likelihood; backtest VaR | Exam Q9 to Q11; homework VL1, VL2 |
| TS-O7 | Test for unit roots with Dickey-Fuller, ADF and KPSS using the right critical values, and recognise spurious regression | Exam Q12; homework CO1 |
| TS-O8 | Test a pair for cointegration by Engle-Granger, read an error-correction model and half-life, and trade a spread without look-ahead | Exam Q13, Q14; `drl.ts.exit-coint` |
| TS-O9 | Explain why models are fitted to returns and not prices | Exam Q15 at 7 of 10 |

### 1.10 2.2 Prerequisites

- **1.6** complete: MLE (ES3, ES4), $t$ and $\chi^2$ tests (IT2, IT3), regression inference and HAC errors (RG2, RG3), AIC and BIC (lessons 46, 64), the bootstrap (25).
- **1.5**: covariance, the CLT, geometric and normal distributions, conditional expectation.
- **1.4** lessons 64 and 70 (eigenvalues, stability of linear recursions, vector autoregressions) for lessons 13 and 41.
- **1.3** lessons 85 (mean reversion), 87 (geometric series, for MA($\infty$) and EWMA weights), 104 (Euler's formula, for AR(2) cycles).
- **1.1** level D4 (time series in pandas, returns, look-ahead) and 1.2 (repository and tests for the capstone).
- **1.7** (finance basics) for prices, spreads and VaR vocabulary.

| Homework | 8 sets at about 2 hours | 16.0 | 26.7% |

| Projects and drills | Exit capstone `drl.ts.exit-coint` (2.5 h); Time Series Lab, 8 drills of 30 minutes (4 h); project `prj.ts.vol-forecast` (4.5 h) | 11.0 | 18.3% |
| **Total** | | **60.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except 8 hours of watching and reading) is 52 hours, 87%.

**Pace.** About 5 weeks at 15 hours a week; on the researcher track, where 2.2 is core, the Build block can run the project alongside 2.1's.

## 2. Courses

### 2.0 Conventions for every lesson below

**Ids** follow CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 4.1: `crs.<course>`, `lvl.<course>.<level>`, `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, `set.<course>.<level>` for homework, `exm.stats` and `exm.ts` for the topic exams, `prj.stats.<name>` and `prj.ts.<name>` for projects. Course namespaces: 1.6 `estimators` (ES), `testing` (IT), `linreg` (RG), `bayesian` (BY); 2.2 `arma` (SM), `volatility` (VL), `cointegration` (CO). Libraries use the topic namespaces `stats` and `ts`: `skl.stats.*`, `mc.stats.*`, `gen.stats.*`, `drl.stats.*`, and the same with `ts`. Misconception ids are the widget catalogue's register (section 10) under the topic namespace (`se-vs-sd` becomes `mc.stats.se-vs-sd`); new ids are marked [new]. The `#` column is the lesson's number within its item (1.6: 1 to 80; 2.2: 1 to 43); display numbers are computed from position and never stored.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps, a transfer screen where a true quant or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 3 to 5 items. From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, one worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, at least one line the learner derives (a `derive` screen; `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo` wherever the step is a creative one), and "concreteness fades": the first screens use pictures and small numbers, the last ones symbols.

In statistics these take particular forms:

- **Manipulation before notation** means drawing samples and watching a statistic's histogram build, dragging a parameter handle along a likelihood, dragging a critical value and watching $\alpha$ and $\beta$ trade, dragging a hedge ratio until a spread stops wandering, before any formula appears. The prediction is made before the simulation runs, and the prediction is what is graded (decision 3).
- **The worked example's decision point** is usually a choice between two defensible procedures that differ in what they assume: $z$ or $t$, Wald or Wilson, paired or Welch, Bonferroni or BH, classical or HC errors, Dickey-Fuller or Engle-Granger critical values, prices or returns.
- **Derive** means completing or ordering the lines of an expectation or variance calculation, a score equation, a short proof (Cramér-Rao, Neyman-Pearson, Gauss-Markov), or building a formula from the picture (the standard error from adding variances; the half-life from $\varphi^h = \tfrac12$).

**Widgets** use the ids of widgets-prob-stats-ts.md sections 3 to 8 and its naming table 0.3 (`plot-axes`, `marks`, `draggable-point`, `slider`, `choice-cards`, `numeric-entry`, `data-table`, `derivation`, `code-cell`, `sim-runner`, `card-sort`, `free-response`): statistics composites `estimator-compare`, `likelihood-lab`, `ci-ladder`, `test-lab`, `multiple-testing`, `regression-lab`, `summary-table`, `qq-plot`, `posterior-lab`; time-series composites `series-lab`, `acf-lab`, `arma-lab`, `forecast-lab`, `garch-lab`, `coint-lab`; from 1.5's set `sampling-lab`, `dist-explorer`, `icon-array`, `odds-ledger`, `contingency-table`, `gaussian-2d`, `covariance-lab`, `mass-editor`; from the sibling catalogues `dot-plot-stats`, `stat-chart`, `complex-plane` (Stage 0), `least-squares-fit`, `taylor-builder`, `quadratic-form`, `surface-explorer`, `linear-dynamics`, `sde-stepper` (calc-linalg), `predict-output` (programming). A mode the catalogue does not yet list is marked [mode to add]; section 1.12 collects them.

**Bertie** is built in from the first lesson. Every screen carries `tutor.focus`, `tutor.notes` and `tutor.avoid` (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.10); section 7 shows them in full. In widget screens Bertie reads the widget's `describe()` and, in lesson and explore modes only, its `secret()` block (true parameters, exact answers) under the never-reveal instruction (catalogue decision 9). He never changes answer state; he may offer a "Try this" chip that sets a slider, which the learner taps.

**Quiz item types.** The Stage 0 letters (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.4): N numeric, X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps of a derivation or proof, S sort or match, I interval, P point or handle on a canvas, T predict the output of code, K code with tests. This file adds one:

| Letter | Type | Check kind |
|---|---|---|
| D | A distribution or a set of bars: a grid posterior, predicted ACF stems, a predicted histogram centre and spread | `distribution` (total variation or maximum absolute difference, tolerance stated per item) |

Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice (C and M) stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (design research 10); section 2.8 counts them.

**Runtime tags.** Lesson screens run on both targets. The tags are void (header rows): `code` screens and drills declare `needs`, and in the Artifact every code screen shows the handoff link and no Parsons fallback; a lesson screen that runs learner code resolves as `read` there and is finished in the web app.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% drawn from earlier levels of the same course (ASSESSMENT.md 2.4). Homework follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with, with its answer where it has one. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

### 2.1 Course ES: Estimators and Their Errors (`crs.estimators`, 4 levels, 20 lessons)

What a statistic is, how far it lands from the truth, and how to build good ones. It starts from the sampling distribution (the picture under all of statistics), measures estimators by bias, variance and MSE, builds them by moments and by maximum likelihood, and ends with the large-sample theory that turns any MLE into an estimate with a standard error, including the Sharpe ratio's.

**Level ES1: Sampling distributions** (`lvl.estimators.sampling`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.estimators.statistic-is-random` A statistic is a random variable | Distinguish parameter, estimator and estimate, and predict the centre and spread of the sampling distribution of a sample proportion from the binomial before simulating it | 60% of a bank's traders use Python daily. You poll 20. Before polling, which results would surprise you? (The share $\hat p$ has mean 0.6 and SD $\sqrt{0.24/20} = 0.110$; a result of 9 or fewer has probability 0.128) | `sampling-lab` (sampling, proportion, prediction bracket), `dist-explorer` (binomial) | Trap: the estimate is the parameter ("our poll says 70%, so 70% use Python"). Edge: a census has no sampling error but can still be biased (non-response); a biased sampling scheme gives a sampling distribution centred in the wrong place, and more data do not move it | C, N, M, P |
| 2 | `lsn.estimators.standard-error` The standard error and the square-root law (section 7) | Derive $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \sigma^2/n$ for independent draws, compute and interpret $\sigma/\sqrt n$ and $s/\sqrt n$, size a sample for a target standard error, and show how correlation between draws breaks the formula | Client orders have SD 40 lots. How far from the true mean does the average of 4 orders typically land: 40, 20, 10 or 5 lots? (20) | `sampling-lab` (predict-se; correlated population [mode to add]), `derivation`, `numeric-entry`, `predict-output` | Trap: the SD of the data used where the SE of the mean belongs, and the reverse. Edge: 16 equicorrelated orders with $\rho = 0.2$ are worth 4 independent ones; perfectly correlated draws are worth one | N, N, C, N, N |
| 3 | `lsn.estimators.sample-variance` The sample variance and its $n - 1$ | Prove $E\left[\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2\right] = (n - 1)\sigma^2$, explain the lost degree of freedom by the constraint $\sum_i (x_i - \bar x) = 0$, and show that $S$ is biased low for $\sigma$ although $S^2$ is unbiased | Drag a centre $c$ along five returns: the sum of squared distances is smallest at $c = \bar x$. So spread measured from $\bar x$ always looks smaller than spread measured from the true $\mu$. By how much, on average? (By exactly $\sigma^2$) | `dot-plot-stats` (movable centre, divisor chips), `estimator-compare` (var-n, var-n-1), `derivation` | Trap: "$n - 1$ makes every spread estimate unbiased" ($E[S] < \sigma$ by Jensen). Edge: $n = 1$ gives $\tfrac00$; with $\mu$ known, divide by $n$; NumPy's default `ddof=0` against pandas' `ddof=1` (1.1 lesson 63) | N, O, C, T |
| 4 | `lsn.estimators.ecdf-plugin` The empirical distribution and plug-in estimates | Build the empirical CDF, estimate probabilities, quantiles and any functional $T(F)$ by plugging in $\hat F_n$, and attach the DKW band $\varepsilon = \sqrt{\ln(2/\alpha)/(2n)}$ | 250 daily returns: on 6 days the loss exceeded 2%. Estimate the chance tomorrow loses more than 2% (0.024), and the 1% quantile (historical VaR). How sure are you of either? (The 95% DKW band is $\pm 0.086$ for $n = 250$) | `sampling-lab` (ecdf view [mode to add]), `dist-explorer`, `data-table` | Trap: one quantile definition (NumPy's default puts the 1% quantile of 250 points between the 3rd and 4th smallest; other software uses the 3rd). Edge: the ECDF is a right-continuous step function; ties make jumps larger than $\tfrac1n$; 1% VaR from 250 days rests on two or three points | N, N, C, M |
| 5 | `lsn.estimators.normal-sampling-theory` Normal samples: $\chi^2$, $t$ and the independence of $\bar X$ and $S^2$ | Use $\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2_{n-1}$ and $\frac{\bar X - \mu}{S/\sqrt n}\sim t_{n-1}$ for normal samples, and explain why replacing $\sigma$ by $S$ fattens the tails | Five normal returns, standardised with $s$ instead of $\sigma$: how often is $\lvert T\rvert > 1.96$? (0.122, not 0.05) | `sampling-lab` (t-stat), `dist-explorer` ($t$, $\chi^2$), `derivation` | Trap: 1.96 with $s$ at small $n$. Edge: $\bar X$ and $S^2$ are independent only for normal data (for exponential data they are positively correlated); $t_1$ is the Cauchy; $t_\infty$ is the normal | N, N, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 1 reads a backtest's hit rate as a sample proportion; lesson 2 shows a year of daily returns cannot pin down a mean (section 7); lesson 4 computes historical VaR and its uncertainty.

**Level ES2: Judging estimators** (`lvl.estimators.quality`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | `lsn.estimators.bias-variance-mse` Bias, variance and mean squared error | Compute bias, variance and MSE of an estimator, prove $\text{MSE} = \text{bias}^2 + \text{variance}$, and choose between estimators by MSE across the plausible range of $\theta$ | Two analysts estimate a stock's mean daily return (true 0.05%, SD 1%) from 20 days: one reports $\bar x$, the other $\tfrac12\bar x$. Whose MSE is smaller? (Shrunk: 0.0131 against 0.05, in $\%^2$) | `estimator-compare` (mse-bars, mse-vs-c), `derivation` | Trap: unbiased means best (`mc.stats.unbiased-is-best`). Edge: MSE depends on the unknown $\theta$: the shrunk estimator loses once $\lvert\mu\rvert > 0.387\%$; an estimator that ignores the data ($\hat\theta = 0$) has the best MSE at $\theta = 0$ | N, O, C, N |
| 7 | `lsn.estimators.consistency` Consistency | Show an estimator is consistent from MSE $\to 0$ and Chebyshev, and separate consistency from unbiasedness with a counterexample each way | $X_1$ alone is unbiased for $\mu$ and never improves; $\bar X + \tfrac1n$ is biased at every $n$ and closes in. Which would you rather use with 1,000 observations? | `estimator-compare` (mse-vs-n), `sampling-lab` (lln) | Trap: consistent means unbiased (`mc.stats.consistent-means-unbiased`). Edge: the maximum of uniforms is biased but consistent; the Cauchy sample mean is unbiased for nothing and consistent for nothing | C, M, N, O |
| 8 | `lsn.estimators.resistant` Efficiency and resistance: mean, median and trimmed mean | Compare the sampling variances of the mean and the median under normal and heavy-tailed data, and choose the median, a trimmed mean or the MAD for returns with outliers | Normal data: which estimate of the centre wobbles less, mean or median? Now Student $t_3$ data. (Normal: the median's efficiency is $2/\pi = 0.64$. $t_3$: the median's asymptotic variance is $1.85/n$ against the mean's $3/n$, so the median wins by a factor 1.62) | `estimator-compare` (mean, median, trimmed [mode to add]), `dist-explorer` ($t$), `qq-plot` | Trap: the mean is always best because it uses every observation. Edge: breakdown points (mean 0%, median 50%); $1.4826\times$ MAD estimates $\sigma$ for normal data; for the Cauchy the median is consistent and the mean is not | N, C, M, N |
| 9 | `lsn.estimators.method-of-moments` Method of moments | Derive method-of-moments estimators for one- and two-parameter families by equating sample and population moments, and detect impossible estimates | Waiting times between trades: sample mean 2.0 s, variance 1.0 s$^2$. Fit a Gamma by matching both moments (shape $\hat\alpha = \bar x^2/s^2 = 4$, rate $\hat\beta = \bar x/s^2 = 2$) | `dist-explorer` (match moments), `estimator-compare` (mom, mle) | Trap: matching only the mean for a two-parameter family. Edge: for Uniform$(0, \theta)$, $2\bar x$ can fall below the largest observation (data 1, 2, 9 give 8 < 9), an impossible estimate | X, N, C, N |
| 10 | `lsn.estimators.german-tank` The German tank problem | Derive $E[M] = \frac{k(N + 1)}{k + 1}$ for the maximum $M$ of $k$ serial numbers drawn without replacement from $1, \dots, N$, build the unbiased $M\left(1 + \tfrac1k\right) - 1$, and compare its spread with $2\bar X - 1$ | Captured tanks carry serials 19, 40, 42, 60. How many tanks were made? ($60 + \tfrac{60}{4} - 1 = 74$; the mean-based estimate is 79.5) | `estimator-compare` (german-tank), `numeric-entry`, `derivation` | Trap: the largest serial is the estimate (always too low). Edge: $2\bar x - 1$ can fall below the largest serial (1, 2, 30 give 21); with $k = 1$ the estimator is $2M - 1$ | N, N, C, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 6 shrinks a noisy mean-return estimate; lesson 8 estimates a return distribution's centre and scale with one crash day in the sample; lesson 9 fits trade inter-arrival times.

**Level ES3: Maximum likelihood** (`lvl.estimators.likelihood`, 5 lessons)

1.3 lessons 47 and 123 found maximum-likelihood estimates as optimisation exercises. This level makes the likelihood a statistical object: a function of the parameter, compared by ratios, maximised on its logs, at boundaries and numerically.

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 | `lsn.estimators.likelihood` The likelihood function | Write $L(\theta) = \prod_i f(x_i; \theta)$ for independent data, read it as a function of $\theta$ with the data fixed, and compare parameter values by likelihood ratios | A coin gives 7 heads in 10. Is $p = 0.7$ or $p = 0.5$ more plausible, and by how much? ($L(0.7)/L(0.5) = 2.28$) | `likelihood-lab` (bernoulli, point-densities, product) | Trap: the likelihood is a probability distribution over $\theta$ (`mc.stats.likelihood-is-probability-of-theta`: $\int_0^1 p^7(1-p)^3\,dp = \tfrac{1}{1320}$, not 1). Edge: constants such as $\binom{10}{7}$ cancel in ratios; densities in a likelihood can exceed 1 | N, C, N, M |
| 12 | `lsn.estimators.log-likelihood-score` The log-likelihood and the score equation | Maximise $\ell(\theta) = \sum_i \ln f(x_i; \theta)$ by solving $\ell'(\theta) = 0$, confirm with $\ell'' < 0$, and derive the MLEs for Bernoulli, Poisson and exponential data | 500 normal observations: the likelihood is a product of 500 numbers near 0.1, and the computer returns 0. Repair it without changing the answer | `likelihood-lab` (log), `derivation`, `predict-output` | Trap: logs change where the maximum is (they do not: $\ln$ is increasing) (`mc.stats.why-logs`). Edge: the exponential rate's MLE $1/\bar x$ is biased ($E = \frac{n\lambda}{n-1}$); a product of 500 values near 0.1 is about $10^{-500}$, below the smallest double | X, X, N, T |
| 13 | `lsn.estimators.mle-normal` Two parameters: the normal MLE | Maximise the normal log-likelihood over $(\mu, \sigma^2)$, obtain $\hat\mu = \bar x$ and $\hat\sigma^2 = \frac1n\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$, and explain why the MLE of $\sigma^2$ is biased yet consistent | Six monthly returns: find the peak of the $(\mu, \sigma)$ log-likelihood contour map by dragging, then by algebra | `likelihood-lab` (normal-mu-sigma, contours), `surface-explorer` | Trap: maximum likelihood estimates are unbiased (`mc.stats.mle-is-unbiased`). Edge: with $n = 1$ the likelihood is unbounded as $\sigma\to 0$; the profile likelihood for $\mu$ | X, N, C, P |
| 14 | `lsn.estimators.boundary-mle` When calculus fails: boundary maxima | Find the MLE when the peak is not a stationary point (Uniform$(0, \theta)$ gives $\max_i x_i$; the Laplace location gives the median) and recognise a score equation with no root | Bus waits are uniform on $[0, \theta]$: 3, 7, 4.5 and 9 minutes. Solve $\ell'(\theta) = 0$. ($-\tfrac{n}{\theta} = 0$ has no solution; $\hat\theta = 9$) | `likelihood-lab` (uniform-theta, show-why boundary) | Trap: the derivative is always zero at the MLE (`mc.stats.derivative-is-always-zero-at-mle`). Edge: $L(\theta) = 0$ for $\theta < 9$, so the maximum sits at a jump; the Cauchy location likelihood can have several local maxima | N, C, X, N |
| 15 | `lsn.estimators.invariance-numerical` Invariance and numerical maximum likelihood | Use invariance (the MLE of $g(\theta)$ is $g(\hat\theta)$), and maximise a log-likelihood numerically with `scipy.optimize.minimize` on an unconstrained scale ($\log\sigma$), checked against a closed form | A Poisson rate is estimated at 2.4 trades a minute. What is the MLE of the chance a minute has no trades? ($e^{-2.4} = 0.091$) | `likelihood-lab`, `code-cell` (`web`), `predict-output` | Trap: optimising $\sigma$ directly lets the optimiser try $\sigma < 0$ and return `nan`; minimising $\ell$ instead of $-\ell$. Edge: invariance holds for MLEs but not for unbiasedness ($1/\bar x$); a flat likelihood ridge (non-identified parameters) | N, T, K, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 12 fits trade counts per minute; lesson 13 fits monthly returns; lesson 15 fits a Student $t$ to daily returns and compares its log-likelihood with the normal's.

**Level ES4: Large-sample theory** (`lvl.estimators.asymptotics`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 16 | `lsn.estimators.fisher-information` Fisher information: curvature is precision | Compute $I(\theta) = -E[\ell_1''(\theta)] = \text{Var}(\text{score})$ for one observation and the standard error $1/\sqrt{nI(\hat\theta)}$ from the curvature at the peak | Two log-likelihoods peak at $\hat p = 0.6$, one from 10 flips and one from 1,000. Which estimate do you trust, and which feature of the curve says so? | `likelihood-lab` (curvature parabola), `derivation` | Trap: a flat log-likelihood means a good fit (it means little information). Edge: the score has mean zero; information adds over independent observations; information about $\sigma$ and about $\sigma^2$ differ by the reparametrisation factor | X, N, N, C |
| 17 | `lsn.estimators.cramer-rao` The Cramér-Rao lower bound | Derive $\text{Var}(T)\ge\frac{1}{nI(\theta)}$ for unbiased $T$ from Cauchy-Schwarz applied to $\text{Cov}(T, \text{score})$, and identify efficient estimators | Can any unbiased estimator of a Poisson rate from 50 counts have SD below $\sqrt{\lambda/50}$? (No; $\bar X$ attains it) | `estimator-compare` (crlb), `derivation` | Trap: the bound limits every estimator (biased estimators can beat it: shrinkage, lesson 6). Edge: Uniform$(0, \theta)$ breaks the regularity conditions (support depends on $\theta$) and its unbiased estimator has variance of order $\tfrac{1}{n^2}$ | O, N, C, M |
| 18 | `lsn.estimators.mle-asymptotics` The MLE is approximately normal | State $\sqrt n(\hat\theta - \theta)\to N(0, 1/I(\theta))$, build Wald intervals $\hat\theta\pm 1.96\,\text{SE}$, and recognise when the approximation fails | Exponential waiting times: the histogram of $\hat\lambda$ is skewed at $n = 10$ and bell-shaped at $n = 200$. When can you use the normal? | `sampling-lab` (statistic mle), `likelihood-lab` | Trap: the approximation holds at every $n$. Edge: $\hat p = 0$ gives a standard error of 0 and the interval $[0, 0]$; the "rule of three" ($3/n$) is the honest 95% upper bound | N, C, N, M |
| 19 | `lsn.estimators.delta-method` The delta method and the standard error of a Sharpe ratio | Derive $\text{Var}(g(\hat\theta))\approx g'(\theta)^2\text{Var}(\hat\theta)$ from a first-order Taylor expansion, extend it with a gradient to two parameters, and obtain $\text{SE}(\widehat{SR})\approx\sqrt{(1 + SR^2/2)/n}$ for independent normal returns (Lo 2002) | A strategy shows an annualised Sharpe ratio of 1.0 over 3 years of daily data. Is it distinguishable from 0? (Annual SE 0.58, so $t\approx 1.73$) | `taylor-builder` (delta method), `sampling-lab`, `derivation` | Trap: annualising the standard error by 252 instead of $\sqrt{252}$. Edge: $g'(\theta) = 0$ needs the second-order term; autocorrelated or fat-tailed returns change the formula (Lo's correction, 2.2 homework SM2) | X, N, N, C |
| 20 | `lsn.estimators.sufficiency` Sufficiency and Rao-Blackwell | Find sufficient statistics with the factorisation theorem ($\sum x_i$ for Bernoulli and Poisson, $(\sum x_i, \sum x_i^2)$ for the normal, $\max x_i$ for the uniform), and improve an unbiased estimator by conditioning on one | Two traders each flip a coin 10 times and get 6 heads in different orders. Is there information about $p$ in the order? (No: the likelihood depends only on the count) | `likelihood-lab` (two data sets, same likelihood), `derivation` | Trap: summarising returns by mean and SD loses nothing (only under a normal model). Edge: the whole sample is always sufficient; sufficiency is relative to the model | M, C, X, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 18 gives the honest upper bound on a default rate with zero defaults; lesson 19 is the Sharpe ratio's standard error, used again in lessons 29 and 42 and the project.

### 2.2 Course IT: Intervals and Tests (`crs.testing`, 4 levels, 22 lessons)

From a standard error to statements about the truth: intervals that cover it at a stated rate, tests that control false alarms at a stated rate, and what happens when hundreds of tests run at once. The course ends where quant research lives: screening many signals and backtests without fooling yourself.

**Level IT1: Confidence intervals** (`lvl.testing.intervals`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 21 | `lsn.testing.ci-meaning` What "95% confident" means | Construct $\bar x\pm 1.96\,\sigma/\sqrt n$ and interpret 95% as the long-run coverage of the method, predicting how many of 100 intervals miss | 100 analysts each sample 25 days and report an interval for the mean return. How many intervals miss the truth? (About 5; ten or more miss with probability 0.028) | `ci-ladder` (ladder, predictFirst) | Trap: "there is a 95% chance $\mu$ lies in this interval" (`mc.stats.ci-is-probability-for-this-interval`). Edge: the number that miss is random, Bin(100, 0.05); a 100% interval is the whole line | C, N, N, M |
| 22 | `lsn.testing.pivots-t` Pivots and the $t$ interval | Derive an interval by inverting a pivot, build $\bar x\pm t_{n-1, 0.975}\,s/\sqrt n$, and explain why 1.96 with $s$ undercovers at small $n$ | With $n = 5$ and $s$ in place of $\sigma$, the ladder's coverage drops. To what? (87.8%) | `ci-ladder` (z-with-s against t), `dist-explorer` ($t$), `derivation` | Trap: $z$ at small $n$ (`mc.stats.z-at-small-n`). Edge: the $t$ interval assumes normal data, and skewed data at small $n$ undercover even with $t$; $t_{30, 0.975} = 2.04$, $t_{100, 0.975} = 1.98$ | N, O, N, C |
| 23 | `lsn.testing.proportion-intervals` Intervals for a proportion: Wald fails, Wilson works | Compute Wald and Wilson intervals, read the exact coverage curve, and choose Wilson (or Clopper-Pearson) near 0 or 1 and at small $n$; size a poll by the worst case $p = \tfrac12$ | A strategy won 9 of 10 trades. The Wald interval is $[0.714, 1.086]$: above 1. What went wrong, and what is the Wilson interval? ($[0.596, 0.982]$) | `ci-ladder` (proportion, coverage-curve, wald, wilson) | Trap: Wald near 0 or 1 (`mc.stats.wald-near-boundary`). Edge: 0 successes gives the Wald interval $[0, 0]$; coverage oscillates with $n$ because counts are whole numbers (Brown, Cai and DasGupta 2001); a $\pm 1\%$ poll needs 9,604 people | N, N, I, M |
| 24 | `lsn.testing.variance-interval` An interval for a volatility, and why it breaks | Build the $\chi^2$ interval for $\sigma^2$, convert it to $\sigma$, and show by the coverage ladder that it fails for heavy-tailed returns while the $t$ interval for a mean survives | 60 monthly returns with $s = 4.5\%$. Give a 95% interval for $\sigma$. ($[3.81\%, 5.49\%]$) | `ci-ladder` (variance [mode to add]), `dist-explorer` ($\chi^2$) | Trap: the interval is symmetric about $s$. Edge: $\text{Var}(S^2)\approx\sigma^4\left(\frac{2}{n-1} + \frac{\kappa - 3}{n}\right)$ with $\kappa$ the kurtosis, so fat tails widen the true spread the formula ignores | N, I, N, M |
| 25 | `lsn.testing.bootstrap` The bootstrap | Estimate a standard error and a percentile interval by resampling the data with replacement, explain it as the plug-in principle of lesson 4, and state where it fails | 40 trade P&Ls, and you want a 95% interval for the median. There is no formula. Can the data simulate their own sampling distribution? | `sampling-lab` (bootstrap), `ci-ladder` (bootstrap-percentile), `code-cell` | Trap: resampling creates information (`mc.stats.bootstrap-creates-information`); resampling without replacement returns the same sample every time. Edge: the bootstrap of a maximum fails (each resample repeats the maximum with probability 0.637 for $n = 40$); dependent data need the block bootstrap | C, I, N, K |

Transfer screens: lesson 21 is an interval for a mean daily return; lesson 23 for a hit rate; lesson 24 for a volatility; lesson 25 for a strategy's median P&L.

**Level IT2: Hypothesis tests** (`lvl.testing.tests`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 26 | `lsn.testing.test-logic` The logic of a test | State null and alternative hypotheses, choose a statistic and a rejection region of size $\alpha$ before seeing the data, and apply the rule | A trader calls the market's direction right on 60 of 100 days. Would a guesser do this well? ($P(X\ge 60\mid p = \tfrac12) = 0.028$) | `test-lab` (binom-exact, z-prop), `dist-explorer` | Trap: failing to reject means the null is true (`mc.stats.fail-to-reject-means-accept`). Edge: a discrete statistic cannot hit $\alpha$ exactly; the null is the boring hypothesis, fixed before looking | C, N, N, S |
| 27 | `lsn.testing.p-values` p-values as tail areas | Compute one- and two-sided p-values for $z$ and $t$ statistics as null tail areas, and connect $p\le\alpha$ to rejection | The same trader: what is the smallest $\alpha$ at which you would reject? (0.028 one-sided, 0.057 two-sided) | `test-lab` (p shading, two-sided show-why) | Trap: $\alpha$ and $p$ are the same thing (`mc.stats.alpha-equals-p`); choosing the side after seeing the data (`mc.stats.one-sided-after-looking`). Edge: a discrete p-value includes the observed value's own probability; the normal approximation without continuity correction gives 0.023 here | N, N, C, P |
| 28 | `lsn.testing.p-value-not` What a p-value is not | Compute the share of significant results that are real from the prior share of true effects, $\alpha$ and power, and reject the three standard misreadings of $p$ | A fund screens 1,000 signals; 10% are real; each is tested at 5% with 80% power. Of the significant ones, what share are real? (80 of 125: 64%) | `icon-array` (fdr), `odds-ledger`, `test-lab` | Trap: $p$ is the probability the null is true (`mc.stats.p-is-probability-null-true`). Edge: $p = 0.049$ and $p = 0.051$ are the same evidence; at low power, significant estimates exaggerate the effect (the winner's curse) | N, C, N, S |
| 29 | `lsn.testing.power` Errors and power | Compute the power of one- and two-sided $z$-tests as a function of effect, $n$ and $\alpha$, and find the $n$ that gives 80% power | To detect a mean daily return of 0.05% with SD 1% at 5% (one-sided) with 80% power, how many days? (2,474 days: 9.8 years) | `test-lab` (alt, beta, power-curve) | Trap: significant means large (`mc.stats.significant-means-large`). Edge: power at the null equals $\alpha$; "post hoc power" is a function of $p$ and adds nothing | N, N, P, C |
| 30 | `lsn.testing.p-uniform` Under the null, p-values are uniform | Prove $P(p\le u) = u$ for a continuous statistic under $H_0$, and read the histogram of a batch of p-values | 10,000 backtests of pure noise: sketch the histogram of their p-values before running | `sampling-lab` (pvalues), `derivation` | Trap: null p-values cluster near 0.05 (`mc.stats.p-values-cluster-near-005`). Edge: discrete statistics give conservative p-values; under the alternative they pile near 0, so a spike near 0 over a flat floor estimates the share of real effects | D, O, N, C |
| 31 | `lsn.testing.duality` Tests and intervals are one idea | Show that a 95% interval holds exactly the null values a two-sided 5% test keeps, and use the duality both ways | A beta's 95% interval is $[0.82, 1.06]$. Does a 5% test reject $\beta = 1$? $\beta = 0.8$? (No; yes) | `test-lab`, `ci-ladder` | Trap: overlapping intervals for two groups mean no significant difference (two 95% intervals can overlap while the difference is significant). Edge: one-sided tests pair with one-sided bounds; Wilson's interval inverts the score test, Wald's the Wald test | I, M, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 26 tests a hit rate; lesson 28 is a signal screen; lesson 29 shows why a mean return needs a decade of data; lesson 31 tests a beta against 1.

**Level IT3: The testing toolkit** (`lvl.testing.toolkit`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 32 | `lsn.testing.two-sample` Comparing two groups: paired and Welch | Choose between the paired $t$ (one-sample $t$ on differences) and Welch's $t$ (with the Welch-Satterthwaite degrees of freedom) from the design, and compute either as a test and as an interval | Two execution algorithms were run on the same 10 orders. Analysed as paired, B beats A clearly; analysed as two independent groups, the same numbers show nothing. Which analysis is right, and why? | `test-lab` (t-paired, t-welch), `data-table`, `card-sort` | Trap: treating paired data as independent, which throws away the pairing. Edge: the pooled-variance $t$ assumes equal variances (Welch does not, so use Welch by default); Welch's degrees of freedom are not whole numbers | S, N, N, I |
| 33 | `lsn.testing.chi-squared` $\chi^2$ tests: goodness of fit and independence | Compute $\sum(O - E)^2/E$ for a goodness-of-fit test and a contingency table, find the degrees of freedom, and check the expected-count rule | Is this die fair? 60 rolls give 5, 8, 9, 8, 10, 20. ($\chi^2 = 13.4$ on 5 df, $p = 0.020$) | `contingency-table` (expected, chi2), `test-lab` (chi2-gof) | Trap: using proportions instead of counts; $(r - 1)(c - 1)$ forgotten for independence. Edge: expected counts below 5; each estimated parameter costs one degree of freedom | N, N, C, X |
| 34 | `lsn.testing.ks-normality` Is it normal? KS and Jarque-Bera | Compute the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistic as the largest gap between the ECDF and a hypothesised CDF, compute Jarque-Bera from skewness and kurtosis, and explain why thousands of returns always reject normality | 2,500 daily returns have skewness $-0.4$ and kurtosis 8. Is that normal? ($\text{JB} = 2{,}671$ on 2 df) | `qq-plot`, `test-lab` (ks [mode to add]), `dist-explorer` | Trap: KS with parameters estimated from the same data (the standard p-value is far too large; Lilliefors' correction). Edge: with large $n$ every tiny departure rejects, so read the QQ plot for where and how much; the 5% KS critical value is about $1.36/\sqrt n$ | N, N, C, M |
| 35 | `lsn.testing.permutation` Permutation tests | Build the exact null distribution of a difference in means by enumerating relabellings, compute the p-value, and state the exchangeability assumption | Signal on: +1.2, +0.8, +0.5, +1.5. Signal off: −0.3, +0.4, −0.6, +0.1. Of the 70 ways to label 4 of the 8 days "on", how many give a difference at least 1.1? (One: $p = \tfrac{1}{70} = 0.014$) | `sampling-lab` (permutation), `card-sort` | Trap: permuting autocorrelated returns (exchangeability fails). Edge: the smallest attainable p-value is $\tfrac{1}{70}$; a Monte Carlo p-value must count the observed labelling, $(b + 1)/(R + 1)$, so it is never 0 | N, N, C, K |
| 36 | `lsn.testing.neyman-pearson` The Neyman-Pearson lemma | Build the most powerful test of a simple null against a simple alternative by ranking outcomes by likelihood ratio, and prove the lemma for discrete outcomes | A die is fair or loaded (six with probability $\tfrac12$, each other face $\tfrac1{10}$). One roll; you may reject "fair" on at most $\tfrac16$ of fair rolls. Which face? (The six: likelihood ratio 3; power $\tfrac12$) | `test-lab`, `likelihood-lab`, `derivation` | Trap: rejecting where the null is least likely instead of where the ratio is largest. Edge: randomised tests reach exact $\alpha$; the one-sided $z$-test is most powerful for every $\mu_1 > \mu_0$ at once, and no two-sided test is | S, O, N, N |
| 37 | `lsn.testing.likelihood-ratio` Likelihood-ratio tests and Wilks' theorem | Compute $-2\ln\Lambda$ for nested models, refer it to $\chi^2$ with as many degrees of freedom as restrictions, and cut the log-likelihood 1.92 below its peak to get a likelihood interval | 7 heads in 10 against a fair coin: $-2\ln\Lambda = 1.65$, $p = 0.20$. The 95% likelihood interval for $p$ is $[0.393, 0.915]$ | `likelihood-lab` (lr-cut), `test-lab` (chi2) | Trap: degrees of freedom from the number of parameters instead of the number of restrictions. Edge: the exact binomial test gives $p = 0.34$ here, so the $\chi^2$ approximation is rough at $n = 10$; a parameter on its boundary gives a 50:50 mixture of $\chi^2_0$ and $\chi^2_1$ | N, N, C, I |

Transfer screens: lesson 32 compares execution algorithms; lesson 34 tests return normality; lesson 35 tests a signal without a distributional model; lesson 37 compares trade-arrival rates (homework IT3).

**Level IT4: Many tests at once** (`lvl.testing.multiple`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 38 | `lsn.testing.fwer` Many tests, many false alarms | Compute the family-wise error rate $1 - (1 - \alpha)^m$ for independent tests and the expected number of false rejections $m\alpha$ | 20 independent factor tests at 5%, all pure noise. Chance of at least one "discovery"? (0.64) | `multiple-testing` (fwer) | Trap: each test's 5% stays 5% for the family (`mc.stats.more-tests-same-error`). Edge: dependence changes the FWER but not the expected count $m\alpha$ | N, N, C, N |
| 39 | `lsn.testing.bonferroni-holm` Bonferroni and Holm | Apply Bonferroni ($p\le\alpha/m$) and Holm's step-down procedure, prove Bonferroni from the union bound, and show Holm rejects at least as many | p-values 0.004, 0.011, 0.019, 0.030, 0.400 at $\alpha = 0.05$: Bonferroni rejects one, Holm two | `multiple-testing` (corrections), `derivation` | Trap: Holm continuing past its first failure. Edge: Bonferroni holds under any dependence and is conservative when tests are correlated | M, M, O, N |
| 40 | `lsn.testing.fdr-bh` False discovery rate and Benjamini-Hochberg | Apply BH at level $q$ (reject the $k$ smallest, where $k$ is the largest $i$ with $p_{(i)}\le iq/m$), interpret FDR as the expected share of false discoveries, and choose FDR for screening | The same five p-values: BH at 5% rejects four. Why is that not reckless? | `multiple-testing` (bh, drag the line) | Trap: Bonferroni controls the FDR target (`mc.stats.bonferroni-controls-fdr`); BH applied step-down (stopping at the first p-value above its line). Edge: with p-values 0.010, 0.030, 0.032, 0.9 BH rejects three although 0.030 sits above its own line | M, N, C, N |
| 41 | `lsn.testing.forking-paths` p-hacking and forking paths | Quantify how choosing the best of $k$ analyses inflates false positives, and list the defences: pre-registration, held-out data, counting every analysis tried | An analyst tries 5 independent definitions of "momentum" and reports the best. False-positive rate? (0.23) | `multiple-testing` (forking), `sim-runner` | Trap: trying several analyses is harmless if each is honest (`mc.stats.p-hacking-is-harmless`). Edge: correlated variants inflate less, but still above 5%; optional stopping (peeking every 10 observations) inflates without any second analysis | N, M, N, M |
| 42 | `lsn.testing.backtest-overfitting` Backtest overfitting and the $t > 3$ hurdle | Compute the expected best Sharpe ratio among $N$ noise strategies ($E[\max]$ of $N$ normals times the standard error), and explain the deflated Sharpe ratio and Harvey, Liu and Zhu's hurdle of $t\approx 3$ for new factors | 200 noise strategies, 2 years each. Best in-sample annual Sharpe? ($E[\max] = 2.746$ for 200 normals, times SE $1/\sqrt 2$: about 1.94) Then reveal its out-of-sample Sharpe | `multiple-testing` (zoo) | Trap: the best backtest shows skill (`mc.stats.best-backtest-is-skill`). Edge: the number of trials is rarely reported; correlated strategies make the effective $N$ smaller; $\sqrt{2\ln N}$ overstates $E[\max]$ at these $N$ (3.26 against 2.75) | N, C, M, N |

Transfer screens: every lesson in IT4 is a transfer lesson: factor screens (38, 40), a strategy search (41), a backtest zoo (42).

### 2.3 Course RG: Linear Regression (`crs.linreg`, 5 levels, 27 lessons)

1.4 fitted lines and planes by projection. This course turns the fit into a statistical model: what the coefficients estimate, how precisely, under which assumptions, what breaks when the assumptions fail, and every number statsmodels prints. Its last level is the exit.

**Level RG1: The model** (`lvl.linreg.model`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 43 | `lsn.linreg.model` From a fitted line to a statistical model | Write $\mathbf y = X\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\varepsilon$ with its assumptions (linearity, $E[\boldsymbol\varepsilon\mid X] = \mathbf 0$, constant variance, no autocorrelation, full rank, normality for exact small-sample inference) and say what each buys | 1.4 lesson 43 gave a stock's beta as 1.3 from 12 months. A different 12 months: would you get 1.3 again? What would make the difference? | `regression-lab` (refits), `least-squares-fit` | Trap: OLS needs the predictors to be normal (`mc.stats.x-must-be-normal`). Edge: "linear" means linear in $\boldsymbol\beta$, so $x^2$ and $\ln x$ are allowed; fixed against random $X$ | M, S, S, C |
| 44 | `lsn.linreg.interpretation` Reading coefficients; regression to the mean | Interpret slope and intercept in units, compute $b_1 = r\,s_y/s_x$, and explain regression to the mean, including why regressing $x$ on $y$ gives a different line | Fund managers' returns in two years correlate at 0.3. The top decile in year 1 averages $z = 1.755$; predict its year-2 average. ($0.3\times 1.755 = 0.53$: no loss of skill required) | `regression-lab`, `gaussian-2d` | Trap: the reverse regression's slope is $1/b_1$ (it is $r^2/b_1$). Edge: the intercept means nothing when $x = 0$ lies outside the data; centring $x$ makes it the mean response | N, N, C, P |
| 45 | `lsn.linreg.r-squared` Sums of squares and $R^2$ | Decompose $\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}$ (with an intercept), compute $R^2$ and adjusted $R^2$, show $R^2 = r^2$ in simple regression, and explain why $R^2$ cannot tell a right model from a wrong one | Add a predictor that is pure noise. $R^2$ rises. Can it ever fall? (No; adjusted $R^2$ can) | `regression-lab` (decomposition), `summary-table` (noise-predictor toggle) | Trap: a higher $R^2$ is a better model (`mc.stats.higher-r2-is-better-model`). Edge: without an intercept statsmodels prints an uncentred $R^2$; daily return models have $R^2$ near 0.01 and can still be valuable | N, N, C, N |
| 46 | `lsn.linreg.ols-mle` OLS is maximum likelihood; AIC and BIC | Derive that maximising the normal log-likelihood gives OLS, compute $\ell = -\tfrac n2\left(\ln 2\pi + \ln\tfrac{\text{SSE}}{n} + 1\right)$, and compute $\text{AIC} = -2\ell + 2p$ and $\text{BIC} = -2\ell + p\ln n$ as statsmodels does ($p$ counts the coefficients, not $\sigma^2$) | Model A: SSE 185 with 5 coefficients. Model B: SSE 200 with 2. Same 60 observations. Which does AIC prefer? (B: $\text{AIC}_B - \text{AIC}_A = 60\ln\tfrac{200}{185} - 6 = -1.32$; BIC prefers B by 7.6) | `likelihood-lab` (regression), `derivation`, `summary-table` | Trap: comparing AIC across different responses ($\ln y$ against $y$) or different samples. Edge: the MLE of $\sigma^2$ divides SSE by $n$, $s^2$ by $n - p$; R's `AIC()` counts $\sigma^2$ and differs from statsmodels by 2 | X, N, N, C |
| 47 | `lsn.linreg.gauss-markov` The Gauss-Markov theorem | Prove that OLS has the smallest variance among linear unbiased estimators under the classical assumptions, and name what is lost without each assumption | Another slope estimator: the line through the first and last points. It is linear and unbiased. Is its variance smaller? | `estimator-compare` (ols, other-linear), `derivation` | Trap: "best" means best of all estimators (biased ridge and non-linear resistant estimators can beat OLS). Edge: under unequal error variances weighted least squares beats OLS (1.4 lesson 46); with heavy tails least absolute deviations can | O, C, S, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 43 refits a beta on new windows; lesson 44 is the managers' persistence puzzle; lesson 45 shows a useful daily-return model with $R^2 = 0.01$.

**Level RG2: Inference for coefficients** (`lvl.linreg.inference`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 48 | `lsn.linreg.beta-sampling` The sampling distribution of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ | Derive $E[\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}] = \boldsymbol\beta$ and $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}$, and in simple regression $\text{Var}(b_1) = \sigma^2/\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$; say how $n$, noise and the spread of $x$ drive precision | Refit the beta on 1,000 new samples; the slopes build a histogram. Predict its width before running | `regression-lab` (refits), `derivation`, `matrix` | Trap: more spread in $x$ makes the slope worse (it makes it better). Edge: all $x$ equal gives infinite variance; one extreme $x$ dominates $\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$ | X, N, P, O |
| 49 | `lsn.linreg.sigma-df` Estimating $\sigma^2$: residual degrees of freedom | Show $E[\text{SSE}] = (n - p)\sigma^2$ by $\text{tr}(I - H) = n - p$, estimate $s^2 = \text{SSE}/(n - p)$, and read $n - p$ as the dimension of the residual space | Fit a line through two points: SSE = 0. Does that mean there is no noise? | `least-squares-fit` (r3), `derivation` | Trap: dividing by $n$ or $n - 1$. Edge: $p = n$ leaves no degrees of freedom; $\text{tr}(H) = p$ | N, N, X, O |
| 50 | `lsn.linreg.t-and-ci` Standard errors, $t$-statistics and coefficient intervals | Compute $\text{se}(\hat\beta_j) = s\sqrt{[(X^\top X)^{-1}]_{jj}}$, $t = \hat\beta_j/\text{se}$ with $n - p$ degrees of freedom, its p-value and the 95% interval, and test $\beta_j = 1$ as well as $\beta_j = 0$ | A CAPM regression on 60 months gives $\hat\beta = 1.3$, se 0.12. Is the stock riskier than the market? ($t = 2.5$ on 58 df, $p = 0.015$; interval $[1.06, 1.54]$) | `regression-lab`, `summary-table`, `test-lab` ($t$) | Trap: $P > \lvert t\rvert$ is the chance the coefficient is zero (`mc.stats.p-of-coef-is-probability-it-is-zero`); testing against 0 when the question is 1. Edge: individually insignificant coefficients can be jointly significant (lesson 51) | N, N, I, C |
| 51 | `lsn.linreg.f-test` The $F$-test: overall and nested | Compute $F = \frac{(\text{SSE}_r - \text{SSE}_u)/q}{\text{SSE}_u/(n - p)}$ and its $R^2$ form, and explain how two individually insignificant coefficients can be jointly significant | Two highly correlated factors each have $t\approx 1.2$, yet the overall $F$ has $p < 0.001$. How? | `test-lab` (f-nested), `regression-lab` (collinear), `summary-table` | Trap: concluding neither factor matters from the individual $t$'s. Edge: with $q = 1$, $F = t^2$; $F$ compares nested models only | N, N, C, X |
| 52 | `lsn.linreg.prediction-intervals` Confidence against prediction intervals | Compute the interval for the mean response at $x_0$ and the prediction interval for a new observation, and explain why the second never shrinks below about $\pm t\,s$ | Predict tomorrow's return given today's signal, against predicting the average return on all days with this signal. Which interval is wider, and does more data fix it? | `regression-lab` (ci-band, pi-band) | Trap: the confidence band used for one new observation. Edge: both bands widen away from $\bar x$; extrapolation (`mc.stats.extrapolation-is-safe`) | N, C, N, I |
| 53 | `lsn.linreg.numpy-statsmodels` Regression in NumPy, checked against statsmodels | Compute $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$, $s^2$, standard errors, $t$, p-values, $R^2$ and $F$ with NumPy (by QR, not an explicit inverse) and match each to `sm.OLS(...).fit()` | Your NumPy coefficients match statsmodels; your standard errors are 1.02 times too small. Find the bug ($s^2$ divided by $n$; $\sqrt{50/48} = 1.02$ for $n = 50$, $p = 2$) | `code-cell` (`web`), `summary-table`, `predict-output` | Trap: forgetting the constant column (`sm.add_constant`). Edge: statsmodels does not drop a collinear column; it warns through the condition number | T, N, K, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 50 tests whether a stock's beta exceeds 1; lesson 51 tests whether two extra factors add to CAPM; lesson 52 is a return forecast's honest interval.

**Level RG3: When the assumptions fail** (`lvl.linreg.assumptions`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 54 | `lsn.linreg.residual-plots` Non-linearity and residual plots | Read residuals against fitted values to detect curvature, repair it with a transformation or a polynomial term, and explain why a significant slope does not validate the model | A straight line through curved data: $R^2 = 0.9$ and a huge $t$. The residual plot is a U | `regression-lab` (violations: curve; resid-fitted), `card-sort` | Trap: a significant slope means a good model (`mc.stats.significant-slope-means-good-model`). Edge: Anscombe's quartet: four data sets with the same coefficients and $R^2\approx 0.67$ and four different stories | P, S, M, C |
| 55 | `lsn.linreg.heteroscedasticity` Unequal variances and HC standard errors | Detect non-constant error variance, compute White's covariance $(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\text{diag}(e_i^2)X(X^\top X)^{-1}$ and statsmodels' HC1 to HC3, and say what changes (standard errors) and what does not (coefficients) | Fan-shaped residuals: the nominal 95% slope interval covers far less than 95% on the coverage ladder. Repair it | `regression-lab` (fan; cov switch), `ci-ladder` (coverage), `summary-table` | Trap: HC errors change $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$. Edge: HC0 undercovers in small samples, and HC3 is the usual small-sample choice [check: Long and Ervin 2000]; the Breusch-Pagan test | C, N, M, N |
| 56 | `lsn.linreg.autocorrelated-errors` Correlated errors, Durbin-Watson and Newey-West | Compute $\text{DW} = \sum(e_t - e_{t-1})^2/\sum e_t^2\approx 2(1 - r_1)$, explain why positively correlated errors make OLS standard errors too small, and use HAC (Newey-West) errors, including for overlapping returns | Monthly observations of 12-month returns regressed on a valuation ratio: $t = 3$. Is that 240 independent observations? | `regression-lab` (ar1), `summary-table` (autocorr), `acf-lab` | Trap: a Durbin-Watson near 2 is bad (`mc.stats.durbin-watson-two-is-bad`); DW sees lag 1 only. Edge: $h$-period overlapping returns give MA($h - 1$) errors; DW is invalid with a lagged dependent variable; $r_1 = 0.7$ gives $\text{DW}\approx 0.6$ | N, C, M, N |
| 57 | `lsn.linreg.non-normal-errors` Non-normal errors: skew, kurtosis, Omnibus and Jarque-Bera | Compute residual skewness, kurtosis and $\text{JB} = \tfrac n6\left(S^2 + \tfrac{(K - 3)^2}{4}\right)$, read the Omnibus test, and explain that normality matters for exact small-sample inference, not for unbiasedness | Residuals of a return regression have kurtosis 9. Is $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ biased? (No) What is at risk? | `qq-plot`, `summary-table`, `regression-lab` (tails) | Trap: non-normal errors bias the coefficients. Edge: at large $n$ the CLT rescues the $t$-tests; heavy tails make OLS inefficient, not wrong | N, C, N, M |
| 58 | `lsn.linreg.influence` Hat values, outliers and Cook's distance | Compute hat values $h_{ii}$, studentised residuals and Cook's distance, and decide what to do with an influential point | One crash day moves a stock's beta from 0.9 to 1.4. Delete it? | `regression-lab` (influence, draggable points) | Trap: delete every outlier (`mc.stats.always-delete-outliers`); a high-hat-value point with a small residual is harmless (it may have pulled the line onto itself). Edge: $\sum_i h_{ii} = p$, so the average is $p/n$; flag above $2p/n$; Cook's distance above $4/n$ | N, C, P, N |
| 59 | `lsn.linreg.diagnose` Diagnosis: match the plot to the problem | Match residual, QQ, scale-location and influence plots to the violation each shows, and choose the remedy | Six diagnostic panels, six problems, one remedy each | `regression-lab` (diagnostics), `qq-plot`, `card-sort` | Trap: any wiggle means a violation (`mc.stats.any-wiggle-means-non-normal`). Edge: two problems at once (curvature that looks like unequal variance) | S, S, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 55 regresses returns of stocks with different volatilities; lesson 56 is the overlapping-returns predictability regression; lesson 58 is the crash-day beta.

**Level RG4: Multiple regression in practice** (`lvl.linreg.practice`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | `lsn.linreg.partial-effects` Holding the others fixed | Interpret a multiple-regression coefficient as a partial effect through Frisch-Waugh-Lovell (1.4 lesson 44), draw the added-variable plot whose slope it is, and read its standard error there | A fund's alpha is significant against the market alone and vanishes when momentum is added. Which regression answers "does the manager add value beyond known factors"? | `regression-lab` (two predictors, plane, avplot) | Trap: a multiple coefficient read as a simple slope. Edge: a coefficient can change sign when a correlated predictor enters | C, N, M, N |
| 61 | `lsn.linreg.omitted-variable` Omitted-variable bias | Derive $E[b_1] = \beta_1 + \beta_2\delta$ for the short regression, where $\delta$ is the slope of $x_2$ on $x_1$, sign it, and spot confounders | True model: $r = 0.2 + 1.0\,\text{MKT} + 0.5\,\text{SMB}$; SMB on MKT has slope 0.3. What does regressing $r$ on MKT alone give? (1.15) | `regression-lab` (ovb, show-why), `derivation` | Trap: the slope is the effect of intervening on $x$ (`mc.stats.correlation-is-causation`). Edge: no bias when the omitted variable is irrelevant or uncorrelated with $x_1$; adding a "bad control" that $x_1$ itself causes creates bias | X, N, C, M |
| 62 | `lsn.linreg.collinearity` Collinearity, VIF and the condition number | Compute $\text{VIF}_j = 1/(1 - R_j^2)$, relate it to the inflation of $\text{se}(\hat\beta_j)$, and read statsmodels' condition-number note | Two factor returns correlate at 0.95. By how much are their standard errors inflated? ($\text{VIF} = 10.26$; factor $\sqrt{10.26} = 3.2$) | `regression-lab` (collinear), `quadratic-form` (coefficient ellipse), `summary-table` | Trap: a large condition number means the fit failed (`mc.stats.condition-number-is-an-error`). Edge: collinearity does not bias, and does not hurt prediction inside the data's range; the sum of two collinear coefficients is precise while their difference is not | N, N, C, N |
| 63 | `lsn.linreg.dummies-interactions` Categorical predictors, interactions and transformations | Encode a category with $k - 1$ dummies, interpret dummy, interaction and log coefficients (log-level: $100(e^{b} - 1)\%$ per unit; log-log: an elasticity), and avoid the dummy trap (1.4 lesson 45) | Does a stock's beta rise in high-volatility regimes? Add a regime dummy and its interaction with the market | `regression-lab`, `data-table` | Trap: with an interaction, the main effect read as the average effect. Edge: a log-level coefficient of 0.10 means 10.5%, not 10%; predicting $y$ from a model for $\ln y$ needs a correction | N, C, N, M |
| 64 | `lsn.linreg.model-selection` Choosing a model: adjusted $R^2$, AIC, BIC and held-out error | Compare models by adjusted $R^2$, AIC, BIC and held-out error, explain why in-sample fit rewards complexity, and preview cross-validation (2.1) | Polynomials of degree 1 to 10 on 30 points: in-sample error falls for ever; held-out error is U-shaped. Which degree? | `regression-lab`, `forecast-lab` (split), `code-cell` | Trap: in-sample fit is skill (`mc.stats.in-sample-fit-is-skill`); the lowest AIC is the true model.
