Edge: BIC is consistent for the true order, AIC predicts better; p-values after stepwise selection are invalid (lesson 41 again) | N, C, M, N |
| 65 | `lsn.linreg.logistic` When $y$ is 0 or 1: logistic regression | Write the logistic model and its log-likelihood, fit it by Newton's method (iteratively reweighted least squares), interpret coefficients as log odds ratios, and explain why OLS on 0/1 data fails | Will a limit order fill? A straight line in the distance from the mid predicts probabilities of 1.3 and $-0.2$. With $\text{logit}\,p = 2 - 1.5d$: $p = 0.88$ at the mid, 0.27 two ticks away; each tick multiplies the odds by 0.22 | `likelihood-lab` (logistic [mode to add]), `code-cell`, `regression-lab` | Trap: a coefficient read as a change in probability. Edge: perfectly separated data have no finite MLE; the negative log-likelihood is the cross-entropy loss of 2.1 | X, N, C, K |

Transfer screens: lesson 60 is factor attribution; lesson 61 omits the size factor; lesson 63 is a regime-dependent beta; lesson 65 is fill probability.

**Level RG5: The summary table and the exit** (`lvl.linreg.summary`, 4 lessons)

All four lessons use the shipped fixture `fix.stats.fund-60` (60 months of a fund's excess returns on market and momentum factors, generated with Student $t_4$ errors; section 4.2 prints its table) and two more fixtures for variety.

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 66 | `lsn.linreg.summary-top` The summary table I: the header block | Recompute and explain No. Observations, Df Residuals, Df Model, Covariance Type, R-squared, Adj. R-squared, F-statistic, Prob (F-statistic), Log-Likelihood, AIC and BIC from SSE, SST, $n$ and $p$ | Five header numbers are blanked; fill them from the others | `summary-table` (predict mode) | Trap: Df Model counts the constant (it does not: $p - 1$). Edge: AIC and BIC count coefficients, not $\sigma^2$; a no-constant model switches to uncentred $R^2$ | N, N, N, C |
| 67 | `lsn.linreg.summary-coefs` The summary table II: coefficients | Recompute coef, std err, t, $P > \lvert t\rvert$ and the $[0.025, 0.975]$ interval for each coefficient, and state what each claims and does not | mom's $P > \lvert t\rvert$ is 0.079. A colleague says "92% chance momentum matters". Fix the sentence | `summary-table`, `test-lab` ($t$) | Trap: the p-value as the chance the coefficient is zero. Edge: OLS uses the $t$ distribution with $n - p$ df (here $t_{57, 0.975} = 2.002$), not 1.96 | N, N, I, C |
| 68 | `lsn.linreg.summary-diagnostics` The summary table III: the diagnostic block | Recompute and interpret Omnibus, Prob(Omnibus), Skew, Kurtosis, Durbin-Watson, Jarque-Bera, Prob(JB), Cond. No. and the numbered notes, and say which numbers above them each one undermines | DW 0.6 and Cond. No. $3\times 10^4$ appear in a colleague's table. What do they say, and which numbers higher up can you no longer trust? | `summary-table` (break-it toggles), `qq-plot` | Trap: kurtosis printed as excess (statsmodels prints Pearson's, normal = 3); a Durbin-Watson near 2 is bad. Edge: Omnibus (D'Agostino-Pearson) and JB can disagree at small $n$; the condition number depends on the units of $X$ | N, C, M, N |
| 69 | `lsn.linreg.exit-rehearsal` Rehearsal: fit, match and explain | Fit a two-predictor regression in NumPy, match every number in the statsmodels table, and write the explanation of each block against the exit rubric | The exit in miniature, with Bertie marking the explanation against section 4.2's rubric | `code-cell` (`web`), `summary-table`, `free-response` | Trap: everything from the course at once: $n$ against $n - p$, Pearson against excess kurtosis, AIC's $p$, the uncentred $R^2$. Edge: a fixture with a near-duplicate predictor (Cond. No. above $10^3$) | T, N, N, C |

### 2.4 Course BY: Bayesian Inference (`crs.bayesian`, 2 levels, 11 lessons)

The other way to use a likelihood: put a distribution on the parameter, multiply, renormalise. The course runs from grids to conjugacy, contrasts credible with confidence intervals, and ends with what Bayesian thinking buys a quant: shrinkage of many noisy estimates, priors as penalties, and sampling when there is no formula.

**Level BY1: Updating** (`lvl.bayesian.updating`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 70 | `lsn.bayesian.grid-update` Updating on a grid | Compute a posterior on a grid by multiplying prior by likelihood point by point and renormalising, and read posterior probabilities from it | A coin's $p$ is 0.2, 0.4, 0.6 or 0.8, equally likely. It shows 3 heads in 4 flips. Posterior? ($\tfrac{2}{73}, \tfrac{12}{73}, \tfrac{27}{73}, \tfrac{32}{73}$) | `posterior-lab` (grid-binomial), `mass-editor` | Trap: forgetting to renormalise; the posterior is the likelihood. Edge: a zero prior stays zero whatever the data (Cromwell's rule) | N, N, C, D |
| 71 | `lsn.bayesian.beta-binomial` Conjugacy: the Beta-binomial | Update $\text{Beta}(\alpha, \beta)$ to $\text{Beta}(\alpha + k, \beta + n - k)$, read $\alpha$ and $\beta$ as pseudo-counts, and write the posterior mean as a weighted average of the prior mean and $\hat p$ | A new trader wins 8 of 10 trades; the desk's prior is Beta(10, 10). Posterior mean? (0.6: one third of the way from 0.5 to 0.8) | `posterior-lab` (beta-binomial) | Trap: the posterior mean is the MLE. Edge: Beta(1, 1) is uniform; Beta($\tfrac12, \tfrac12$) is Jeffreys' prior | X, N, N, C |
| 72 | `lsn.bayesian.sequential` Sequential updating equals batch updating | Show that updating flip by flip gives the same posterior as updating on all the data at once, in any order, for conditionally independent data | Update after each of 10 trades, then on all 10 at once. Do you land in the same place? | `posterior-lab` (sequential), `odds-ledger` | Trap: the order of the data matters (`mc.stats.order-of-data-matters`); using the same data to set the prior and again in the likelihood. Edge: with dependent data the factorisation fails | C, N, O, D |
| 73 | `lsn.bayesian.credible-vs-confidence` Credible against confidence intervals | Compute equal-tailed and highest-density credible intervals from a Beta posterior, state the probability claim each makes, and contrast it with the confidence interval's | 8 of 10 with a uniform prior gives Beta(9, 3). The 95% equal-tailed credible interval is $[0.482, 0.940]$; Wilson gives $[0.490, 0.943]$. Same numbers, different claims: which? | `posterior-lab` (interval), `ci-ladder` | Trap: credible and confidence intervals say the same thing (`mc.stats.credible-equals-confidence`). Edge: for a normal mean with a flat prior they coincide numerically; for a skewed posterior the highest-density interval is shorter than the equal-tailed one | I, C, N, I |
| 74 | `lsn.bayesian.predictive` The posterior predictive | Compute the probability that the next observation is a success, $\frac{\alpha + k}{\alpha + \beta + n}$, derive Laplace's rule of succession $\frac{k + 1}{n + 2}$, and use predictive draws to check a model | A new strategy won all 5 of its first trades. Probability it wins the next, with a uniform prior? ($\tfrac67$, not 1) | `posterior-lab` (predictive) | Trap: plugging in $\hat p = 1$. Edge: the predictive spread exceeds the plug-in spread because it includes parameter uncertainty; a posterior predictive check compares simulated data with the real data | N, N, C, M |

**Level BY2: Priors, shrinkage and computation** (`lvl.bayesian.shrinkage`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 75 | `lsn.bayesian.prior-sensitivity` Priors: sensitivity and "uninformative" | Measure how much the posterior moves with the prior at small and large $n$, and show that a flat prior on $p$ is not flat on the log odds | Two analysts, priors Beta(1, 1) and Beta(20, 5), watch the same trades. When do they agree? | `posterior-lab` (two priors) | Trap: a flat prior is neutral (`mc.stats.flat-prior-is-neutral`); the prior is irrelevant at small $n$. Edge: an improper prior can give an improper posterior | D, N, M, C |
| 76 | `lsn.bayesian.normal-normal` Normal-normal updating: precision weighting | Derive the posterior mean $\frac{\mu_0/\tau^2 + n\bar x/\sigma^2}{1/\tau^2 + n/\sigma^2}$ and variance $\left(1/\tau^2 + n/\sigma^2\right)^{-1}$, and combine a prior view with noisy data | Prior for a stock's annual expected return: 6% with SD 4%. Five years of data average 20% with volatility 30%. Posterior mean? (7.1%, SD 3.8%: five years barely move you) | `posterior-lab` (normal-normal), `derivation` | Trap: averaging prior and data with equal weights. Edge: as $n\to\infty$ the data dominate; Black-Litterman is this formula with matrices | X, N, N, C |
| 77 | `lsn.bayesian.shrinkage-groups` Shrinkage across many groups: empirical Bayes | Shrink many noisy estimates towards their pooled mean with weight $\frac{\tau^2}{\tau^2 + s^2}$ on the raw estimate, estimate $\tau^2$ from the spread of the estimates, and explain Stein's paradox | 20 managers' alphas each have SE 4%; true alphas spread with SD 2% around 0. The top manager shows 12%. Best estimate of his true alpha? ($0.2\times 12 = 2.4\%$) | `posterior-lab` (groups) | Trap: taking the top raw estimate at face value (the winner's curse). Edge: James-Stein beats separate estimation in total squared error whenever there are 3 or more groups; if the estimates spread less than their noise, $\hat\tau^2 < 0$ and everything shrinks to the pool | N, N, C, M |
| 78 | `lsn.bayesian.regression-ridge` A prior is a penalty: Bayesian regression | Show that the posterior mode under a $N(\mathbf 0, \tau^2 I)$ prior on $\boldsymbol\beta$ is ridge regression with $\lambda = \sigma^2/\tau^2$, and that a Laplace prior gives the lasso | 1.4 lesson 47 chose ridge's $\lambda$ by taste. What belief about the coefficients is it? | `least-squares-fit` (ridge), `derivation`, `posterior-lab` | Trap: the posterior mode equals the posterior mean in general (true for Gaussians, false for the lasso's Laplace prior). Edge: the prior acts on $\boldsymbol\beta$, so feature scaling matters; the intercept is left unpenalised | X, O, C, N |
| 79 | `lsn.bayesian.bayes-factors` Comparing models: Bayes factors | Compute a Bayes factor as a ratio of marginal likelihoods, update model odds with it, and contrast it with a p-value (Lindley's paradox) | 60 wins in 100 trades. Fair coin against $p\sim\text{Uniform}(0, 1)$: the Bayes factor is 1.10 in favour of the fair coin, while the two-sided p-value is 0.057 | `odds-ledger` (bayes-factor), `posterior-lab` | Trap: the likelihood ratio is the posterior (`mc.stats.lr-is-posterior`). Edge: Bayes factors depend strongly on the prior under the alternative; vaguer priors favour the null | N, N, C, N |
| 80 | `lsn.bayesian.mcmc` When there is no formula: sampling the posterior | Run a random-walk Metropolis sampler on a one-parameter posterior, check it against the grid answer, and diagnose burn-in, step size and autocorrelation of the draws | The posterior for a Student-$t$ location with a normal prior has no closed form. Can a random walk that prefers uphill steps draw from it? | `code-cell` (`micropython-ok`), `posterior-lab` (grid comparison), `acf-lab` (trace autocorrelation) | Trap: treating correlated draws as independent (2.2 lesson 10's effective sample size). Edge: steps too small stick, steps too large are all rejected; a rejected proposal repeats the current value, and dropping it biases the sample | C, T, M, K |

Transfer screens: lesson 71 rates a new trader; lesson 76 combines a return forecast with data; lesson 77 shrinks managers' alphas; lesson 79 weighs evidence of skill.

### 2.5 Course SM: Stationary Models (`crs.arma`, 4 levels, 21 lessons)

From prices to returns, from returns to their dependence over time, and from dependence to models that forecast. The course follows FPP's order (graphics, decomposition, toolbox, ARIMA) with Tsay's returns-first framing, and adds the one result every quant needs from it: autocorrelation changes the standard error of a mean.

**Level SM1: Prices, returns and stationarity** (`lvl.arma.stationarity`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.arma.prices-returns` Prices wander, returns hover | Transform a price series into simple and log returns, state which add over time (log) and which across a portfolio (simple), and recognise a random walk in prices | Two charts: one is a share price, one is a running total of coin flips. Which is which? Now difference both | `series-lab` (transforms: log, diff, logdiff) | Trap: simple returns add over time (`mc.ts.simple-returns-add`); prices are stationary (`mc.ts.prices-are-stationary`). Edge: $\ln(1 + r)\approx r$ only for small $r$; a 50% fall needs a 100% rise | S, N, M, C |
| 2 | `lsn.arma.stationarity` Weak stationarity | Define weak stationarity (constant mean, constant variance, autocovariance depending only on the lag), test it with two windows, and classify series with reasons | Drag two windows across a random walk and across its returns; compare the two windows' histograms | `series-lab` (split windows) | Trap: a stationary series has no visible pattern (AR(1) with $\varphi = 0.95$ wanders for weeks and is stationary). Edge: GARCH returns are stationary yet have changing conditional variance; strict against weak stationarity | C, S, P, C |
| 3 | `lsn.arma.random-walk-variance` The random walk: variance grows with time | Derive $\text{Var}(P_t - P_0) = t\sigma^2$, the $\sqrt h$ scaling of $h$-day volatility and annualisation by $\sqrt{252}$ | Daily volatility 1%. Ten-day volatility? (3.16%) Annual? (15.9%) | `series-lab`, `sim-runner` (fan of paths), `derivation` | Trap: annualising by 252 instead of $\sqrt{252}$. Edge: autocorrelated returns break $\sqrt h$ (the variance ratio, homework SM1); a random walk with drift has mean $t\mu$ | N, N, X, C |
| 4 | `lsn.arma.decomposition` Trend, seasonality and decomposition | Decompose a series into trend, seasonal and remainder by classical moving averages and STL, choose additive or multiplicative, and remove seasonality by a seasonal difference | Daily trading volume has a weekly pattern and a trend. Pull them out | `series-lab` (decompose) | Trap: detrending a random walk (`mc.ts.trend-or-random-walk`: differencing, not a fitted line, removes a stochastic trend). Edge: multiplicative seasonality becomes additive after logs; a centred moving average loses observations at both ends | C, N, N, S |
| 5 | `lsn.arma.transform-choose` Making a series stationary | Choose the transform sequence (log, difference, seasonal difference) that makes each of six series stationary, and recognise over-differencing | Six series, six recipes: match them | `series-lab` (classify), `acf-lab`, `card-sort` | Trap: differencing a series that is already stationary. Edge: differencing white noise gives an MA(1) with $\theta = -1$ and lag-1 autocorrelation $-0.5$, a sign of over-differencing | S, C, N, D |

**Level SM2: Autocorrelation** (`lvl.arma.autocorrelation`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | `lsn.arma.acf` Lag plots and the autocorrelation function | Compute $r_k = \frac{\sum_{t=k+1}^{T}(x_t - \bar x)(x_{t-k} - \bar x)}{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(x_t - \bar x)^2}$ and read it from a lag-$k$ scatter | Plot today against yesterday for the series 1, 3, 2, 4, 5. What is $r_1$? (0.1) | `acf-lab` (lag-scatter, correlogram) | Trap: computing $r_k$ as an ordinary correlation of two shifted pieces (separate means and denominators). Edge: $r_0 = 1$; a trending series' ACF decays slowly from near 1; $r_k$ is biased towards 0 in short samples | N, N, C, P |
| 7 | `lsn.arma.white-noise-bands` White noise and the $\pm 1.96/\sqrt T$ bands | Use $\text{SE}(r_k)\approx 1/\sqrt T$ for white noise (lesson 2 of 1.6 again), apply the bands, and expect about one bar in twenty outside by chance | 400 days of pure noise, 20 lags: one bar pokes outside $\pm 0.098$. A signal? | `acf-lab` (bands) | Trap: one bar out means dependence (`mc.ts.one-bar-out-means-dependence`). Edge: the bands assume independence, not just zero correlation, so GARCH returns need wider bands; Bartlett's formula widens them for MA($q$) | N, N, C, D |
| 8 | `lsn.arma.ljung-box` Testing many lags at once: Ljung-Box | Compute $Q = T(T + 2)\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{r_k^2}{T - k}$, refer it to $\chi^2_m$ (minus fitted ARMA parameters for residuals), and use it to check a model's residuals | $T = 100$, $r_1 = 0.2$, $r_2 = 0.1$, $r_3 = -0.05$. Jointly significant? ($Q = 5.42$, $p = 0.14$) | `acf-lab` (ljung-box), `test-lab` (chi2) | Trap: not reducing the degrees of freedom for residuals of a fitted ARMA($p, q$). Edge: Box-Pierce against Ljung-Box; the choice of $m$ trades power across lags | N, N, C, N |
| 9 | `lsn.arma.pacf` Partial autocorrelation | Define the PACF at lag $k$ as the last coefficient of an AR($k$) regression, compute $\phi_{22} = \frac{\rho_2 - \rho_1^2}{1 - \rho_1^2}$, and read cut-offs | AR(1) with $\varphi = 0.8$ has $\rho_2 = 0.64$. Does lag 2 add anything once lag 1 is known? ($\phi_{22} = 0$) | `acf-lab` (pacf), `regression-lab` (partial residuals) | Trap: an AR model's ACF cuts off (`mc.ts.ar-acf-cuts-off`: its PACF does). Edge: $\phi_{11} = \rho_1$ | N, X, C, M |
| 10 | `lsn.arma.long-run-variance` Autocorrelation and the standard error of a mean | Derive $\text{Var}(\bar x)\approx\frac{\sigma^2}{T}\left(1 + 2\sum_{k\ge 1}\rho_k\right)$, the AR(1) effective sample size $T\frac{1 - \varphi}{1 + \varphi}$, and connect both to Newey-West (1.6 lesson 56) | A spread with $\varphi = 0.9$ is observed for 1,000 days. How many independent days is that worth? (52.6) | `acf-lab`, `sampling-lab`, `derivation` | Trap: $\sigma/\sqrt T$ for autocorrelated data. Edge: negative autocorrelation makes the mean more precise than iid; smoothed hedge-fund returns overstate Sharpe ratios (homework SM2) | X, N, C, N |

**Level SM3: ARMA models** (`lvl.arma.models`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 | `lsn.arma.ar1` AR(1): mean reversion and half-life | Derive the stationary AR(1)'s mean $\frac{c}{1 - \varphi}$, variance $\frac{\sigma^2}{1 - \varphi^2}$, ACF $\varphi^k$ and half-life $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln\varphi}$ | A spread sits 2.0 above its mean with $\varphi = 0.9$. Expected gap in 5 days? (1.18) Half-life? (6.6 days) | `arma-lab` (AR1, irf), `derivation` | Trap: $\varphi = 1$ reverts slowly (`mc.ts.phi-one-reverts`: it never reverts). Edge: negative $\varphi$ alternates; $\lvert\varphi\rvert\ge 1$ is not stationary | N, N, X, C |
| 12 | `lsn.arma.ma` MA($q$): finite memory | Derive the MA(1) ACF $\rho_1 = \frac{\theta}{1 + \theta^2}$, $\rho_k = 0$ for $k > 1$, show $\lvert\rho_1\rvert\le\tfrac12$, and recognise MA structure in bid-ask bounce and overlapping returns | Trade-to-trade price changes show negative lag-1 autocorrelation and nothing beyond. Why? (Roll's model: bounce between bid and ask) | `arma-lab` (MA), `acf-lab` (theory) | Trap: MA models have long memory (`mc.ts.ma-has-long-memory`). Edge: $\theta$ and $1/\theta$ give the same ACF (lesson 14) | N, N, C, D |
| 13 | `lsn.arma.ar2` AR(2): the stationarity triangle and cycles | Decide stationarity from the triangle $\varphi_1 + \varphi_2 < 1$, $\varphi_2 - \varphi_1 < 1$, $\lvert\varphi_2\rvert < 1$ and from the characteristic roots, find complex roots ($\varphi_1^2 + 4\varphi_2 < 0$) and the cycle length | Drag $(\varphi_1, \varphi_2)$ until the series cycles. At $(1.0, -0.5)$: what period? (8 steps: $\cos\omega = \frac{\varphi_1}{2\sqrt{-\varphi_2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2}$) | `arma-lab` (triangle, roots), `complex-plane` | Trap: checking $\lvert\varphi_1\rvert < 1$ and $\lvert\varphi_2\rvert < 1$ separately. Edge: the boundary is a unit root; 1.4 lesson 70's eigenvalue test is the same condition | P, C, N, P |
| 14 | `lsn.arma.invertibility-wold` Invertibility, ARMA and Wold's theorem | Combine AR and MA into ARMA($p, q$), require roots outside the unit circle for stationarity and invertibility, write AR(1) as MA($\infty$), and state Wold's decomposition | MA(1) with $\theta = 0.5$ and with $\theta = 2$ have the same ACF ($\rho_1 = 0.4$). Which should you fit? | `arma-lab` (roots), `complex-plane`, `derivation` | Trap: ignoring a common factor (ARMA(1, 1) with $\varphi = -\theta$ is white noise). Edge: invertibility makes the shocks recoverable from the past | N, C, O, N |
| 15 | `lsn.arma.identification` Identification from the ACF and PACF | Choose candidate orders from correlogram patterns (AR: PACF cuts off; MA: ACF cuts off; ARMA: both tail off) and run the Box-Jenkins cycle | Six correlograms, six models: match them | `acf-lab`, `card-sort` | Trap: an AR's ACF cuts off. Edge: sampling noise blurs cut-offs; mixed models need an information criterion | S, S, C, M |
| 16 | `lsn.arma.estimation` Estimation, AIC and residual checks | Fit ARMA models by maximum likelihood (AR($p$) by OLS as the conditional MLE), choose orders by AIC, AICc or BIC, and check residuals with Ljung-Box | Tap cells of a $(p, q)$ grid to fit each order; which wins, and is it the true model? | `arma-lab` (fit-grid), `code-cell` (`web`) | Trap: the lowest AIC is the true model (`mc.ts.lowest-aic-is-truth`). Edge: near-cancelling AR and MA roots give unstable estimates | C, N, K, M |

**Level SM4: Forecasting and evaluation** (`lvl.arma.forecasting`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17 | `lsn.arma.benchmarks` Benchmark forecasts | Produce the naive, mean, drift and seasonal naive forecasts, and explain why the naive (random-walk) forecast is the bar every price model must beat | Forecast tomorrow's price. Can anything beat "same as today"? | `forecast-lab` (naive, mean, drift, snaive) | Trap: a complex model beats the naive one by default. Edge: the drift forecast is the line through the first and last observations | N, N, C, N |
| 18 | `lsn.arma.forecast-intervals` ARMA forecasts and their intervals | Compute $h$-step AR(1) forecasts $\mu + \varphi^h(x_T - \mu)$ with error variance $\sigma^2\frac{1 - \varphi^{2h}}{1 - \varphi^2}$, and contrast with the random walk's $h\sigma^2$ | AR(1), $\mu = 0$, $\varphi = 0.8$, $\sigma = 1$, $x_T = 5$: the 5-step forecast is 1.64 with variance 2.48, saturating at 2.78. The random walk's variance at 5 steps is 5 | `forecast-lab` (fan), `derivation` | Trap: narrow intervals are better (`mc.ts.narrow-intervals-are-better`); intervals that ignore parameter uncertainty. Edge: as $h\to\infty$ the forecast is the unconditional mean | N, N, X, C |
| 19 | `lsn.arma.exp-smoothing` Exponential smoothing | Apply $\ell_t = \alpha y_t + (1 - \alpha)\ell_{t-1}$, show the weights $\alpha(1 - \alpha)^k$ decay geometrically, connect it to ARIMA(0, 1, 1), and extend it with Holt's trend | Slide $\alpha$: how much weight sits on yesterday, and how much on the last 5 observations? (Half sits in the last 5 when $\alpha = 0.129$) | `forecast-lab` (ses, holt [mode to add]) | Trap: a large $\alpha$ smooths more (it smooths less). Edge: SES is ARIMA(0, 1, 1) with $\theta = \alpha - 1$; the EWMA volatility of lesson 23 is the same filter on squared returns | N, X, C, N |
| 20 | `lsn.arma.evaluation` Evaluating forecasts | Compute MAE, RMSE and MASE on a test set, run rolling-origin evaluation, and never score on the training period | Model A has the lower in-sample RMSE; model B the lower rolling-origin RMSE. Which do you deploy? | `forecast-lab` (rolling-origin, errors) | Trap: in-sample fit is skill (`mc.ts.in-sample-fit-is-skill`). Edge: MASE is scale-free; MAPE breaks near zero, which is where returns live (Hyndman and Koehler 2006) | N, N, O, C |
| 21 | `lsn.arma.leakage` Leakage: why shuffled cross-validation lies | Show by experiment that shuffled K-fold flatters a time-series model, and set up expanding-window and embargoed splits | Shuffled K-fold says $R^2 = 0.3$; walk-forward says 0. Which is lying, and how? | `forecast-lab` (shuffled-cv), `code-cell` | Trap: shuffled cross-validation is fine (`mc.ts.shuffled-cv-is-fine`). Edge: overlapping labels need purging and an embargo (2.1, López de Prado) | C, M, N, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 1 uses a seeded price series; lesson 3 annualises volatility; lesson 10 is a spread's effective sample size; lesson 11 a spread's half-life; lesson 12 Roll's bid-ask model; lessons 20 and 21 evaluate a return forecaster honestly.

### 2.6 Course VL: Volatility (`crs.volatility`, 2 levels, 10 lessons)

Returns are nearly unpredictable; their size is not. This course documents the stylised facts, builds EWMA, ARCH and GARCH from the same shocks, fits GARCH by maximum likelihood, and uses the forecasts for VaR, its backtest and position sizing.

**Level VL1: Volatility clustering and its models** (`lvl.volatility.models`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 22 | `lsn.volatility.stylised-facts` Stylised facts of returns | Document fat tails (kurtosis, QQ plot), volatility clustering (the ACF of squared and absolute returns), volatility asymmetry, and near-zero autocorrelation of returns on a seeded index-like series | The ACF of returns is flat; the ACF of squared returns stays positive for 50 lags. Independent? | `series-lab` (facts), `acf-lab` (squares), `qq-plot` | Trap: uncorrelated returns are independent (`mc.ts.uncorrelated-returns-are-independent`); volatility is constant (`mc.ts.volatility-is-constant`). Edge: Ljung-Box on squared returns (McLeod-Li) is a test for ARCH effects | D, M, N, C |
| 23 | `lsn.volatility.ewma` Rolling and EWMA volatility | Compute rolling-window and EWMA variance $\sigma_t^2 = \lambda\sigma_{t-1}^2 + (1 - \lambda)r_{t-1}^2$, read the weights $(1 - \lambda)\lambda^k$, and use RiskMetrics' $\lambda = 0.94$ | One 10% crash day in a calm 1% market: the 60-day rolling volatility jumps to 1.63% and falls off a cliff 60 days later; EWMA jumps to 2.63% and decays smoothly. Which is the better risk number? | `garch-lab` (ewma), `series-lab` (rolling) | Trap: the rolling window's "ghost" drop read as calm returning. Edge: EWMA's weights halve every $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln 0.94} = 11.2$ days; EWMA is GARCH with $\omega = 0$ and $\alpha + \beta = 1$ | N, N, C, M |
| 24 | `lsn.volatility.arch` ARCH(1): today's shock sets tomorrow's variance | Write $\sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha r_{t-1}^2$, derive the unconditional variance $\frac{\omega}{1 - \alpha}$, and show that ARCH has fat tails with normal shocks: kurtosis $\frac{3(1 - \alpha^2)}{1 - 3\alpha^2}$ | With normal shocks and $\alpha = 0.5$, what is the kurtosis of the returns? (9) | `garch-lab` (arch1), `derivation` | Trap: conditional and unconditional variance confused. Edge: the kurtosis is finite only if $3\alpha^2 < 1$ | X, N, C, N |
| 25 | `lsn.volatility.garch` GARCH(1, 1): persistence and the long-run variance | Write $\sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha r_{t-1}^2 + \beta\sigma_{t-1}^2$, compute persistence $\alpha + \beta$, the long-run variance $\frac{\omega}{1 - \alpha - \beta}$ and the half-life of a volatility shock | $\omega = 0.02$, $\alpha = 0.08$, $\beta = 0.90$ in daily $\%^2$: long-run daily volatility? (1%) Half-life? (34 days) | `garch-lab` (garch11) | Trap: $\alpha + \beta = 1$ is harmless (`mc.ts.alpha-plus-beta-one-is-fine`: no long-run variance exists). Edge: IGARCH is EWMA; $\alpha + \beta$ near 1 is typical for daily equity data | N, N, N, C |
| 26 | `lsn.volatility.asymmetry` Volatility asymmetry and GJR-GARCH | Read a news impact curve, add the GJR term $\gamma\,\mathbb 1(r_{t-1} < 0)\,r_{t-1}^2$, and test whether bad news raises volatility more than good news | Equal-sized moves of 2%, one down and one up. With $\alpha = 0.03$, $\gamma = 0.10$: how much does each add to tomorrow's variance? (0.52 against 0.12) | `garch-lab` (gjr [mode to add], nic) | Trap: GARCH predicts direction (`mc.ts.garch-predicts-direction`: it predicts size). Edge: EGARCH models $\ln\sigma^2$ and needs no positivity constraints | C, N, P, X |

**Level VL2: Fitting, forecasting and risk** (`lvl.volatility.risk`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 27 | `lsn.volatility.garch-mle` Fitting GARCH by maximum likelihood | Write the Gaussian log-likelihood $-\tfrac12\sum_t\left(\ln 2\pi + \ln\sigma_t^2 + r_t^2/\sigma_t^2\right)$, maximise it inside $\omega > 0$, $\alpha, \beta\ge 0$, $\alpha + \beta < 1$, and check the standardised residuals | Drag $(\alpha, \beta)$ on the log-likelihood map to its peak, then let the optimiser try | `garch-lab` (fit, map), `code-cell` (`web`) | Trap: no constraints (the optimiser walks to $\alpha + \beta > 1$ or negative $\omega$). Edge: the start value $\sigma_1^2$; the Gaussian likelihood still gives consistent estimates when the shocks are not normal (QMLE); Student-$t$ shocks | X, P, C, K |
| 28 | `lsn.volatility.vol-forecast` Volatility forecasts and their term structure | Forecast $\sigma_{T+h}^2 = \sigma_L^2 + (\alpha + \beta)^{h-1}(\sigma_{T+1}^2 - \sigma_L^2)$ and the variance of an $h$-day return as the sum, and compare with $\sqrt h$ scaling | Tomorrow's forecast volatility is 2% daily, the long-run level 1%, persistence 0.98. 20-day volatility? (8.36%, against 8.94% from $\sqrt{20}\times 2\%$ and 4.47% from the long-run level) | `garch-lab` (term) | Trap: $\sqrt h$ times today's volatility. Edge: mean-reverting volatility gives the term structure of implied volatility its shape (1.7) | N, N, C, X |
| 29 | `lsn.volatility.realised` Realised volatility and judging volatility forecasts | Compute realised variance from intraday returns, explain why a squared daily return is a noisy proxy, and score forecasts with MSE and QLIKE against realised variance | A perfect variance forecast scores a low $R^2$ against squared daily returns. Is the forecast bad? (No: $r_t^2 = \sigma_t^2 z_t^2$, and $z_t^2$ is noise; Andersen and Bollerslev 1998) | `garch-lab`, `forecast-lab` (errors) | Trap: judging a volatility model by its $R^2$ on $r_t^2$. Edge: microstructure noise biases realised variance at very high frequencies | N, C, M, N |
| 30 | `lsn.volatility.var-backtest` Value at risk and exceedance backtests | Compute one-day VaR from a volatility forecast, count exceedances, and test the count with the binomial and Kupiec's likelihood ratio | 250 days at 99% VaR: you expect 2.5 exceedances and see 7. Bad luck? ($P(X\ge 7) = 0.014$; Kupiec $LR = 5.50$, $p = 0.019$) | `garch-lab` (var), `test-lab` (binom-exact) | Trap: VaR is the worst loss. Edge: a constant-volatility VaR passes the count test while its exceedances cluster (Christoffersen's independence test); expected shortfall | N, N, C, N |
| 31 | `lsn.volatility.vol-targeting` Volatility targeting and standardised returns | Scale a position by forecast volatility to target constant risk, show standardised returns $r_t/\hat\sigma_t$ have kurtosis much closer to 3, and judge the effect on a Sharpe ratio | One strategy, two sizings: constant position against volatility-targeted. Which has the steadier risk, and why might its Sharpe ratio change? | `garch-lab`, `series-lab`, `qq-plot` | Trap: using today's return in today's volatility estimate (look-ahead). Edge: targeting does not remove jump risk; standardised residuals still show some excess kurtosis | C, N, M, T |

Transfer screens: every VL lesson is a transfer lesson on a seeded index-like series: risk numbers (23, 28), VaR and its backtest (30), position sizing (31).

### 2.7 Course CO: Unit Roots and Cointegration (`crs.cointegration`, 2 levels, 12 lessons)

Series with no mean to return to: how to test for a unit root when the usual $t$ table is wrong, why regressions on prices invent relationships, and the one case where a regression on prices is right: cointegration. The course ends with the exit.

**Level CO1: Unit roots and spurious regression** (`lvl.cointegration.unit-roots`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 32 | `lsn.cointegration.unit-root` Unit roots and integration order | Define I(0) and I(1), show that at $\varphi = 1$ shocks never fade, and relate the order of integration to the number of differences needed | Slide $\varphi$ to 1 with the same shocks: watch the impulse response stop decaying | `arma-lab` ($\varphi$ to 1, irf), `series-lab` | Trap: a near-unit root is a unit root (or the reverse): $\varphi = 0.99$ has a half-life of 69 days and looks like a random walk in a year of data. Edge: I(2) series (prices with a drifting growth rate) | P, N, M, C |
| 33 | `lsn.cointegration.dickey-fuller` The Dickey-Fuller test: the null is not $t$ | Regress $\Delta y_t$ on a constant and $y_{t-1}$, compute the $t$-ratio on $\gamma = \varphi - 1$, and compare it with Dickey-Fuller critical values (5%: $-2.86$ asymptotically, $-2.89$ at $T = 100$), not $-1.645$ | Simulate the $t$-ratio under a true unit root. With the usual one-sided $-1.645$, how often would you "reject the unit root"? (46% of the time) | `test-lab` (simulated null), `regression-lab` | Trap: normal or $t$ critical values. Edge: three cases with different critical values (no constant $-1.94$, constant $-2.86$, trend $-3.41$) | N, C, N, N |
| 34 | `lsn.cointegration.adf-kpss` ADF and KPSS | Add lagged differences (augmented Dickey-Fuller) chosen by AIC, run KPSS, whose null is stationarity (5% critical value 0.463), and combine the two verdicts | ADF fails to reject; KPSS rejects. What do you conclude? (Evidence for a unit root from both directions) | `test-lab`, `series-lab`, `code-cell` (`web`) | Trap: failing to reject a unit root proves one (`mc.stats.fail-to-reject-means-accept`). Edge: unit-root tests have low power against $\varphi = 0.98$; a structural break mimics a unit root | S, C, S, T |
| 35 | `lsn.cointegration.spurious` Spurious regression | Show by simulation that regressing one independent random walk on another gives $\lvert t\rvert > 1.96$ far more than 5% of the time, and recognise the Granger-Newbold symptom $R^2 > \text{DW}$ | Two independent random walks, $T = 100$: the median $\lvert t\rvert$ is 4.5. How often does $\lvert t\rvert$ exceed 1.96? (76%) | `coint-lab` (spurious), `regression-lab`, `summary-table` | Trap: a high $R^2$ means a relationship (`mc.ts.high-r2-means-a-relationship`); regressing prices on prices (`mc.ts.regress-prices-not-returns`). Edge: the $t$-statistic grows with $T$ instead of settling; differencing removes the problem unless the series are cointegrated | N, C, N, C |
| 36 | `lsn.cointegration.arima` ARIMA: modelling the differences | Fit ARIMA($p, d, q$) by differencing $d$ times and fitting ARMA, forecast on the original scale, and explain why I(1) forecasts have intervals growing like $\sqrt h$ | Forecast a price with ARIMA(0, 1, 0) and with ARIMA(1, 1, 0). Do the long-run forecasts differ? (Both flatten; their intervals keep growing) | `forecast-lab`, `arma-lab`, `code-cell` (`web`) | Trap: over-differencing; a constant in a $d = 1$ model is a drift. Edge: ARIMA(0, 1, 1) is simple exponential smoothing | N, C, X, K |

**Level CO2: Cointegration and pairs** (`lvl.cointegration.pairs`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 37 | `lsn.cointegration.common-trend` Common stochastic trends | Define cointegration (two I(1) series with an I(0) combination $y_t - \beta x_t$), find the $\beta$ that makes the spread stationary, and give economic cases (futures and spot, dual listings, ETF and basket) | Drag the hedge ratio until the spread stops wandering | `coint-lab` (levels, scatter, spread) | Trap: correlation is cointegration (`mc.ts.correlation-is-cointegration`). Edge: for two series the cointegrating vector is unique up to scale | C, P, N, C |
| 38 | `lsn.cointegration.corr-vs-coint` Correlation is not cointegration | Recognise pairs with high return correlation and no cointegration, and the reverse, and explain why return correlation measures short-run co-movement while cointegration is a long-run constraint | Pair A: return correlation 0.9, spread wanders. Pair B: return correlation 0.2, spread mean-reverts. Which do you trade as a pair? | `coint-lab` (pair kinds) | Trap: the same. Edge: a cointegrated pair can have near-zero return correlation when the spread's noise dominates daily moves | C, N, P, S |
| 39 | `lsn.cointegration.engle-granger` Engle-Granger in two steps | Estimate $\beta$ by OLS on levels, test the residuals for a unit root against Engle-Granger critical values (5%: $-3.34$ asymptotically for two series with a constant), and explain why Dickey-Fuller values are wrong here | The residual ADF statistic is $-3.0$. Reject with Dickey-Fuller's $-2.86$; do not reject with Engle-Granger's $-3.34$. Which applies, and why? | `coint-lab` (adf), `test-lab` | Trap: Dickey-Fuller critical values for residuals (`mc.ts.df-critical-values-for-residuals`: $\beta$ was fitted to make the residuals look stationary). Edge: which series is $y$ changes the answer in finite samples; Johansen for more than two series | N, C, N, N |
| 40 | `lsn.cointegration.ecm` Error correction and the half-life of a spread | Fit $\Delta y_t = \alpha(y_{t-1} - \beta x_{t-1}) + \dots$, read the speed of adjustment, and compute the spread's half-life from its AR(1) coefficient | A spread's AR(1) coefficient is 0.95. Half-life? (13.5 days) | `coint-lab` (ecm), `sde-stepper` (Ornstein-Uhlenbeck) | Trap: the sign of $\alpha$ (it must pull $y$ back towards the long-run relation). Edge: Granger's representation theorem: cointegration and error correction are the same property | N, X, C, N |
| 41 | `lsn.cointegration.var-johansen` Two series together: VAR, Granger causality and Johansen | Write a bivariate VAR(1), check stability by eigenvalues (1.4 lesson 70), test Granger causality with an $F$-test, and read the Johansen trace test's rank decision | $A = \begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.2\\0.1&0.6\end{pmatrix}$: stable? (Eigenvalues 0.7 and 0.4: yes.) Does the ETF lead the basket, or the reverse? | `linear-dynamics` (VAR), `test-lab` ($F$), `code-cell` (`web`) | Trap: Granger causality is causation. Edge: a VAR in differences discards the long-run relation of a cointegrated pair; the vector error-correction model keeps it | C, N, N, T |
| 42 | `lsn.cointegration.pairs-trading` Pairs trading without look-ahead | Build a z-score rule on the spread with entry and exit bands, estimate $\beta$ and the bands on a formation period only, and evaluate on a later trading period (Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst) | In-sample spread trading looks perfect. Re-estimate $\beta$ on the formation year only and trade the next: what happens? | `coint-lab` (trading, split) | Trap: a full-sample $\beta$ in the backtest (`mc.ts.full-sample-beta-in-backtest`). Edge: costs on two legs; cointegration that breaks after a corporate event | C, M, N, P |
| 43 | `lsn.cointegration.exit-rehearsal` Rehearsal: test a pair and explain returns against prices | Run Engle-Granger by hand and with `statsmodels.tsa.stattools.coint` on a supplied pair, report the decision with the right critical values, and write the explanation of why models use returns and not prices against the exit rubric | The exit in miniature, with Bertie marking the explanation against section 4.5's rubric | `coint-lab`, `code-cell` (`web`), `free-response` | Trap: everything at once: Dickey-Fuller values, a full-sample $\beta$, a high $R^2$ in levels. Edge: a pair whose returns correlate at 0.9 and which is not cointegrated | T, C, N, N |

Transfer screens: every CO lesson is a transfer lesson: spot and futures (37), ETF against basket (38, 41), a pair's half-life (40), a pairs strategy (42).

### 2.8 Coverage checks

**1.6: the curriculum's cover list.**

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow ASSESSMENT.md 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission; code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission; long-form items are self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's.
- **Size and mix.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. Levels of 6 or 7 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**. Exactly one long-form item (a proof, derivation or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, at difficulty 4 unless stated) and exactly one code drill sit inside those counts. At least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items per set. Expected time about 2 hours. 1.6: 15 sets, 132 items (9 sets of 8, 6 of 10). 2.2: 8 sets, 68 items (6 of 8, 2 of 10).
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, and each point names the model-answer lines that evidence it (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Code drills** list their hidden tests here so CI can check that the reference solution passes and that each known-wrong solution fails its named test (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.9). `micropython-ok` drills use only `math`, lists and the shipped `zqrandom` (a pure-Python port of the engine RNG, catalogue decision 7); `web` drills use NumPy, SciPy and statsmodels.
- **Generators** named per level are `gen.stats.*` and `gen.ts.*` families built on the widget catalogue's section 9 generators, with tidy answers by construction (residuals projected out of the column space so coefficients are round; counts chosen so p-values are exact; series rejected when their test statistic sits within 1.0 of a critical value).

Each level below gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill, generators, and two example problems in full: example A is the long-form item with its model answer (numbered lines) and rubric, example B a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and its decision point.

### 3.1 Course ES

**ES1 Sampling distributions** (`set.estimators.sampling`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the unbiasedness of $S^2$. Code drill `drl.stats.mean-se` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `mean_sd_se(xs)` returns the mean, the sample SD (divisor $n - 1$) and the standard error, raising `ValueError` for fewer than 2 values; `required_n(sigma, target_se)` returns the smallest whole $n$ with $\sigma/\sqrt n\le$ target. Hidden tests: the orders 120, 60, 150, 100, 80, 130, 70, 110, 80 give $(100, 30, 10)$; $[1, 2, 3, 4]$ gives $(2.5, 1.2910, 0.6455)$ to $10^{-4}$; `[5]` raises; `required_n(40, 4) == 100`; `required_n(40, 7) == 33`; `required_n(0.9, 0.06) == 225` (in floating point $(0.9/0.06)^2 = 225.00000000000006$, so a bare `ceil` returns 226 [verified]). Known-wrong solutions: divisor $n$ (fails test 1); bare `math.ceil` (fails test 6). Generators: `gen.stats.predict-se`, `gen.stats.required-n`, `gen.stats.ecdf-quantile`, `gen.stats.t-prob`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For independent $X_1, \dots, X_n$ with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$, prove that $E\left[\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \bar X)^2\right] = (n - 1)\sigma^2$, explain where the "missing" $\sigma^2$ went, and say whether $S = \sqrt{S^2}$ is unbiased for $\sigma$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $X_i - \bar X = (X_i - \mu) - (\bar X - \mu)$. (2) Expand and sum: $\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2 = \sum_i (X_i - \mu)^2 - 2(\bar X - \mu)\sum_i (X_i - \mu) + n(\bar X - \mu)^2$. (3) Since $\sum_i (X_i - \mu) = n(\bar X - \mu)$, the last two terms combine: $\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2 = \sum_i (X_i - \mu)^2 - n(\bar X - \mu)^2$. (4) Take expectations: $E\sum_i (X_i - \mu)^2 = n\sigma^2$, and $E[n(\bar X - \mu)^2] = n\,\text{Var}(\bar X) = n\cdot\frac{\sigma^2}{n} = \sigma^2$ (lesson 2, using independence). (5) So $E\left[\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2\right] = n\sigma^2 - \sigma^2 = (n - 1)\sigma^2$, and $S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2$ is unbiased. (6) The leap explained: measuring from $\bar X$ instead of $\mu$ costs exactly the spread of $\bar X$ around $\mu$, one $\sigma^2$ in total, because $\bar X$ is the point that minimises the sum of squares (lesson 3's movable centre). (7) Equivalently, the $n$ residuals satisfy one linear constraint, $\sum_i (X_i - \bar X) = 0$, so only $n - 1$ of them are free. (8) $S$ is biased low: $\sqrt{\cdot}$ is strictly concave, so by Jensen $E[S] = E\sqrt{S^2} < \sqrt{E[S^2]} = \sigma$ unless $S^2$ is constant.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the decomposition (line 1). 2: the expansion and the cross-term simplification (lines 2, 3). 2: both expectations with the independence step named (line 4). 1: conclusion (line 5). 2: an explanation of the lost $\sigma^2$, by the minimising centre or the constraint (lines 6, 7). 2: Jensen's inequality applied correctly to $S$ (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A risk team estimates 99% one-day VaR from the last 250 daily returns as the 3rd smallest return. (a) What is the probability that fewer than 3 of the 250 returns fall below the true 1% quantile $q$, so that the estimate sits above $q$ and understates the risk? (b) NumPy's `np.quantile(r, 0.01)` with its default method returns a different value: which order statistics does it use? (c) What should the team report?
  *Answer.* (a) The number of returns below $q$ is $X\sim\text{Bin}(250, 0.01)$. The 3rd smallest return exceeds $q$ exactly when $X\le 2$: $P(X\le 2) = 0.543$. More often than not the estimate understates the loss; even $P(X = 0) = 0.081$. (b) The default method interpolates at position $0.01\times(250 - 1) = 2.49$ counted from 0, that is between the 3rd and 4th smallest returns, 49% of the way: less extreme still. (c) The decision point: the naive report is one number with no error bar. The honest report gives the estimator and its uncertainty (a binomial or DKW band, lesson 4), uses a longer window or a model (2.2 course VL), and states the quantile method, because two libraries disagree on the same data.

**ES2 Judging estimators** (`set.estimators.quality`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the MSE decomposition and the best shrinkage factor. Code drill `drl.stats.resistant` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `median(xs)`, `trimmed_mean(xs, prop)` (drop $\lfloor n\cdot\text{prop}\rfloor$ from each end of the sorted data) and `mad(xs, scaled=True)` (times 1.4826). Hidden tests: `trimmed_mean([100, 1, 2, 3, 4], 0.2) == 3.0` (unsorted on purpose); `median([4, 1, 3, 2]) == 2.5`; `mad([1, 2, 3, 4, 100], scaled=False) == 1`; scaled gives 1.4826; the input list is not mutated. Known-wrong: trimming before sorting (fails test 1); sorting in place (fails test 5). Generators: `gen.stats.bias-var`, `gen.stats.german-tank`, `gen.stats.mom-fit`, `gen.stats.mse-crossover`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove $\text{MSE}(\hat\theta) = \text{bias}(\hat\theta)^2 + \text{Var}(\hat\theta)$. (b) For $\hat\mu_c = c\bar X$ with independent data of mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$, find the $c$ that minimises the MSE, and explain why it cannot be used as it stands.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $\hat\theta - \theta = (\hat\theta - E\hat\theta) + (E\hat\theta - \theta)$. (2) Square and take expectations: $E(\hat\theta - \theta)^2 = E(\hat\theta - E\hat\theta)^2 + 2(E\hat\theta - \theta)E(\hat\theta - E\hat\theta) + (E\hat\theta - \theta)^2$. (3) The middle term is zero because $E(\hat\theta - E\hat\theta) = 0$, and $E\hat\theta - \theta$ is a constant. (4) So $\text{MSE} = \text{Var} + \text{bias}^2$. (5) For $c\bar X$: bias $(c - 1)\mu$, variance $c^2\sigma^2/n$, so $\text{MSE}(c) = (c - 1)^2\mu^2 + c^2\sigma^2/n$. (6) Setting the derivative $2(c - 1)\mu^2 + 2c\sigma^2/n$ to zero gives $c^* = \frac{\mu^2}{\mu^2 + \sigma^2/n}$, which is below 1: shrinking towards 0 always helps a little. (7) The second derivative $2\mu^2 + 2\sigma^2/n > 0$ confirms a minimum. (8) But $c^*$ depends on the unknown $\mu$: plugging in $\bar X$ makes the factor random and the analysis no longer holds. Usable shrinkage needs information from outside the sample: a prior (lesson 76) or many related estimates (lesson 77).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the split (line 1). 2: the expansion (line 2). 2: the vanishing cross-term with its reason (line 3). 1: bias and variance of $c\bar X$ (line 5). 2: $c^*$ with the derivative (line 6) and the second-order check (line 7). 2: why $c^*$ is not directly usable, and what would make shrinkage usable (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* For normal data, which divisor $d$ makes $\hat\sigma^2_d = \frac1d\sum_i (X_i - \bar X)^2$ have the smallest MSE? Give the MSE at $d = n - 1$, $n$ and $n + 1$ for $n = 5$.
  *Answer.* $\text{SS} = \sum (X_i - \bar X)^2 = \sigma^2 W$ with $W\sim\chi^2_{n-1}$, so $E[\text{SS}] = (n - 1)\sigma^2$ and $\text{Var}(\text{SS}) = 2(n - 1)\sigma^4$. Then $\text{MSE}(d) = \sigma^4\left[\frac{2(n - 1)}{d^2} + \left(\frac{n - 1}{d} - 1\right)^2\right]$. With $u = 1/d$ the derivative is $4(n - 1)u + 2(n - 1)\left((n - 1)u - 1\right) = 0$, so $u = \frac{1}{n + 1}$: $d = n + 1$, with minimum MSE $\frac{2\sigma^4}{n + 1}$. For $n = 5$: $d = 4$ gives $0.5\sigma^4$, $d = 5$ gives $0.36\sigma^4$, $d = 6$ gives $0.333\sigma^4$. Decision point: the unbiased choice ($n - 1$) has the largest error of the three; whether to prefer it depends on whether bias or total error matters for the use, and the optimum relies on normality (with fatter tails the variance term grows and a larger divisor helps more).

**ES3 Maximum likelihood** (`set.estimators.likelihood`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Pareto tail-index MLE. Code drill `drl.stats.mle-fit` (d4, `web`): `fit_normal(xs)` (closed form), `fit_exponential(xs)` and `fit_student_t(xs)` by `scipy.optimize.minimize` on $(\mu, \ln s, \ln(\nu - 2))$. Hidden tests: the normal fit equals $(\bar x, \hat\sigma)$ with divisor $n$ to $10^{-10}$; exponential $\hat\lambda = 1/\bar x$; on three seeded return samples the learner's $t$ log-likelihood is within $10^{-4}$ of `scipy.stats.t.fit`'s or above it; $\nu > 2$ always. Known-wrong: optimising $s$ directly (returns `nan` on seed 2); minimising $+\ell$ (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.stats.mle` (Bernoulli, Poisson, exponential, geometric, Rayleigh), `gen.stats.mle-boundary`, `gen.stats.lr-compare`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Losses beyond a threshold $x_m = 2\%$ follow a Pareto law with density $f(x) = \alpha x_m^\alpha x^{-(\alpha + 1)}$ for $x\ge x_m$. (a) Derive the MLE of the tail index $\alpha$. (b) Check it is a maximum. (c) Compute it for the losses 2.1, 2.5, 3.0, 4.2 and 6.0 (in %), with a standard error from the Fisher information. (d) Say what a small $\alpha$ means for risk.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\ell(\alpha) = \sum_i\left[\ln\alpha + \alpha\ln x_m - (\alpha + 1)\ln x_i\right] = n\ln\alpha - \alpha\sum_i\ln\frac{x_i}{x_m} - \sum_i\ln x_i$ after collecting terms. (2) $\ell'(\alpha) = \frac{n}{\alpha} - \sum_i\ln\frac{x_i}{x_m} = 0$ gives $\hat\alpha = \frac{n}{\sum_i\ln(x_i/x_m)}$ (the Hill estimator with a known threshold). (3) $\ell''(\alpha) = -\frac{n}{\alpha^2} < 0$, so it is the maximum. (4) Here $\sum\ln(x_i/2) = 2.518$, so $\hat\alpha = 5/2.518 = 1.99$. (5) The information per observation is $I(\alpha) = \frac{1}{\alpha^2}$, so $\text{SE}\approx\hat\alpha/\sqrt n = 0.89$: five losses say very little. (6) The leap that makes it work: the log turns the power law into a linear function of $\ln(x_i/x_m)$, which are exponential with rate $\alpha$, so the problem becomes lesson 12's exponential MLE. (7) Interpretation: moments of order $\alpha$ and above are infinite; $\hat\alpha\approx 2$ puts the variance of losses on the edge of existing, so volatility-based risk measures understate tail risk.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the log-likelihood (line 1). 2: the score equation and $\hat\alpha$ (line 2). 1: second-order check (line 3). 1: the number (line 4). 2: the standard error with $I(\alpha)$ (line 5). 1: the reduction to the exponential (line 6). 1: the risk interpretation (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten limit orders are watched for 60 seconds. Seven fill, at times adding to 140 s; three have not filled at 60 s. Fill times are exponential with rate $\lambda$. Find the MLE of $\lambda$ and compare it with two naive answers.
  *Answer.* A filled order contributes the density $\lambda e^{-\lambda t_i}$; an unfilled one contributes the survival probability $P(T > 60) = e^{-60\lambda}$. So $\ell(\lambda) = 7\ln\lambda - \lambda(140 + 3\times 60) = 7\ln\lambda - 320\lambda$, and $\hat\lambda = \frac{7}{320} = 0.0219$ per second (mean fill time 45.7 s). Naive answer 1, ignore the unfilled orders: $\frac{7}{140} = 0.05$, which more than doubles the rate because the slow orders are exactly the ones dropped. Naive answer 2, treat unfilled orders as filling at 60 s: $\frac{10}{320} = 0.031$, still too fast. Decision point: censored observations are information (they say "longer than 60 s"); the likelihood uses them through the survival function.

**ES4 Large-sample theory** (`set.estimators.asymptotics`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Cramér-Rao bound. Code drill `drl.stats.sharpe-se` (d4, `micropython-ok`): `sharpe(rets)` (mean over SD with divisor $n - 1$), `sharpe_se_iid(rets)` $=\sqrt{(1 + \widehat{SR}^2/2)/n}$, `annualise(sr, se, periods)`. Hidden tests: constant returns raise `ValueError`; a fixed 10-value list gives the reference values to $10^{-9}$; `annualise(1/sqrt(252), x, 252)` multiplies both by $\sqrt{252}$; at $\widehat{SR} = 0$ the SE is $1/\sqrt n$. Known-wrong: annualising the SE by 252 (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.stats.fisher-info`, `gen.stats.wald-interval`, `gen.stats.delta`, `gen.stats.sufficient`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the Cramér-Rao bound: if $T$ is unbiased for $\theta$ from $n$ independent observations with density $f(x; \theta)$ satisfying the usual regularity conditions, then $\text{Var}(T)\ge\frac{1}{nI(\theta)}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $U = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\sum_i\ln f(X_i; \theta)$, the score. (2) $E[U] = 0$: for one observation $E\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ln f\right] = \int\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\,dx = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\int f\,dx = 0$, swapping derivative and integral (the regularity condition), and the score is a sum. (3) $\text{Var}(U) = nI(\theta)$, since the terms are independent with variance $I(\theta)$ each. (4) Unbiasedness: $\theta = E[T] = \int T(\mathbf x)L(\theta; \mathbf x)\,d\mathbf x$. Differentiate both sides: $1 = \int T\frac{\partial L}{\partial\theta}\,d\mathbf x = \int T\,U\,L\,d\mathbf x = E[TU]$. (5) Since $E[U] = 0$, $\text{Cov}(T, U) = E[TU] = 1$. (6) The leap: we want a lower bound on a variance and we know a covariance, so Cauchy-Schwarz for covariances (1.5) is the tool: $\text{Cov}(T, U)^2\le\text{Var}(T)\,\text{Var}(U)$. (7) So $1\le\text{Var}(T)\cdot nI(\theta)$, which is the bound. (8) Equality needs $T$ to be an affine function of $U$, which is why efficient estimators exist only in special families (the exponential families, for example $\bar X$ for the Poisson mean).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the score defined (line 1). 2: $E[U] = 0$ with the swap named (line 2). 1: $\text{Var}(U) = nI$ (line 3). 2: differentiating the unbiasedness identity (line 4). 1: the covariance (line 5). 2: Cauchy-Schwarz applied with its motivation (lines 6, 7). 1: the equality condition (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* (a) Use the delta method to derive $\text{Var}(\widehat{SR})\approx\frac{1}{n}\left(1 + \frac{SR^2}{2}\right)$ for $\widehat{SR} = \bar X/S$ with independent normal returns. (b) How many years of daily data does a strategy with a true annual Sharpe ratio of 0.5 need before its estimate is, on average, two standard errors from zero? (c) What would make the answer worse in practice?
  *Answer.* (a) For normal data $\bar X$ and $S^2$ are independent with variances $\sigma^2/n$ and approximately $2\sigma^4/n$. With $g(\mu, v) = \mu/\sqrt v$: $\partial g/\partial\mu = 1/\sigma$ and $\partial g/\partial v = -\mu/(2\sigma^3)$. So $\text{Var}\approx\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\frac{\sigma^2}{n} + \frac{\mu^2}{4\sigma^6}\frac{2\sigma^4}{n} = \frac1n\left(1 + \frac{\mu^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)$. (b) In annual units with $Y$ years of daily data, $\text{SE}\approx\sqrt{(1 + SR^2/504)/Y}$, close to $1/\sqrt Y$. Two standard errors: $0.5 = 2/\sqrt Y$, so $Y\approx 16$ years (16.0 with the correction term). (c) Decision point: the naive answer reads a Sharpe ratio of 0.5 as "a good strategy, a few years to prove it". Fat tails, autocorrelation, and having chosen this strategy from many (lesson 42) all enlarge the error; a decade-plus requirement is why desks lean on economic reasoning and out-of-sample tests, not on the backtest's $t$.

### 3.2 Course IT

**IT1 Confidence intervals** (`set.testing.intervals`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the $t$ interval from its pivot, and what "95%" claims. Code drill `drl.stats.bootstrap` (d4, `micropython-ok`): `bootstrap(xs, stat, B, seed)` returns $B$ resampled statistics using `zqrandom`; `percentile_ci(xs, stat, B, seed, level)`. Hidden tests: seed 1 reproduces the reference list exactly; each resample has length $n$; for the nine orders of lesson 2 the bootstrap SE of the mean at $B = 5000$, seed 3, is within 5% of $\hat\sigma/\sqrt n = 9.43$ (divisor $n$, the plug-in value); the 95% interval for the median of a fixed 40-value sample matches the reference. Known-wrong: sampling without replacement (SE 0, fails test 3). Generators: `gen.stats.ci-compute`, `gen.stats.ci-interpret`, `gen.stats.wilson`, `gen.stats.var-interval`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A fund's 36 monthly alphas have mean 1.0% and SD 2.4%. (a) Derive the 95% $t$ interval from a pivot and compute it. (b) State its assumptions. (c) The portfolio manager says "so there is a 95% chance the true alpha is between 0.19% and 1.81%". Correct the sentence and explain the difference.
  *Model answer.* (1) For normal data, $T = \frac{\bar X - \mu}{S/\sqrt n}\sim t_{n-1}$ whatever $\mu$ and $\sigma$ are: a pivot. (2) So $P(-t^* \le T\le t^*) = 0.95$ with $t^* = t_{35, 0.975} = 2.030$. (3) Rearranging the event inside the probability: $P\left(\bar X - t^*S/\sqrt n\le\mu\le\bar X + t^*S/\sqrt n\right) = 0.95$. (4) Here $\text{SE} = 2.4/6 = 0.4$, and the interval is $1.0\pm 2.030\times 0.4 = [0.19\%, 1.81\%]$. (5) Assumptions: independent months, roughly normal alphas (or $n$ large enough for the CLT), a stable mean over the 36 months. (6) The probability in line 3 is about the random interval before the data arrive. Once computed, $[0.19, 1.81]$ either contains the fixed true alpha or not. (7) Correct sentence: "intervals built this way contain the true alpha in 95% of samples; this is one such interval". (8) A probability for $\mu$ itself needs a prior: a Bayesian credible interval (lesson 73), which with a flat prior happens to give the same numbers here.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the pivot and why it is one (line 1). 2: the probability statement and its rearrangement (lines 2, 3). 1: the computation (line 4). 1: assumptions, at least two (line 5). 3: the correction, with the before-and-after distinction (lines 6, 7). 1: the Bayesian alternative (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A portfolio of 200 bonds had no defaults this year. Give a 95% upper bound on the annual default rate, compare with the Wald interval and the rule of three, and say what a credit desk should report.
  *Answer.* Wald: $\hat p = 0$ gives SE 0 and the interval $[0, 0]$, which claims certainty. Exact one-sided bound: the largest $p$ with $P(X = 0) = (1 - p)^{200}\ge 0.05$ is $p = 1 - 0.05^{1/200} = 0.0149$. Rule of three: $3/n = 0.015$, the same to two figures because $\ln 0.05\approx -3$. The two-sided Clopper-Pearson interval is $[0, 0.0183]$ (upper tail 2.5%), and Wilson gives $[0, 0.0188]$. Decision point: reporting "0%" invites a capital model to treat these bonds as riskless; reporting "below 1.5% at 95% confidence, one year of data" is honest, and pooling with other years or similar issuers (lesson 77) is the way to sharpen it.

**IT2 Hypothesis tests** (`set.testing.tests`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: null p-values are uniform. Code drill `drl.stats.power` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `norm_cdf(z)` via `math.erf` (fallback series if the build lacks it, open item 7), `norm_ppf(p)` by bisection, `power_z(effect, sigma, n, alpha, sided)`, `n_for_power(effect, sigma, alpha, power, sided)`. Hidden tests: `n_for_power(0.05, 1, 0.05, 0.8, "one") == 2474`; two-sided gives 3140; `power_z(0, 1, 100, 0.05, "two")` equals 0.05 to $10^{-9}$; `norm_ppf(0.975)` within $10^{-9}$ of 1.959964. Known-wrong: two-sided power with $z_{1-\alpha}$ (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.stats.test-decide`, `gen.stats.p-value`, `gen.stats.power`, `gen.stats.fdr-share`, `gen.stats.duality`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove that if a test statistic $T$ has a continuous distribution function $F_0$ under $H_0$ and $p = 1 - F_0(T)$, then $p\sim\text{Uniform}(0, 1)$ under $H_0$. (b) A desk tests 1,000 signals that are all noise. How many p-values below 0.05 and below 0.001 should it expect, and what does the histogram look like? (c) What changes when the statistic is discrete?
  *Model answer.* (1) For $u\in(0, 1)$: $P(p\le u) = P(1 - F_0(T)\le u) = P(F_0(T)\ge 1 - u)$. (2) Because $F_0$ is continuous, $F_0(T)$ is uniform on $(0, 1)$ under $H_0$ (the probability integral transform, 1.5): $P(F_0(T)\le v) = P(T\le F_0^{-1}(v)) = F_0(F_0^{-1}(v)) = v$. (3) Hence $P(F_0(T)\ge 1 - u) = 1 - (1 - u) = u$: the distribution function of $p$ is $u$, the uniform's. (4) Expected counts: $1000\times 0.05 = 50$ below 0.05 and $1000\times 0.001 = 1$ below 0.001. (5) The histogram is flat; a spike near 0 over the flat floor is the signature of real effects, and its height over the floor estimates how many. (6) For a discrete statistic $P(p\le u)\le u$: the test is conservative, p-values come in steps, and the histogram has gaps. (7) So "about 50 signals at $p < 0.05$" is exactly what a screen of pure noise produces; any claim must beat that.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the rewriting of the event (line 1). 3: the probability integral transform used correctly, with continuity named (line 2). 1: conclusion (line 3). 2: the two counts and the flat histogram (lines 4, 5). 1: the discrete case (line 6). 1: the practical reading (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A fund screens 1,000 signals: 100 are real with an effect giving 80% power for a one-sided 5% test. (a) What share of significant signals is real? (b) The head of research proposes $\alpha = 0.005$ at the same sample size. Recompute. (c) What did the proposal cost?
  *Answer.* (a) True positives $100\times 0.8 = 80$, false positives $900\times 0.05 = 45$: share real $80/125 = 64\%$. (b) The effect in standard-error units is $\delta = z_{0.95} + z_{0.80} = 1.645 + 0.842 = 2.486$. At $\alpha = 0.005$ the cut-off is $z_{0.995} = 2.576$, so power falls to $P(Z > 2.576 - 2.486) = 0.464$. True positives 46.4, false positives $900\times 0.005 = 4.5$: share real $46.4/50.9 = 91\%$. (c) Decision point: the stricter threshold made discoveries far more trustworthy (64% to 91%) but lost 34 of the 80 real signals. The naive view that lowering $\alpha$ is free ignores power; the trade-off is set by what a false signal costs against a missed one.

**IT3 The testing toolkit** (`set.testing.toolkit`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Neyman-Pearson lemma. Code drill `drl.stats.permutation` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `perm_test_exact(a, b, alternative)` enumerating all relabellings without `itertools`; `perm_test_mc(a, b, R, seed)` returning $(b + 1)/(R + 1)$. Hidden tests: lesson 35's data give $\tfrac{1}{70}$ one-sided and $\tfrac{2}{70}$ two-sided; identical groups give $p = 1$; the Monte Carlo p-value is never 0 and is within 0.01 of the exact value at $R = 20{,}000$, seed 5. Known-wrong: Monte Carlo without the $+1$ (fails test 3 on extreme data). Generators: `gen.stats.t-choose`, `gen.stats.chi2-table`, `gen.stats.jb`, `gen.stats.lr-test`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* State and prove the Neyman-Pearson lemma for a discrete outcome space: among all tests of $H_0: f_0$ against $H_1: f_1$ with size at most $\alpha$, the test that rejects when $\frac{f_1(x)}{f_0(x)} > k$ (with $k$ chosen so its size is exactly $\alpha$) has the largest power.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $R^*$ be the likelihood-ratio region $\{x: f_1(x) > kf_0(x)\}$ with $P_0(R^*) = \alpha$, and $R$ any region with $P_0(R)\le\alpha$. (2) Compare powers: $P_1(R^*) - P_1(R) = \sum_{x\in R^*\setminus R}f_1(x) - \sum_{x\in R\setminus R^*}f_1(x)$, since the shared part cancels. (3) On $R^*\setminus R$, $f_1 > kf_0$; on $R\setminus R^*$, $f_1\le kf_0$. (4) So $P_1(R^*) - P_1(R)\ge k\left[\sum_{R^*\setminus R}f_0 - \sum_{R\setminus R^*}f_0\right] = k\left[P_0(R^*) - P_0(R)\right]$. (5) The bracket is $\alpha - P_0(R)\ge 0$ and $k\ge 0$, so $P_1(R^*)\ge P_1(R)$. (6) The idea that makes it work: think of each outcome as costing $f_0(x)$ of the size budget and buying $f_1(x)$ of power; the best buys have the highest power per unit cost, the likelihood ratio. (7) Lesson 36's die: the six has ratio $\frac{1/2}{1/6} = 3$ and the other faces 0.6, so reject on a six, size $\tfrac16$, power $\tfrac12$. (8) If no $k$ gives size exactly $\alpha$, a randomised test on the boundary outcomes reaches it.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: setting up both regions (line 1). 2: the power difference with the cancellation (line 2). 2: the two inequalities (line 3). 2: combining them (lines 4, 5). 1: the budget explanation (line 6). 1: a worked instance (line 7). 1: the randomisation remark (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Trades on a stock arrive as a Poisson process. In the 10 minutes before a news release there were 30 trades; in the 10 minutes after, 50. (a) Test equal rates with a likelihood-ratio test. (b) Test them exactly by conditioning on the total. (c) Which do you report?
  *Answer.* (a) Unrestricted MLEs are 3 and 5 a minute; under $H_0$ the common rate is 4. $-2\ln\Lambda = 2\left[30\ln\tfrac{30}{40} + 50\ln\tfrac{50}{40}\right] = 5.05$ on 1 degree of freedom: $p = 0.025$. (b) Given 80 trades in total, the number before is Bin(80, $\tfrac12$) under $H_0$; $P(X\le 30)$ doubled is $p = 0.033$. (c) Decision point: both reject at 5%, but the exact conditional test needs no large-sample approximation and gives the larger, more honest p-value; report it, and note that trades cluster (2.2), so even it overstates the evidence if arrivals are not Poisson.

**IT4 Many tests at once** (`set.testing.multiple`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why Bonferroni controls the FWER under any dependence, and what BH controls instead. Code drill `drl.stats.multiple` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `bonferroni(ps, alpha)`, `holm(ps, alpha)` and `bh(ps, q)` each return the sorted list of rejected indices in the original order. Hidden tests: $[0.004, 0.011, 0.019, 0.030, 0.400]$ at 0.05 gives $\{0\}$, $\{0, 1\}$, $\{0, 1, 2, 3\}$; $[0.010, 0.032, 0.030, 0.9]$ with BH at 0.05 gives $\{0, 1, 2\}$; ties are handled; an empty list returns an empty list. Known-wrong: BH applied step-down (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.stats.fwer`, `gen.stats.holm-set`, `gen.stats.bh-set`, `gen.stats.max-sharpe`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove that rejecting every $H_i$ with $p_i\le\alpha/m$ keeps the family-wise error rate at or below $\alpha$, whatever the dependence between tests. (b) Explain in words what Benjamini-Hochberg controls instead, and when a research desk should prefer each.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $I_0$ be the set of true nulls, with $m_0\le m$ members. (2) A family-wise error is the event $\bigcup_{i\in I_0}\{p_i\le\alpha/m\}$. (3) By the union bound (1.5, Boole), $P\left(\bigcup A_i\right)\le\sum P(A_i)$ for any events, independent or not. (4) For a true null, $P(p_i\le\alpha/m)\le\alpha/m$ (uniform or conservative p-values, homework IT2). (5) So $\text{FWER}\le m_0\cdot\alpha/m\le\alpha$. (6) No independence was used: only the union bound, which is why Bonferroni holds under any dependence and is conservative when tests are positively correlated. (7) BH controls the false discovery rate: the expected share of false rejections among all rejections, $E[V/\max(R, 1)]\le q$ (under independence or positive dependence). (8) Prefer FWER control when one false claim is costly (a single strategy going live, a regulatory claim); prefer FDR when screening many candidates for further testing, where some false leads are acceptable and power matters.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: true-null set (line 1). 1: the error event (line 2). 2: the union bound named and applied (line 3). 1: the per-test bound (line 4). 1: conclusion (line 5). 1: the dependence remark (line 6). 2: the FDR definition (line 7). 1: when to use each (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* An analyst backtests 100 independent strategies with no edge over 5 years and reports the best annual Sharpe ratio, 1.2. (a) What best Sharpe ratio does pure noise produce on average? (b) Is 1.2 evidence of skill? (c) What should the analyst have reported?
  *Answer.* (a) Each estimated annual Sharpe ratio is about $N(0, 1/5)$ (homework ES4: SE $\approx 1/\sqrt 5 = 0.447$). The expected maximum of 100 independent standard normals is 2.508 (numerical integration), so the expected best Sharpe ratio is $2.508\times 0.447 = 1.12$. (b) No: 1.2 is what noise delivers. (c) Decision point: the naive report is the winner's Sharpe ratio and its single-test $t = 1.2\times\sqrt 5 = 2.7$; the honest report states the number of trials, compares the winner with the expected maximum (or computes the deflated Sharpe ratio, Bailey and López de Prado 2014), and tests the winner on data it was not chosen on.

### 3.3 Course RG

**RG1 The model** (`set.linreg.model`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Gauss-Markov theorem. Code drill `drl.stats.simple-ols` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `simple_ols(x, y)` returns $(b_0, b_1, R^2, \text{residuals})$ and raises `ValueError` when $x$ is constant. Hidden tests: $x = (0, 1, 2)$, $y = (6, 0, 0)$ gives $(5, -3, 0.75)$ and residuals $(1, -2, 1)$; residuals sum to 0 to $10^{-12}$; $R^2$ equals the squared correlation; constant $x$ raises. Known-wrong: slope $S_{xy}/S_{yy}$ (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.stats.coef-interpret`, `gen.stats.r2`, `gen.stats.aic`, `gen.stats.regression-to-mean`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the Gauss-Markov theorem: if $\mathbf y = X\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\varepsilon$ with $X$ of full column rank, $E[\boldsymbol\varepsilon] = \mathbf 0$ and $\text{Cov}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon) = \sigma^2 I$, then for any linear unbiased estimator $\tilde{\boldsymbol\beta} = C\mathbf y$, $\text{Cov}(\tilde{\boldsymbol\beta}) - \text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})$ is positive semidefinite.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $C = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top + D$ for some matrix $D$ (any $C$ can be written so). (2) Unbiasedness for every $\boldsymbol\beta$: $E[C\mathbf y] = CX\boldsymbol\beta = \boldsymbol\beta + DX\boldsymbol\beta$, so $DX = 0$. (3) $\text{Cov}(\tilde{\boldsymbol\beta}) = C\,\sigma^2I\,C^\top = \sigma^2CC^\top$. (4) Expand: $CC^\top = (X^\top X)^{-1} + (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top D^\top + DX(X^\top X)^{-1} + DD^\top$. (5) The middle terms vanish because $DX = 0$ (and its transpose $X^\top D^\top = 0$). (6) So $\text{Cov}(\tilde{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1} + \sigma^2DD^\top = \text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) + \sigma^2DD^\top$. (7) $DD^\top$ is positive semidefinite ($\mathbf a^\top DD^\top\mathbf a = \lVert D^\top\mathbf a\rVert^2\ge 0$), which is the claim; in particular every coefficient's variance is at least OLS's. (8) What the proof used: zero-mean errors (for unbiasedness) and $\sigma^2I$ (in line 3). With unequal variances, line 3 changes and weighted least squares wins; biased or non-linear estimators are outside the comparison altogether.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the decomposition of $C$ (line 1). 2: $DX = 0$ from unbiasedness (line 2). 1: the covariance (line 3). 2: expansion and cancellation (lines 4, 5). 2: positive semidefiniteness (lines 6, 7). 1: the assumptions' roles (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Managers' standardised returns in consecutive years are bivariate normal with correlation 0.3. (a) What is the average year-1 $z$-score of the top 10%? (b) Predict that group's average in year 2. (c) A consultant concludes that top managers lose their edge. Assess the claim, and say what regressing year 1 on year 2 would show.
  *Answer.* (a) The top 10% sit above $c = z_{0.9} = 1.2816$; their mean is $\frac{\varphi(c)}{0.1} = 1.755$. (b) $E[Z_2\mid Z_1] = 0.3Z_1$, so the group averages $0.3\times 1.755 = 0.53$. (c) Decision point: the drop is regression to the mean, and it happens with constant skill whenever the year-to-year correlation is below 1. Regressing year 1 on year 2 gives slope 0.3 too, so last year's top performers also "came from" more ordinary years: the effect runs in both directions, which a story about fading skill cannot explain. The right test of persistence asks whether 0.3 differs from what pure luck gives (0) and whether it holds out of sample.

**RG2 Inference for coefficients** (`set.linreg.inference`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})$ and $E[\text{SSE}]$. Code drill `drl.stats.ols-inference` (d4, `web`): `ols_table(X, y)` returns coefficients, standard errors, $t$, p-values, 95% intervals, $R^2$, adjusted $R^2$, $F$ and its p-value, computed through `np.linalg.qr`. Hidden tests: every value matches statsmodels on 10 seeded fixtures to relative $10^{-9}$; `fix.stats.fund-60` reproduces section 4.2's printed values; a rank-deficient $X$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: $s^2$ with divisor $n$ (fails test 1 on standard errors). Generators: `gen.stats.se-from-design`, `gen.stats.coef-t`, `gen.stats.f-from-r2`, `gen.stats.pred-interval`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Under the classical assumptions, prove (a) $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}$ and (b) $E[\text{SSE}] = (n - p)\sigma^2$, and (c) explain why the residual degrees of freedom are $n - p$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\mathbf y = \boldsymbol\beta + (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\boldsymbol\varepsilon$. (2) So $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top(\sigma^2I)X(X^\top X)^{-1} = \sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}$. (3) Residuals: $\mathbf e = (I - H)\mathbf y = (I - H)\boldsymbol\varepsilon$ with $H = X(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top$, because $(I - H)X = 0$. (4) $I - H$ is symmetric and idempotent (1.4 lesson 40), so $\text{SSE} = \mathbf e^\top\mathbf e = \boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top(I - H)\boldsymbol\varepsilon$. (5) The leap: a scalar equals its own trace, and the trace lets the matrices cycle: $E[\boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top A\boldsymbol\varepsilon] = E[\text{tr}(A\boldsymbol\varepsilon\boldsymbol\varepsilon^\top)] = \text{tr}(A\,\sigma^2 I) = \sigma^2\text{tr}A$. (6) $\text{tr}(I - H) = n - \text{tr}(X(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top) = n - \text{tr}((X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top X) = n - p$. (7) So $E[\text{SSE}] = (n - p)\sigma^2$ and $s^2 = \text{SSE}/(n - p)$ is unbiased. (8) Degrees of freedom: $\mathbf e$ lies in the $(n - p)$-dimensional orthogonal complement of the column space (it satisfies the $p$ equations $X^\top\mathbf e = \mathbf 0$), just as lesson 3's residuals satisfied one equation.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta$ (line 1). 2: the covariance (line 2). 1: residuals in terms of errors (line 3). 1: idempotence (line 4). 2: the trace step with its motivation (line 5). 2: the trace of $I - H$ (line 6) and the conclusion (line 7). 1: the geometric reading (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* 120 monthly returns of a fund: CAPM has $R^2 = 0.40$; adding size and value factors gives $R^2 = 0.46$. Each added coefficient has $\lvert t\rvert < 1.6$. (a) Test whether the two factors add anything jointly. (b) Reconcile the result with the $t$-statistics. (c) What is the overall $F$ of the three-factor model?
  *Answer.* (a) $F = \frac{(0.46 - 0.40)/2}{(1 - 0.46)/(120 - 4)} = 6.44$ on $(2, 116)$ degrees of freedom: $p = 0.0022$. (b) Decision point: individual $t$'s test each factor with the other present; if size and value are correlated, each one's standard error is inflated (lesson 62) and each looks dispensable given the other, yet dropping both loses real fit. The joint $F$ asks the question that was meant. (c) $F = \frac{0.46/3}{0.54/116} = 32.9$. The $R^2$ form of $F$ needs both models fitted to the same $y$ on the same months.

**RG3 When the assumptions fail** (`set.linreg.assumptions`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the sandwich. Code drill `drl.stats.sandwich` (d4, `web`): `hc(X, e, kind)` for HC0 to HC3 and `newey_west(X, e, L)` with Bartlett weights $1 - \frac{l}{L + 1}$. Hidden tests: HC0 to HC3 match statsmodels' `cov_type` results on 5 fixtures to relative $10^{-10}$; `newey_west` with $L = 4$ on `fix.stats.fund-60` gives standard errors $(0.2794, 0.0565, 0.0780)$, statsmodels 0.15.0's `cov_type="HAC"` with `maxlags=4`, which applies no small-sample factor [verified]; $L = 0$ equals HC0. Known-wrong: weights $1 - \frac{l}{L}$ (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.stats.diagnose`, `gen.stats.dw`, `gen.stats.jb`, `gen.stats.influence`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Errors are independent with $\text{Var}(\varepsilon_i) = \sigma_i^2$, not all equal. (a) Show that OLS is still unbiased. (b) Derive the true covariance of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ and explain why the classical formula is wrong. (c) Explain White's estimator and why it works without knowing the $\sigma_i^2$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} - \boldsymbol\beta = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\boldsymbol\varepsilon$ has mean $\mathbf 0$ because $E[\boldsymbol\varepsilon] = \mathbf 0$; equal variances were never used. (2) $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\Omega X(X^\top X)^{-1}$ with $\Omega = \text{diag}(\sigma_i^2)$: bread, meat, bread. (3) The classical formula replaces $\Omega$ by $s^2I$; if large $\sigma_i^2$ sit where $x$ is far from its mean, the classical standard errors are too small (and too large in the reverse case). (4) White's idea: we cannot estimate $n$ separate $\sigma_i^2$, but we only need the $p\times p$ matrix $X^\top\Omega X = \sum_i\sigma_i^2\mathbf x_i\mathbf x_i^\top$, an average of $n$ terms. (5) Replacing each $\sigma_i^2$ by $e_i^2$ gives $\sum_i e_i^2\mathbf x_i\mathbf x_i^\top$, which estimates that average consistently even though each $e_i^2$ is a poor estimate of its own $\sigma_i^2$. (6) HC1 to HC3 rescale $e_i^2$ (by $\frac{n}{n - p}$, or by $\frac{1}{1 - h_{ii}}$ and $\frac{1}{(1 - h_{ii})^2}$) to correct the downward bias of residuals at high-hat-value points in small samples. (7) The coefficients do not change; only the standard errors, $t$'s and intervals do.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: unbiasedness (line 1). 2: the sandwich (line 2). 2: why the classical formula fails and in which direction (line 3). 3: the averaging argument (lines 4, 5). 1: HC1 to HC3 (line 6). 1: what changes and what does not (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A predictability study regresses 12-month returns, sampled every month for 20 years (240 overlapping observations), on a slow-moving valuation ratio and reports $t = 3.0$ with classical standard errors. (a) What does overlap do to the errors? (b) If the regressor is close to constant over a year, by roughly what factor is the variance of the slope understated?
