(c) Adjust the $t$, and say what the paper should have reported.
  *Answer.* (a) Consecutive 12-month returns share 11 months, so the errors are MA(11): autocorrelations $1 - \frac{k}{12}$ for $k = 1, \dots, 11$. (b) With a regressor that barely moves, the long-run variance multiplier is that of the errors: $1 + 2\sum_{k=1}^{11}\left(1 - \frac{k}{12}\right) = 12$. (c) $t\approx 3.0/\sqrt{12} = 0.87$. Decision point: 240 observations carry roughly the information of 20 non-overlapping years; the paper should report Newey-West (or Hansen-Hodrick) errors with at least 11 lags, or use non-overlapping data, and a $t$ of 3 with classical errors is no evidence at all.

**RG4 Multiple regression in practice** (`set.linreg.practice`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: omitted-variable bias. Code drill `drl.stats.vif` (d3, `web`): `vif(X)` from auxiliary regressions (constant included in $X$, as statsmodels expects). Hidden tests: matches `statsmodels.stats.outliers_influence.variance_inflation_factor` on 5 fixtures; on `fix.stats.fund-60` the two factor VIFs are 1.1115 (their correlation is 0.317); an orthogonal design gives 1. Known-wrong: VIF from pairwise correlations only (fails test 1 on a three-predictor fixture). Generators: `gen.stats.ovb`, `gen.stats.vif`, `gen.stats.dummy-interpret`, `gen.stats.logit-odds`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* The true model is $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_1 + \beta_2x_2 + \varepsilon$ with exogenous errors. (a) Derive the expected slope when $y$ is regressed on $x_1$ alone. (b) Sign the bias in a factor model where $y$ is a fund's return, $x_1$ the market and $x_2$ the size factor, with $\beta_2 = 0.5$ and a slope of 0.3 when size is regressed on the market. (c) When is there no bias?
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $x_2 = \delta_0 + \delta x_1 + u$ be the least-squares projection of $x_2$ on $x_1$, so $u$ is uncorrelated with $x_1$ in the sample. (2) Substitute: $y = (\beta_0 + \beta_2\delta_0) + (\beta_1 + \beta_2\delta)x_1 + (\beta_2u + \varepsilon)$. (3) The new error $\beta_2u + \varepsilon$ is uncorrelated with $x_1$, so the short regression's slope estimates $\beta_1 + \beta_2\delta$: $E[b_1] = \beta_1 + \beta_2\delta$. (4) Here the bias is $\beta_2\delta = 0.5\times 0.3 = 0.15$: with $\beta_1 = 1.0$ the short regression reports 1.15. (5) Sign rule: the bias has the sign of $\beta_2$ times the sign of the correlation between $x_1$ and $x_2$. (6) No bias when $\beta_2 = 0$ (the omitted variable is irrelevant) or $\delta = 0$ (it is uncorrelated with $x_1$). (7) The reading: the short slope answers "how does $y$ move with $x_1$, letting $x_2$ move as it usually does", which is a fine prediction question and the wrong causal or attribution question.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the auxiliary projection (line 1). 2: substitution (line 2). 2: why the new error is uncorrelated, and the result (line 3). 1: the number (line 4). 1: the sign rule (line 5). 1: no-bias cases (line 6). 1: the interpretation (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Two standardised factors correlate at $\rho = 0.9$. (a) By what factor is each coefficient's variance inflated? (b) Compare the variance of $\hat\beta_1 + \hat\beta_2$ with that of $\hat\beta_1 - \hat\beta_2$. (c) What does this mean for a portfolio built from the two coefficients?
  *Answer.* (a) $\text{VIF} = \frac{1}{1 - 0.81} = 5.26$ (standard errors $\times 2.29$). (b) With standardised predictors $\text{Cov}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})\propto\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\\rho&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1} = \frac{1}{1 - \rho^2}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\rho\\-\rho&1\end{pmatrix}$. So $\text{Var}(\hat\beta_1 + \hat\beta_2)\propto\frac{2 - 2\rho}{1 - \rho^2} = \frac{2}{1 + \rho} = 1.05$ and $\text{Var}(\hat\beta_1 - \hat\beta_2)\propto\frac{2}{1 - \rho} = 20$: nineteen times larger. (c) Decision point: the data pin down the combined exposure well and the split between the factors badly. A strategy that goes long one factor and short the other on the strength of the difference is betting on the noisiest number in the regression; ridge (1.4 lesson 47, lesson 78) or combining the factors is the fix.

**RG5 The summary table and the exit** (`set.linreg.summary`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: reading a suspicious table. Code drill `drl.stats.summary-fields` (d4, `web`): `diagnostics(e, X, y)` returns Log-Likelihood, AIC, BIC, Omnibus and its p-value (`scipy.stats.normaltest` is allowed), skew, kurtosis (Pearson), Jarque-Bera and its p-value, Durbin-Watson and the condition number. Hidden tests: every value matches statsmodels on `fix.stats.fund-60` and 4 other fixtures to printed precision; the no-constant fixture uses $k = p$ in AIC. Known-wrong: excess kurtosis (fails the kurtosis and JB tests); AIC counting $\sigma^2$ (fails the AIC test). Generators: `gen.stats.predict-cell` (each blanked field), `gen.stats.table-diagnose`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A colleague regresses the daily price of share A on the daily price of share B over four years and shows: R-squared 0.97, coefficient on B 1.84 with $t = 182$, Durbin-Watson 0.21, Cond. No. $2.4\times 10^4$, Jarque-Bera $p = 0.31$. He concludes A is "almost exactly 1.84 times B". Explain what each number says and what you would do.
  *Model answer.* (1) Prices of two shares are I(1) series (2.2 lesson 32); a regression of one on the other is spurious unless they are cointegrated. (2) Durbin-Watson 0.21 means residual lag-1 autocorrelation near $1 - 0.21/2 = 0.9$: the residuals wander, as they would if the spread is a random walk. (3) Every standard error, $t$ and p-value in the table assumes independent errors, so $t = 182$ is meaningless; the Granger-Newbold symptom $R^2 > \text{DW}$ (0.97 against 0.21) is present. (4) $R^2 = 0.97$ reflects two trending series, not a stable relation. (5) The condition number is large because a price level near 100 makes the constant column and B's column nearly parallel; centring B would shrink it without changing the slope. It warns about the numerics, not the inference. (6) Jarque-Bera's $p = 0.31$ says the residuals' shape looks normal; that is irrelevant when they are not independent. (7) What to do: test the residuals for a unit root with Engle-Granger critical values (2.2 lesson 39); if cointegrated, model the spread; if not, regress returns on returns, where a beta is meaningful. (8) The sentence "A is 1.84 times B" may be true on average over these four years and still be useless for prediction.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: prices are non-stationary and the regression may be spurious (line 1). 2: DW read correctly (line 2). 1: why the $t$ is meaningless (line 3). 1: $R^2$ (line 4). 1: condition number (line 5). 1: JB (line 6). 2: the remedy: test for cointegration or use returns (lines 7, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* In `fix.stats.fund-60`'s table (section 4.2) these cells are blanked: Adj. R-squared, F-statistic, AIC, BIC, the $t$ and the interval for mom. Recompute each from the other cells, given SSE $= 205.80$ and SST $= 945.62$.
  *Answer.* $R^2 = 1 - 205.80/945.62 = 0.78236$; adjusted $= 1 - 0.21764\times\frac{59}{57} = 0.7747$ (printed 0.775); $F = \frac{0.78236/2}{0.21764/57} = 102.45$ (printed 102.5); AIC $= -2(-122.11) + 2\times 3 = 250.2$; BIC $= 244.23 + 3\ln 60 = 256.5$; $t_{\text{mom}} = 0.1500/0.0839 = 1.788$; interval $0.1500\pm 2.002\times 0.0839 = [-0.018, 0.318]$ ($t_{57, 0.975} = 2.002$). Decision point: carry full precision (rounding $R^2$ to 0.782 first gives an adjusted $R^2$ of 0.774 and $F = 102.2$, neither of which matches the table); AIC and BIC count the 3 coefficients and not $\sigma^2$ (statsmodels' convention; other software adds 2); and the interval uses $t_{57}$, not 1.96.

### 3.4 Course BY

**BY1 Updating** (`set.bayesian.updating`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Beta-binomial update as a weighted average. Code drill `drl.stats.grid-posterior` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `grid_posterior(thetas, prior, k, n)` returns normalised posterior weights as `Fraction`s when inputs are rational; `beta_update(a, b, k, n)`; `predictive_next(a, b)`. Hidden tests: lesson 70's grid gives $\left(\tfrac{2}{73}, \tfrac{12}{73}, \tfrac{27}{73}, \tfrac{32}{73}\right)$ exactly; a zero prior weight stays zero; `beta_update(10, 10, 8, 10) == (18, 12)`; `predictive_next(6, 1) == Fraction(6, 7)`. Known-wrong: no renormalisation (fails test 1). Generators: `gen.stats.conjugate`, `gen.stats.grid`, `gen.stats.predictive`, `gen.stats.credible`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Show that a Beta($\alpha, \beta$) prior and $k$ successes in $n$ Bernoulli trials give a Beta($\alpha + k, \beta + n - k$) posterior. (b) Write the posterior mean as a weighted average of the prior mean and $\hat p = k/n$, and interpret the weights. (c) Apply to the trader of lesson 71.
  *Model answer.* (1) Prior density $\propto p^{\alpha - 1}(1 - p)^{\beta - 1}$; likelihood $\propto p^k(1 - p)^{n - k}$. (2) Posterior $\propto$ prior $\times$ likelihood $= p^{\alpha + k - 1}(1 - p)^{\beta + n - k - 1}$, the kernel of Beta($\alpha + k, \beta + n - k$); the constant is fixed by integrating to 1, so it need not be computed. (3) Posterior mean $\frac{\alpha + k}{\alpha + \beta + n}$. (4) Rewrite: $\frac{\alpha + \beta}{\alpha + \beta + n}\cdot\frac{\alpha}{\alpha + \beta} + \frac{n}{\alpha + \beta + n}\cdot\frac{k}{n}$. (5) The weights are the prior's pseudo-count $\alpha + \beta$ and the data's count $n$: the prior acts like $\alpha + \beta$ earlier trials. (6) As $n$ grows the data's weight tends to 1, so the posterior mean tends to $\hat p$. (7) Trader: prior Beta(10, 10), 8 wins in 10: posterior Beta(18, 12), mean $\frac{20}{30}\times 0.5 + \frac{10}{30}\times 0.8 = 0.6$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: prior and likelihood kernels (line 1). 2: the posterior kernel and why the constant is not needed (line 2). 1: the mean (line 3). 2: the weighted average (line 4). 2: interpretation of the weights and the limit (lines 5, 6). 1: the application (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* The trader of lesson 71 (posterior Beta(18, 12)) asks for a bigger risk limit. (a) What is the posterior probability that his win rate exceeds 50%? (b) What is the predictive distribution of his wins in the next 10 trades, and the chance of at least 6? (c) Compare with a frequentist test of 8 of 10 against 50%. What do you tell the desk head?
  *Answer.* (a) $P(p > 0.5) = 0.868$ under Beta(18, 12). (b) The next 10 trades are Beta-binomial(10, 18, 12): mean 6.0, and $P(\ge 6) = 0.620$. (c) The exact one-sided binomial p-value for 8 of 10 is 0.055: "not significant". Decision point: the frequentist answer ignores the desk's experience that most new traders are near 50%; the Bayesian answer folds it in and says "probably above 50%, but most of the evidence is the prior; expect about 6 wins in 10, and 38% of the time 5 or fewer". A modest increase with a review after 50 more trades fits that.

**BY2 Priors, shrinkage and computation** (`set.bayesian.shrinkage`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: normal-normal updating by completing the square. Code drill `drl.stats.metropolis` (d4, `micropython-ok`): `metropolis(log_target, x0, step, n, seed)` with `zqrandom`, returning every draw including repeats. Hidden tests: on a normal-normal posterior with known mean 7.143 and SD 3.833 (lesson 76), 20,000 draws after 1,000 burn-in at step 4, seed 7, give a mean within 0.4 and an SD within 0.4 of the truth; the acceptance rate lies in $[0.2, 0.8]$; a rejected proposal repeats the current value. Known-wrong: appending only accepted draws (fails test 3 and biases the SD). Generators: `gen.stats.normal-normal`, `gen.stats.shrink`, `gen.stats.bayes-factor`, `gen.stats.ridge-prior`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $X_1, \dots, X_n$ are independent $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ with $\sigma$ known, and the prior is $\mu\sim N(\mu_0, \tau^2)$. Derive the posterior by completing the square, and interpret its mean and variance.
  *Model answer.* (1) Posterior $\propto\exp\left(-\frac{(\mu - \mu_0)^2}{2\tau^2}\right)\prod_i\exp\left(-\frac{(x_i - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)$. (2) Only $\bar x$ matters: $\sum_i(x_i - \mu)^2 = \sum_i(x_i - \bar x)^2 + n(\bar x - \mu)^2$, and the first term does not involve $\mu$. (3) Collect the exponent's terms in $\mu$: $-\frac12\left[\mu^2\left(\frac{1}{\tau^2} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}\right) - 2\mu\left(\frac{\mu_0}{\tau^2} + \frac{n\bar x}{\sigma^2}\right)\right] + \text{const}$. (4) The leap: a quadratic in $\mu$ in an exponent is a normal kernel, so match it to $-\frac{(\mu - m)^2}{2v}$ by completing the square. (5) Matching the $\mu^2$ coefficient: $\frac1v = \frac{1}{\tau^2} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}$ (precisions add). (6) Matching the $\mu$ coefficient: $m = v\left(\frac{\mu_0}{\tau^2} + \frac{n\bar x}{\sigma^2}\right)$, a precision-weighted average of $\mu_0$ and $\bar x$. (7) Lesson 76's numbers: precisions $\frac{1}{16} = 0.0625$ and $\frac{5}{900} = 0.0056$; $m = \frac{6\times 0.0625 + 20\times 0.0056}{0.0681} = 7.14\%$, $\sqrt v = 3.83\%$. (8) Interpretation: five years of 30%-volatility returns carry less information about the mean than a prior with SD 4%, which is why expected-return estimates on a desk start from priors (or models) and not from sample means.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the product (line 1). 1: the reduction to $\bar x$ (line 2). 2: collecting terms (line 3). 1: why completing the square (line 4). 2: the precision (line 5). 2: the mean as a weighted average (line 6). 1: numbers and interpretation (lines 7, 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten managers' three-year alphas (in %) have a sample variance of 20 across managers; each alpha's sampling variance is $s^2 = 16$. The pooled mean is 1%. (a) Estimate the spread $\tau^2$ of true alphas. (b) Shrink the best manager's 12% alpha. (c) What if the sample variance across managers had been 12?
  *Answer.* (a) Observed alphas vary from both true differences and noise: $20\approx\tau^2 + 16$, so $\hat\tau^2 = 4$. (b) The weight on the raw estimate is $\frac{\tau^2}{\tau^2 + s^2} = \frac{4}{20} = 0.2$, so the estimate is $1 + 0.2\times(12 - 1) = 3.2\%$. (c) $\hat\tau^2 = 12 - 16 < 0$: the managers spread less than noise alone would make them, so the estimate is truncated at 0 and every manager shrinks fully to the pooled 1%. Decision point: the raw 12% is the naive answer; the shrunk answer is what a capital allocator should plan on, and (c) is the honest reading of a universe with no detectable skill.

### 3.5 Course SM (2.2)

**SM1 Prices, returns and stationarity** (`set.arma.stationarity`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the random walk's variance and the $\sqrt h$ rule. Code drill `drl.ts.returns` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `log_returns(prices)`, `simple_returns(prices)`, `aggregate_log(rets)`, `annualised_vol(rets, periods=252)` with divisor $n - 1$. Hidden tests: prices 100, 110, 99 give simple returns $(0.10, -0.10)$ and log returns summing to $\ln 0.99$; annualised volatility of a fixed 20-value list matches the reference to $10^{-12}$; a non-positive price raises. Known-wrong: annualising by 252 (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ts.classify`, `gen.ts.transform`, `gen.ts.log-return`, `gen.ts.sqrt-h`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Daily log returns $r_t$ are independent with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$, and $p_t = p_0 + \sum_{s=1}^t r_s$ is the log price. (a) Derive the mean and variance of $p_t - p_0$. (b) Deduce the $\sqrt h$ rule for $h$-day volatility and annualisation. (c) Explain why the random walk is not stationary although its increments are. (d) Show how lag-1 autocorrelation $\rho$ in the returns changes the 5-day variance.
  *Model answer.* (1) $E[p_t - p_0] = \sum_{s=1}^t E[r_s] = t\mu$ by linearity. (2) $\text{Var}(p_t - p_0) = \sum_{s=1}^t\text{Var}(r_s) = t\sigma^2$, because the covariances vanish under independence. (3) So the $h$-day standard deviation is $\sigma\sqrt h$, and with 252 trading days annual volatility is $\sigma\sqrt{252}$. (4) The variance of $p_t$ grows with $t$, so no single distribution describes $p_t$ for all $t$: weak stationarity's constant variance fails, although $r_t = p_t - p_{t-1}$ has the same distribution every day. (5) With lag-1 autocorrelation $\rho$ (and none beyond), $\text{Var}\left(\sum_{s=1}^5 r_s\right) = 5\sigma^2 + 2\times 4\rho\sigma^2$, since 4 adjacent pairs each contribute $2\text{Cov}$. (6) The variance ratio is $\text{VR}(5) = 1 + \frac{8\rho}{5} = 1 + 2\left(1 - \tfrac15\right)\rho$. (7) Positive autocorrelation (momentum) makes long-horizon risk larger than $\sqrt h$ says; negative autocorrelation (mean reversion, bid-ask bounce) makes it smaller.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the mean (line 1). 2: the variance with independence named (line 2). 1: $\sqrt h$ and annualisation (line 3). 2: why the walk is not stationary (line 4). 2: the autocorrelated case (lines 5, 6). 2: the interpretation of the sign (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A strategy's daily returns have volatility 1% and lag-1 autocorrelation 0.1 (none beyond). (a) What are the 5-day and the annual volatilities? (b) By how much does $\sigma\sqrt{252}$ understate annual risk? (c) Which strategies typically show this, and what should a risk report do?
  *Answer.* (a) $\text{VR}(5) = 1 + 2(0.8)(0.1) = 1.16$, so 5-day volatility is $\sqrt{5\times 1.16} = 2.41\%$ against $\sqrt 5 = 2.24\%$. Annually $\text{VR}(252) = 1 + 2\left(1 - \tfrac{1}{252}\right)(0.1) = 1.199$, so volatility is $15.87\%\times\sqrt{1.199} = 17.4\%$. (b) By a factor of 1.095: the true annual volatility is 9.5% higher than $\sigma\sqrt{252}$ (which is 8.7% too low). (c) Decision point: trend-following and illiquid-asset strategies show positive autocorrelation; reporting $\sigma\sqrt{252}$ flatters their Sharpe ratios. The report should estimate risk at the horizon that matters (weekly or monthly returns) or apply the variance ratio, and say which it did.

**SM2 Autocorrelation** (`set.arma.autocorrelation`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the variance of a mean of autocorrelated data. Code drill `drl.ts.acf` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `acf(x, nlags)` with the full-sample mean and denominator, `ljung_box(x, m)` returning $Q$, `pacf2(r1, r2)`. Hidden tests: `acf([1, 3, 2, 4, 5], 1)[1] == 0.1`; a 500-value fixture matches statsmodels' `acf` (`adjusted=False`) to $10^{-12}$; lesson 8's $Q = 5.425$ from the given $r_k$; `pacf2(0.8, 0.64)` within $10^{-12}$ of 0 (in floating point $0.8^2 = 0.6400000000000001$, so an exact `== 0` fails a correct solution [verified]). Known-wrong: `np.corrcoef` of shifted pieces (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ts.acf-value`, `gen.ts.bands`, `gen.ts.ljung-box`, `gen.ts.eff-n`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $x_t$ is a stationary AR(1) with coefficient $\varphi$ and variance $\gamma_0$. (a) Derive $\text{Var}(\bar x)$ for $T$ observations and its large-$T$ form. (b) Define the effective sample size and compute it for $\varphi = 0.9$ and $T = 1000$. (c) Explain why this matters for a Sharpe ratio or a mean spread.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\text{Var}(\bar x) = \frac{1}{T^2}\sum_{s,t}\text{Cov}(x_s, x_t) = \frac{\gamma_0}{T^2}\sum_{s,t}\varphi^{\lvert s - t\rvert}$. (2) Group by lag: there are $T$ pairs at lag 0 and $2(T - k)$ at lag $k$, so $\text{Var}(\bar x) = \frac{\gamma_0}{T}\left[1 + 2\sum_{k=1}^{T-1}\left(1 - \frac kT\right)\varphi^k\right]$. (3) For large $T$ the bracket tends to $1 + \frac{2\varphi}{1 - \varphi} = \frac{1 + \varphi}{1 - \varphi}$ (a geometric series, 1.3 lesson 87). (4) The effective sample size makes the iid formula true: $\text{Var}(\bar x) = \gamma_0/T_{\text{eff}}$ with $T_{\text{eff}} = T\frac{1 - \varphi}{1 + \varphi}$. (5) For $\varphi = 0.9$ and $T = 1000$: $T_{\text{eff}} = 1000\times\frac{0.1}{1.9} = 52.6$. (6) The leap: the standard error of lesson 2 counted independent pieces of information; persistence means consecutive observations repeat each other, so the count is smaller. (7) A mean spread or a Sharpe ratio estimated from persistent data has a standard error $\sqrt{(1 + \varphi)/(1 - \varphi)}$ times the iid one, here 4.4 times; Newey-West errors (1.6 lesson 56) estimate this factor without assuming AR(1).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the double sum (line 1). 2: grouping by lag (line 2). 2: the limit with the geometric series (line 3). 1: the definition (line 4). 1: the number (line 5). 1: the explanation (line 6). 1: the application (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A hedge fund reports a monthly Sharpe ratio of 0.25 and annualises it to $0.25\sqrt{12} = 0.87$. Its monthly returns have autocorrelations $0.3^k$ at lag $k$, typical of smoothed, illiquid holdings. (a) Use Lo's (2002) correction $SR_{12} = SR_1\cdot\frac{12}{\sqrt{12 + 2\sum_{k=1}^{11}(12 - k)\rho_k}}$ to annualise correctly. (b) Why does the naive rule overstate it? (c) What would you ask the manager?
  *Answer.* (a) The denominator is $\sqrt{12 + 2\sum_{k=1}^{11}(12 - k)0.3^k} = 4.589$, so the factor is $12/4.589 = 2.615$ instead of $\sqrt{12} = 3.464$, and the annual Sharpe ratio is $0.25\times 2.615 = 0.65$. (b) Positive autocorrelation makes annual variance larger than 12 times the monthly variance (homework SM1), while the mean scales by exactly 12. (c) Decision point: the naive 0.87 is what the marketing shows; ask how the holdings are priced, whether returns are smoothed by stale marks, and for the Sharpe ratio computed from annual or quarterly returns directly.

**SM3 ARMA models** (`set.arma.models`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the moments of a stationary AR(1). Code drill `drl.ts.ar1-fit` (d4, `web`): `simulate_ar1(phi, c, sigma, shocks)` from supplied shocks; `fit_ar1_ols(x)` returning $(\hat c, \hat\varphi, \hat\sigma)$; `half_life(phi)`. Hidden tests: the simulation reproduces the reference path exactly; on a seeded $T = 2000$ path with $\varphi = 0.7$ the OLS estimate agrees with `statsmodels.tsa.arima.model.ARIMA(order=(1, 0, 0))` to within 0.01; `half_life(0.9) == ln 0.5 / ln 0.9` to $10^{-12}$; `half_life(1.0)` raises. Known-wrong: half-life with $\log_{10}$ in one place (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.ts.half-life`, `gen.ts.region`, `gen.ts.theory-acf`, `gen.ts.identify`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $x_t = c + \varphi x_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t$ with white noise $\varepsilon_t$ of variance $\sigma^2$ and $\lvert\varphi\rvert < 1$, derive the mean, the variance and the autocorrelation function, and the half-life of a deviation. Say what happens at $\varphi = 1$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Stationarity means $E[x_t] = E[x_{t-1}] = \mu$, so $\mu = c + \varphi\mu$ and $\mu = \frac{c}{1 - \varphi}$. (2) Write $y_t = x_t - \mu$, so $y_t = \varphi y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t$. (3) $\varepsilon_t$ is uncorrelated with $y_{t-1}$ (it arrives later), so $\text{Var}(y_t) = \varphi^2\text{Var}(y_{t-1}) + \sigma^2$; stationarity gives $\gamma_0 = \frac{\sigma^2}{1 - \varphi^2}$. (4) Multiply $y_t = \varphi y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t$ by $y_{t-k}$ and take expectations: $\gamma_k = \varphi\gamma_{k-1}$ for $k\ge 1$, so $\rho_k = \varphi^k$. (5) The expected deviation after $h$ steps is $\varphi^h y_t$; it halves when $\varphi^h = \tfrac12$, at $h = \frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln\varphi}$. (6) At $\varphi = 1$ the variance formula divides by zero: no stationary solution exists, the deviation never shrinks, and the series is a random walk. (7) The assumption $\lvert\varphi\rvert < 1$ was used in lines 1 and 3; for $\lvert\varphi\rvert > 1$ deviations explode.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the mean (line 1). 1: demeaning (line 2). 2: the variance with the uncorrelatedness named (line 3). 2: the ACF recursion (line 4). 1: the half-life (line 5). 2: the unit-root and explosive cases (lines 6, 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Trade-to-trade price changes of a stock have variance 0.0005 and lag-1 autocovariance $-0.0001$ (in dollars squared), and nothing beyond lag 1. Roll's model says the observed price is the efficient price plus $\pm c$ (half the spread), with trades equally likely at the bid or the ask. (a) Estimate the spread. (b) Write the price changes as an MA(1) and find $\theta$. (c) What if the lag-1 autocovariance were positive?
  *Answer.* (a) In Roll's model the lag-1 autocovariance of price changes is $-c^2$, so $c = \sqrt{0.0001} = 0.01$ and the spread is $2c = \$0.02$. The efficient-price variance is $0.0005 - 2c^2 = 0.0003$. (b) $\rho_1 = -0.0001/0.0005 = -0.2$; solving $\frac{\theta}{1 + \theta^2} = -0.2$ gives $\theta = -0.209$ (the invertible root; $1/\theta = -4.79$ is the other). (c) Decision point: a positive autocovariance gives $c = \sqrt{\text{negative}}$; the model does not apply (other effects, such as order splitting, dominate), and the estimator should report "undefined", not a number.

**SM4 Forecasting and evaluation** (`set.arma.forecasting`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: AR(1) forecasts and their error variance. Code drill `drl.ts.rolling-origin` (d4, `micropython-ok`): `rolling_origin(x, first_origin, h, method)` for `naive`, `mean` and `ar1` (refitted by OLS at each origin on data up to the origin only), returning errors; `mase(errors, train)`. Hidden tests: the errors on a 300-value fixture match the reference; a planted series whose last 50 values are a step change shows the `ar1` fit never uses values beyond the origin (a leak detector compares results when future values are corrupted); MASE of the naive method on its own training data is 1. Known-wrong: fitting once on the whole series (fails test 2). Generators: `gen.ts.forecast`, `gen.ts.interval`, `gen.ts.rank`, `gen.ts.ses-weights`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a stationary AR(1) with mean $\mu$, coefficient $\varphi$ and shock variance $\sigma^2$, derive the $h$-step forecast and the variance of its error, find their limits as $h\to\infty$, and contrast with a random walk.
  *Model answer.* (1) Iterate: $x_{T+h} - \mu = \varphi^h(x_T - \mu) + \sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\varphi^j\varepsilon_{T+h-j}$. (2) The future shocks have mean zero given the past, so the forecast is $\hat x_{T+h} = \mu + \varphi^h(x_T - \mu)$. (3) The error is the sum of future shocks: $\text{Var} = \sigma^2\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\varphi^{2j} = \sigma^2\frac{1 - \varphi^{2h}}{1 - \varphi^2}$ (a geometric series). (4) As $h\to\infty$ the forecast tends to $\mu$ and the variance to $\frac{\sigma^2}{1 - \varphi^2}$, the unconditional variance: the interval stops widening. (5) For a random walk ($\varphi = 1$) every weight is 1: the forecast stays at $x_T$ and the variance is $h\sigma^2$, growing without bound. (6) Lesson 18's numbers: $\varphi = 0.8$, $x_T = 5$, $\sigma = 1$: at $h = 5$ the forecast is 1.64 with variance 2.48, against a limit of 2.78. (7) Both intervals ignore the uncertainty in $\hat\varphi$ and $\hat\mu$, so in practice they are too narrow, more so near $\varphi = 1$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the iterated form (line 1). 1: the forecast (line 2). 2: the error variance with the geometric sum (line 3). 2: the limits (line 4). 1: the random-walk contrast (line 5). 1: a number (line 6). 1: parameter uncertainty (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* (a) Show that simple exponential smoothing with parameter $\alpha$ gives the optimal forecasts of the ARIMA(0, 1, 1) model $y_t = y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t + \theta\varepsilon_{t-1}$ with $\theta = \alpha - 1$. (b) Derive the $h$-step forecast variance $\sigma^2(1 + (h - 1)\alpha^2)$. (c) For $\alpha = 0.3$, $\sigma = 1$, $h = 10$, compare it with a random walk's.
  *Answer.* (a) The one-step error is $e_t = y_t - \ell_{t-1}$, and SES updates $\ell_t = \ell_{t-1} + \alpha e_t$. So $y_t = \ell_{t-1} + e_t$ and $y_{t-1} = \ell_{t-2} + e_{t-1}$; subtracting, $y_t - y_{t-1} = (\ell_{t-1} - \ell_{t-2}) + e_t - e_{t-1} = \alpha e_{t-1} + e_t - e_{t-1} = e_t + (\alpha - 1)e_{t-1}$: an ARIMA(0, 1, 1) with $\theta = \alpha - 1$. (b) Written in shocks, $y_{T+h}$ loads 1 on $\varepsilon_{T+h}$ and $1 + \theta = \alpha$ on each of $\varepsilon_{T+1}, \dots, \varepsilon_{T+h-1}$, so the variance is $\sigma^2(1 + (h - 1)\alpha^2)$. (c) $1 + 9(0.09) = 1.81$ (SD 1.35) against 10 for a random walk (SD 3.16). Decision point: SES is not an ad hoc smoother; it is the right forecast exactly when the series is a random walk observed with noise, and its intervals follow from that model, not from a rule of thumb.

### 3.6 Course VL (2.2)

**VL1 Volatility clustering and its models** (`set.volatility.models`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: ARCH(1)'s variance and kurtosis. Code drill `drl.ts.vol-filters` (d3, `micropython-ok`): `ewma_var(r, lam, init)` and `garch_var(r, omega, alpha, beta, init)` returning the one-step-ahead variance series. Hidden tests: hand-computed values on a 5-return list; EWMA with $\lambda = 0.94$ after a 10% shock from variance 1 gives 6.94; `garch_var` with $\beta = 0$ equals ARCH(1); output has length $T + 1$ (the last entry forecasts tomorrow). Known-wrong: using $r_t$ in $\sigma_t^2$ (look-ahead; fails test 1). Generators: `gen.ts.persistence`, `gen.ts.long-run`, `gen.ts.ewma-weight`, `gen.ts.nic`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For ARCH(1), $r_t = \sigma_tz_t$ with $z_t$ independent $N(0, 1)$ and $\sigma_t^2 = \omega + \alpha r_{t-1}^2$, derive (a) the unconditional variance, (b) the kurtosis $\frac{3(1 - \alpha^2)}{1 - 3\alpha^2}$, and (c) explain why normal shocks give fat-tailed returns.
  *Model answer.* (1) $E[r_t^2] = E[\sigma_t^2]E[z_t^2] = E[\sigma_t^2]$, because $z_t$ is independent of $\sigma_t$. (2) Taking expectations of the variance equation with stationarity, $v = \omega + \alpha v$, so $v = \frac{\omega}{1 - \alpha}$ (needs $\alpha < 1$). (3) Fourth moments: $E[r_t^4] = E[\sigma_t^4]E[z_t^4] = 3E[\sigma_t^4]$. (4) $E[\sigma_t^4] = \omega^2 + 2\omega\alpha v + \alpha^2E[r_{t-1}^4]$. (5) Let $m = E[r^4]$; stationarity gives $m = 3\left(\omega^2 + \frac{2\omega^2\alpha}{1 - \alpha} + \alpha^2m\right)$, so $m(1 - 3\alpha^2) = 3\omega^2\frac{1 + \alpha}{1 - \alpha}$. (6) Kurtosis $\frac{m}{v^2} = \frac{3\omega^2(1 + \alpha)}{(1 - \alpha)(1 - 3\alpha^2)}\cdot\frac{(1 - \alpha)^2}{\omega^2} = \frac{3(1 - \alpha^2)}{1 - 3\alpha^2}$, finite only when $3\alpha^2 < 1$. (7) Why fat tails: returns are a mixture of normals with a random variance; mixing variances always raises kurtosis above 3 (Jensen: $E[\sigma^4]\ge(E[\sigma^2])^2$). At $\alpha = 0.5$ the kurtosis is 9.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: line 1. 2: the unconditional variance (line 2). 1: the fourth moment of $r$ (line 3). 2: the fourth moment of $\sigma$ and the stationarity equation (lines 4, 5). 2: the kurtosis and its existence condition (line 6). 2: the mixture explanation (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A GARCH(1, 1) with persistence 0.98 and long-run daily volatility 1% has just been hit by a shock: tomorrow's forecast variance is 4 (daily volatility 2%). (a) How many days until forecast volatility is 1.5%? (b) Compare with the half-life of the variance gap. (c) Which number belongs in a risk report that says "volatility will be back to halfway in N days"?
  *Answer.* (a) $\sigma_{T+h}^2 = 1 + 0.98^{h-1}\times 3$; volatility 1.5% means variance 2.25, so $0.98^{h-1} = \tfrac{1.25}{3}$ and $h - 1 = \frac{\ln(1.25/3)}{\ln 0.98} = 43.3$ days. (b) The variance gap halves in $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln 0.98} = 34.3$ days, when variance is 2.5 and volatility 1.58%. (c) Decision point: "halfway" in volatility (2% to 1.5%) takes about 43 days and halfway in variance about 34; the report must say which, because the square root bends the path.

**VL2 Fitting, forecasting and risk** (`set.volatility.risk`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: fitting GARCH by maximum likelihood. Code drill `drl.ts.garch-fit` (d4, `web`): `neg_loglik(params, r)` and `fit_garch(r)` with `scipy.optimize.minimize` on transformed parameters that keep $\omega > 0$, $\alpha, \beta\ge 0$, $\alpha + \beta < 1$. Hidden tests: `neg_loglik` matches the reference at three parameter points to $10^{-8}$; on a seeded $T = 3000$ series simulated with $(0.02, 0.08, 0.90)$ the estimates are within the tolerances that CI records from 200 simulated fits (about two standard errors, [verify in CI]); the fit's log-likelihood is at least the reference optimiser's minus $10^{-3}$; persistence below 1. Known-wrong: maximising $+$`neg_loglik` (fails test 3). Generators: `gen.ts.garch-forecast`, `gen.ts.exceed`, `gen.ts.kupiec`, `gen.ts.term`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain how GARCH(1, 1) is fitted by maximum likelihood: (a) write the log-likelihood and say why it factorises over days although returns are dependent; (b) state the constraints and how to impose them in an unconstrained optimiser; (c) say what happens when the shocks are not normal; (d) say how to check the fit.
  *Model answer.* (1) Given the past, $r_t\sim N(0, \sigma_t^2)$ with $\sigma_t^2$ known from earlier returns and parameters. (2) The joint density is the product of these conditionals (the chain rule of probability, 1.5), so $\ell = -\tfrac12\sum_t\left(\ln 2\pi + \ln\sigma_t^2 + \frac{r_t^2}{\sigma_t^2}\right)$: dependence enters through $\sigma_t^2$, not through the factorisation. (3) The recursion needs a start, $\sigma_1^2$: the sample variance or the long-run variance, with a burn-in. (4) Constraints: $\omega > 0$, $\alpha\ge 0$, $\beta\ge 0$ keep variances positive; $\alpha + \beta < 1$ gives a finite long-run variance. (5) Impose them by optimising transformed parameters, for example $\omega = e^a$ and $(\alpha, \beta)$ from a softmax-style map onto the triangle. (6) If shocks are not normal, the Gaussian likelihood still estimates the parameters consistently (quasi-maximum likelihood), but standard errors need the sandwich form (homework RG3's idea again), or a Student-$t$ likelihood can be used. (7) Checks: standardised residuals $r_t/\hat\sigma_t$ should have mean 0 and variance 1, no autocorrelation in their squares (Ljung-Box), and a QQ plot near the assumed shock distribution.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: conditional normality (line 1). 2: the factorisation and the formula (line 2). 1: the start value (line 3). 2: constraints and their transformation (lines 4, 5). 2: QMLE and its standard errors (line 6). 2: diagnostics (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A bank's 99% one-day VaR model had 7 exceedances in the last 250 days. (a) Test with the binomial. (b) Compute Kupiec's likelihood-ratio statistic and p-value. (c) Where does 7 fall in the Basel traffic-light zones? (d) What is the next check?
  *Answer.* (a) Under a correct model the count is Bin(250, 0.01) with mean 2.5; $P(X\ge 7) = 0.014$. (b) $LR = -2\ln\frac{0.99^{243}0.01^7}{(243/250)^{243}(7/250)^7} = 5.50$ on 1 degree of freedom: $p = 0.019$. (c) The yellow zone (5 to 9 exceedances; green is 0 to 4, red 10 or more) (the Basel Committee's 1996 backtesting framework, confirmed by search). (d) Decision point: the count test cannot see clustering, which is how a constant-volatility model usually fails; check whether the 7 exceedances bunch together (Christoffersen's independence test), and if they do, move to a GARCH-based VaR.

### 3.7 Course CO (2.2)

**CO1 Unit roots and spurious regression** (`set.cointegration.unit-roots`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: why unit-root and spurious-regression statistics break the usual tables. Code drill `drl.ts.df-null` (d4, `micropython-ok`): `df_stat(y, case)` for cases `n`, `c`, `ct`; `df_null_quantile(T, reps, seed, case, q)` by simulation with `zqrandom`. Hidden tests: `df_stat` matches statsmodels' `adfuller(..., maxlag=0, autolag=None)` on 3 fixtures to $10^{-10}$; the 5% quantile for case `c`, $T = 100$, 4,000 replications, seed 11 lies within 0.12 of $-2.89$; case `n` within 0.12 of $-1.94$. Known-wrong: no constant in case `c` (fails test 2 by landing near $-1.94$). Generators: `gen.ts.df-decide`, `gen.ts.adf-kpss-combine`, `gen.ts.spurious`, `gen.ts.integration-order`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Explain why the $t$-ratio on $y_{t-1}$ in the Dickey-Fuller regression does not have a $t$ distribution under a unit root. (b) Explain why regressing one independent random walk on another gives "significant" slopes most of the time. (c) Say what both imply for a researcher working with prices.
  *Model answer.* (1) The usual $t$ theory needs the regressor to be stationary so that $\frac1T\sum y_{t-1}^2$ settles to a constant. (2) Under a unit root $y_{t-1}$ is a random walk: $\sum y_{t-1}^2$ grows like $T^2$, not $T$, and its scaled value stays random (a functional of Brownian motion). (3) The OLS estimate of $\varphi$ is biased downwards in finite samples and its $t$-ratio has a left-shifted, skewed distribution; its 5% point with a constant is $-2.86$, not $-1.645$, which is why the tables come from simulation (lesson 33: 46% of unit-root $t$-ratios fall below $-1.645$). (4) Spurious regression: with $y$ and $x$ independent random walks the true slope is 0 but the errors $y_t - \beta x_t$ are themselves a random walk, so the classical assumption of stationary errors fails. (5) The residuals are extremely autocorrelated, the classical standard error is far too small, and the $t$-statistic grows with $T$ instead of settling: $\lvert t\rvert > 1.96$ about 76% of the time at $T = 100$ (lesson 35). (6) The symptom: $R^2$ high and Durbin-Watson near 0. (7) For prices: test for unit roots with the right tables; regress returns on returns, or test for cointegration with the right tables; never read $t$ or $R^2$ from a regression of levels on levels.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the stationarity the usual theory needs (line 1). 2: why it fails under a unit root (line 2). 2: the left-shifted distribution and simulated tables (line 3). 2: spurious regression's mechanism (lines 4, 5). 1: the symptom (line 6). 2: the consequences (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* An ADF test on a log price series, with lags chosen by AIC, gives $-3.1$ with a constant only and $-3.1$ with a constant and trend. The 5% critical values are $-2.86$ and $-3.41$. (a) What does each specification conclude? (b) How should the specification be chosen? (c) The series has risen steadily for ten years. Which conclusion do you report?
  *Answer.* (a) Constant only: $-3.1 < -2.86$, reject the unit root. With trend: $-3.1 > -3.41$, do not reject. (b) The specification should match the alternative: if the plausible stationary alternative includes a deterministic trend (the series trends up for a decade), the constant-only test is misspecified, because a trending stationary series would look like a unit root to it, and vice versa. Choose it from the plot and economics before running the test, not by which result is preferred. (c) Decision point: report the trend specification (no rejection at 5%), check with KPSS (lesson 34), and note that unit-root tests have low power: the honest conclusion is "cannot distinguish a unit root from slow trend reversion with this sample".

**CO2 Cointegration and pairs** (`set.cointegration.pairs`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Engle-Granger explained. Code drill `drl.ts.engle-granger` (d4, `web`): `engle_granger(y, x)` regresses $y$ on a constant and $x$, runs an ADF on the residuals with no constant and lags chosen by AIC, and returns $\hat\beta$, the statistic and the decision at 5% using the MacKinnon critical value for two series. Hidden tests: the statistic matches `statsmodels.tsa.stattools.coint(y, x)` (statsmodels 0.15.0 runs the residual ADF with `regression="n"` [verified]) to $10^{-6}$ on 3 fixture pairs; decisions are right on a cointegrated pair, a pair of independent walks and a correlated-returns pair; the 5% critical value used is $-3.35$ to two decimals at $T = 500$ (`mackinnoncrit` gives $-3.348$; asymptotically $-3.336$ [verified]). Known-wrong: Dickey-Fuller's $-2.86$ as the critical value (misclassifies the fixture pair with statistic $-3.0$). Generators: `gen.ts.classify-pair`, `gen.ts.half-life`, `gen.ts.hedge`, `gen.ts.ecm`.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain the Engle-Granger procedure: (a) what each step does; (b) why the residual test needs its own critical values; (c) why the first step's $\hat\beta$ is good even though the regression is on non-stationary levels; (d) one weakness of the procedure.
  *Model answer.* (1) Step 1 regresses $y_t$ on a constant and $x_t$ in levels and keeps the residuals $\hat u_t = y_t - \hat a - \hat\beta x_t$, the estimated spread. (2) Step 2 tests $\hat u_t$ for a unit root (ADF without a constant, because the residuals already have mean zero). (3) Cointegration means the true spread is stationary: rejecting the unit root in step 2 is evidence for cointegration. (4) Step 1 chose $\hat\beta$ to make the residual variance as small as possible, so the residuals look more stationary than the true spread would even when no cointegration exists. (5) The test statistic is therefore shifted left, and its critical values (MacKinnon: about $-3.34$ at 5% for two series with a constant) are more negative than Dickey-Fuller's $-2.86$. (6) If the pair is cointegrated, any wrong $\beta$ leaves a random-walk component whose sum of squares grows like $T^2$, so OLS is pulled to the true $\beta$ very fast: $\hat\beta - \beta$ shrinks like $\frac1T$, faster than the usual $\frac{1}{\sqrt T}$ (superconsistency). (7) Weaknesses: the result can depend on which series is on the left in finite samples, it finds at most one cointegrating relation, and the step-1 standard errors are not valid for inference on $\beta$; Johansen's method addresses all three.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the two steps (lines 1, 2). 1: what rejection means (line 3). 3: why the critical values differ (lines 4, 5). 2: superconsistency with its reason (line 6). 2: a weakness (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Two ETFs, 500 daily closes. Step 1: $\hat\beta = 1.52$. Step 2: residual ADF statistic $-3.6$. The spread's AR(1) coefficient is 0.96. (a) Decide at 5% and at 1%. (b) Compute the half-life. (c) The strategy will trade this spread over the next year with $\beta$ estimated on these 500 days. What is the look-ahead risk, and how do you test the strategy honestly?
  *Answer.* (a) MacKinnon's critical values for two series with a constant at $T = 500$ are $-3.35$ (5%) and $-3.92$ (1%): reject at 5%, not at 1%. (b) $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln 0.96} = 17.0$ days. (c) Decision point: a backtest on the same 500 days with this $\beta$ uses information the trader did not have (lesson 42); estimate $\beta$, the spread's mean and SD and the bands on a formation window, trade only on later data, roll the window forward, and charge costs on both legs. If the out-of-sample spread drifts, re-test cointegration before assuming it still holds.

### 3.8 Drills, Labs and projects

**Exit capstones** (`1.6.exit` and `2.2.exit`, `web`; rules in sections 4.3 and 4.6).

**Statistics Lab** (14 drills of 30 minutes, `web` unless marked, ungated, offered at the level shown):

| Drill | Level | Task | Hidden checks |
|---|---|---|---|
| `drl.stats.lab-coverage` | IT1 | Measure the true coverage of $z$, $t$ and bootstrap intervals for the mean of exponential data at $n = 5, 10, 30, 100$ with 20,000 replications | Coverage of $t$ at $n = 5$ below 0.93 and above 0.85; all methods within 0.01 of 0.95 at $n = 100$ [verify bounds in CI] |
| `drl.stats.lab-wald-wilson` | IT1 | Exact coverage curves of Wald and Wilson for $n = 20$ by summing binomial probabilities over $p$ | Matches the reference curve to $10^{-12}$ |
| `drl.stats.lab-mse-race` | ES2 | MSE of mean, median and 10% trimmed mean under normal, $t_3$ and 1% contaminated data | Ranking matches the reference in each scenario |
| `drl.stats.lab-mle-t` | ES3 | Fit normal and Student $t$ to a seeded daily-return series by MLE; compare log-likelihoods and AIC | $t$ preferred by AIC; $\hat\nu$ in $[3, 8]$ for the fixture |
| `drl.stats.lab-delta-sharpe` | ES4 | Compare the delta-method SE of a Sharpe ratio with the bootstrap SE on iid and on autocorrelated returns | Agreement within 10% on iid data; the note says which is wrong on autocorrelated data |
| `drl.stats.lab-pvalue-hist` | IT2 | Histogram of p-values from 10,000 null and 10,000 alternative tests; estimate the share of nulls from the flat floor | Estimate within 0.03 of the truth on the fixture |
| `drl.stats.lab-power` | IT2 | Power curves by simulation against the closed form for one- and two-sided $z$ tests | Maximum gap below 0.01 at 20,000 replications |
| `drl.stats.lab-permutation-vs-t` | IT3 | Permutation and Welch tests on 200 seeded two-group data sets | p-values correlate above 0.95 on normal data |
| `drl.stats.lab-bh-screen` | IT4 | Screen 500 synthetic signals (50 real) with no correction, Bonferroni, Holm and BH | Realised false discovery proportion of BH within the reference's interval |
| `drl.stats.lab-strategy-zoo` | IT4 | Best of 1,000 noise strategies in sample against out of sample; compute the deflated Sharpe ratio | Out-of-sample mean Sharpe within 0.1 of 0 |
| `drl.stats.lab-hc-coverage` | RG3 | Coverage of classical, HC0 and HC3 slope intervals under fan-shaped errors, $n = 30$ and 300 | HC3 closest to 0.95 at $n = 30$ |
| `drl.stats.lab-newey-west` | RG3 | Overlapping 12-month returns: classical against Newey-West $t$ for a predictor with no true effect | Classical rejects far above 5%; Newey-West with 11 lags much closer |
| `drl.stats.lab-logistic-irls` | RG4 | Logistic regression by Newton's method, matched to statsmodels `Logit` | Coefficients to $10^{-8}$ |
| `drl.stats.lab-empirical-bayes` | BY2 | Shrink 50 synthetic managers' alphas; compare total squared error with the raw estimates out of sample | Shrunk error lower on the fixture |

**Time Series Lab** (8 drills of 30 minutes, `web`):

| Drill | Level | Task | Hidden checks |
|---|---|---|---|
| `drl.ts.lab-stylised` | VL1 | Kurtosis, ACF of returns, absolute and squared returns for a seeded GARCH-$t$ series and for its shuffled copy | Shuffling kills the squared-return ACF, not the kurtosis |
| `drl.ts.lab-identify` | SM3 | Identify six seeded ARMA series from ACF and PACF, then confirm by AIC | At least 5 of 6 right |
| `drl.ts.lab-forecast-shootout` | SM4 | Naive, mean, drift, AR(1) and SES on 3 seeded series by rolling origin | Ranking matches the reference |
| `drl.ts.lab-leakage` | SM4 | Shuffled K-fold against walk-forward $R^2$ for an AR(1) predictor | Shuffled $R^2$ above walk-forward on the fixture |
| `drl.ts.lab-ewma-vs-garch` | VL2 | One-step variance forecasts scored by QLIKE against the seeded truth | GARCH beats EWMA on the GARCH fixture |
| `drl.ts.lab-var-backtest` | VL2 | Constant-volatility and GARCH 99% VaR on 2,000 days; count and clustering tests | Constant-volatility exceedances cluster |
| `drl.ts.lab-spurious` | CO1 | Rejection rate of the slope test for independent walks at $T = 50, 100, 500, 1000$ | Rate rises with $T$ |
| `drl.ts.lab-dickey-fuller` | CO1 | Simulated Dickey-Fuller quantiles for the three cases | Within 0.1 of MacKinnon's values |

**Project 1.6: Skill or luck?** (`prj.stats.skill-or-luck`, 9 hours, `web`, in the learner's repository with tests). A shipped, seeded panel of daily returns for 50 strategies over 5 years plus market and momentum factors; a known minority have true alpha, and the generator's truth is kept for checking.

1. **Per-strategy inference.** Sharpe ratios with iid delta-method and stationary-bootstrap standard errors; CAPM and two-factor regressions with Newey-West errors.
2. **Many tests.** p-values for alpha; Bonferroni, Holm and BH at 10%; the expected maximum Sharpe ratio of 50 noise strategies; deflated Sharpe ratios.
3. **Shrinkage.** Empirical-Bayes shrinkage of the 50 alphas; compare with the truth.
4. **Results note** (one page): which strategies you would fund, with what confidence, and what failed.

Tests (`pytest`, at least 8): Sharpe and SE functions on hand-computed cases; regression outputs against statsmodels; BH rejections on a fixed p-value list; shrinkage weights between 0 and 1; results reproducible from the seed.

**Project 2.2: Volatility forecast shoot-out** (`prj.ts.vol-forecast`, 4.5 hours, `web`, in the repository). A seeded daily return series of 3,000 days from a GJR-GARCH with Student-$t$ shocks; the learner may add real index data.

1. Fit rolling 60-day, EWMA ($\lambda = 0.94$), GARCH(1, 1) and GJR-GARCH, re-estimated every 250 days on past data only.
2. Score one-day forecasts by QLIKE and MSE against the generator's true variance and against squared returns; explain the difference (lesson 29).
3. Backtest 99% VaR from each; count and clustering tests.
4. Results note: which model you would put in a risk system, and what failed.

Tests (at least 6): filters against hand values; no look-ahead (a corrupted-future test); VaR exceedance counts reproducible; QLIKE implementation on known inputs.

## 4. Topic exam blueprint and the exit tests

Both papers use the `topic` kind of the exam engine (ASSESSMENT.md 3.1): timed, one sitting, no hints, Bertie away app-wide, at least 3 parallel forms, a 3-day cool-down and a remediation set after a fail, and a practice paper of the same shape built from the practice pool, never the exam reserve. Either paper can be sat before the lessons to test out (ASSESSMENT.md 7.3). **Difficulty mix by marks:** 20% at difficulty 2, 50% at 3, 20% at 4, 10% at 5, met exactly on every form (CI allows 5 percentage points). **Pass mark** 70%.

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | Code in the papers | **None.** Both papers run on both targets; code is examined by the capstones `drl.stats.exit-ols` and `drl.ts.exit-coint` | A NumPy or statsmodels code item in each paper | A paper is never split across targets (ASSESSMENT.md 3.3), and statsmodels does not run in the Artifact (decision 5). The exits already demand code that matches statsmodels; a timed 4-mark code item would test the same skill worse |
| E2 | Aids | **The in-app scientific calculator and one tables card**, `card.stats.tables`: a standard normal CDF table and the quantiles 1.282, 1.645, 1.960, 2.326 and 2.576; $t$ quantiles at 0.95, 0.975 and 0.995 for 1 to 30, 40, 60, 120 and $\infty$ degrees of freedom; $\chi^2$ quantiles at 0.95 and 0.99 for 1 to 10 degrees of freedom. `exm.ts` adds the asymptotic Dickey-Fuller values for the three cases (5%: $-1.94$, $-2.86$, $-3.41$; 1%: $-2.57$, $-3.43$, $-3.96$), KPSS's 5% value 0.463, and MacKinnon's asymptotic two-series Engle-Granger values ($-3.34$ at 5%, $-3.90$ at 1%). Any other quantile an item needs (for example $t_{57, 0.975} = 2.002$) is printed in the item | No tables, with answers left as $\Phi^{-1}$ expressions; a full formula sheet | A written statistics paper hands out tables, and turning a statistic into a p-value with them is the skill. A formula sheet would remove what lessons 2, 22, 50 and 66 to 68 teach: knowing which formula applies |
| E3 | Exit slots | **The exit's explanation is one 10-mark long-form slot with `slotMin` 0.7**: Q19 in `exm.stats`, Q15 in `exm.ts`, each drawn from a held-out pool of six | A separate explanation paper | ASSESSMENT.md decision 28. A missed slot bar is retaken alone as a 15-minute exit-item sitting (10 of 80 marks of a 90-minute paper and 10 of 60 of a 75-minute paper, each rounded up to 5 minutes) |
| E4 | Long-form share | **22.5% of `exm.stats` and 26.7% of `exm.ts`** | 30%, the kind's ceiling | Two long-form items per paper is what the clock allows beside 18 and 13 computational items. The homework carries one long-form item per set, so proof skill is examined there too |

Slot ids are written Q1 to Q20 here; in data they are the lowercase local ids `q1` to `q20` that `slotMin` keys refer to (CONTENT-FORMAT.md `LocalId`).

### 4.1 `exm.stats`: 1.6 Statistics

**Duration** 90 minutes (summed item estimates 60 minutes). **Marks** 80. **Items** 20 in five untimed sections with a suggested split. **Pass** 56 of 80; the `1.6.exit` bar is 7 of 10 on Q19 (section 4.3). **Calculator** scientific. **Aids** `card.stats.tables`. **Runs on** both targets.
**Format mix by marks:** numeric 44 (55%), interval 6 (7.5%), set 3 (3.75%), match 3 (3.75%), expression 2 (2.5%), multi-select 2 (2.5%), single choice 2 (2.5%), long-form 18 (22.5%).
**Difficulty mix by marks:** d2 16 (20%), d3 40 (50%), d4 16 (20%), d5 8 (10%).

**Totals.** Marks 80. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 4 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 16$ (Q1, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q15); d3 $4 \times 7 + 3 \times 4 = 40$ (Q2, Q3, Q4, Q8, Q13, Q17, Q18 at 4; Q9, Q10, Q11, Q14 at 3); d4 $3 + 3 + 10 = 16$ (Q12, Q16, Q19); d5 8 (Q20). Generator slots Q1 to Q18 carry 62 marks (77.5%, above ASSESSMENT.md's 50% floor); the two pools carry 18. Q20 is d5 although its homework twins are d4: in the paper it is written closed-book in 12 minutes, with an added part.

**Coverage.** Every form tests ES1 to ES4, IT1 to IT4, RG2, RG3, RG5, BY1 and BY2 through fixed slots (13 levels), and form A adds RG4, form B RG1, form C RG4 through Q14 and Q16: 14 of 15 levels (93%) on every form, above the 80% rule; across the forms every level appears. **Marks by course:** ES 16 (20%), IT 25 (31%), RG 23 (29%), BY 8 (10%), plus Q20's 8 drawn from ES, IT and RG. Against the lesson shares (ES 25%, IT 27.5%, RG 33.75%, BY 13.75%), BY is light because its central idea, shrinkage, is examined again by the project's empirical-Bayes milestone; IT is heavy because it carries most interview questions and the two most expensive traps (the p-value's meaning and the best of many backtests).

**Worked example of one slot (Q12, d4, form A).** "Fifty strategies with no edge are each backtested on 3 years of daily returns. The analyst reports the best one: an annual Sharpe ratio of 1.40, with a single-test $t = 1.40\sqrt 3 = 2.42$. (a) The expected maximum of 50 independent standard normals is 2.249. What best annual Sharpe ratio does pure noise produce on average? (b) What does the reported 1.40 show?"

- *Answer (a), 2 marks.* At a true Sharpe ratio of 0 the standard error of an annual Sharpe ratio estimated from $Y$ years is about $1/\sqrt Y$ (lesson 19), here $1/\sqrt 3 = 0.577$. The expected best of 50 is $2.249 \times 0.577 = 1.30$. Accepted: 1.27 to 1.33.
- *Answer (b), 1 mark, single choice.* Correct: "About what the best of 50 noise strategies shows: no evidence of skill." The chance that the best of 50 noise strategies reaches 1.40 is $1 - \Phi(2.425)^{50} = 0.32$ (shown in the Why).

| Option | Misconception | Feedback after the paper |
|---|---|---|
| "Skill: $t = 2.42$ exceeds 1.96" | `mc.stats.best-backtest-is-skill` | "1.96 is the bar for one test chosen in advance. This winner was chosen as the best of 50." |
| "Skill at 5% after a Bonferroni correction" | slip | "Bonferroni at 5% over 50 tests needs a one-sided p-value below 0.001; $t = 2.42$ gives 0.0077." |
| "No evidence, because a Sharpe ratio of 1.40 is below 1.96" | `mc.stats.sharpe-is-a-t-statistic` [new] | "Right verdict, wrong reason: a Sharpe ratio is not a $t$-statistic. Its $t$ is $1.40\sqrt 3 = 2.42$." |

Rubric for (a): the standard error with its source (1 mark), the product (1 mark); follow-through from a wrong standard error keeps the second mark (ASSESSMENT.md 3.1).

### 4.2 Q19 and the specimen table: `fix.stats.fund-60`

**What Q19 asks.** The learner is shown a statsmodels OLS summary and writes, for every field, what it is, how it is computed from other quantities, and what it says about this regression; then a judgement: which printed numbers not to trust as they stand, and what to do instead. The specimen below uses `fix.stats.fund-60`, the fixture lessons 66 to 69, homework RG2, RG3, RG4 and RG5 and the capstone use, so learners practise on it; the paper draws from six held-out fixtures built the same way (table at the end of this section).

**The fixture.** 60 months of a fund's excess return (`fund`) and two factor returns (`mkt`, `mom`), all monthly and in percent. It is tidy by construction (generator rule 2), so every number the file quotes holds exactly:

1. Factors: 120 Student-$t_5$ draws from NumPy `default_rng(62)`, centred, orthogonalised by QR and rescaled so that `mkt` has mean 0.70 and `mom` mean 0.40, their correlation is exactly 0.3167 (VIF 1.1115), and their spreads put `mom`'s standard error at 0.0839 and the regression sum of squares at 739.82. `mkt` runs from $-8.94$ to 10.52, `mom` from $-7.19$ to 5.93.
2. Errors: 60 Student-$t_4$ draws from the same generator, projected off the column space, then moved along 12 further seeded standard-normal directions inside that space, each candidate rescaled to SSE $= 205.80$, by SciPy `least_squares` from zero on three residual groups (the percentage errors of the three Newey-West standard errors with 4 lags against 0.2794, 0.0565 and 0.0780; three times the errors of the skewness against $-0.350$ and of the Pearson kurtosis against 4.600; 0.01 times the 12 weights). Most seeds from 1 to 79 met every target; seed 62 was chosen because every factor month lies within 2.5 SDs of its mean, where seed 1 had a 21% market month (section 6).
3. Response: $\text{fund} = 0.30 + 0.75\,\text{mkt} + 0.15\,\text{mom} + e$. Because $e$ is orthogonal to the columns, the fitted coefficients equal the true ones exactly.

The CSV (`content/fixtures/stats/fund-60.csv`, 10 decimal places) and statsmodels 0.15.0's stored output travel together; CI regenerates the CSV from this recipe and refits it (section 6). Printed by `sm.OLS(fund, sm.add_constant(X)).fit().summary(xname=["const", "mkt", "mom"], yname="fund")` [verified]:

```text
                            OLS Regression Results
==============================================================================
Dep. Variable:                   fund   R-squared:                       0.782
Model:                            OLS   Adj. R-squared:                  0.775
Method:                 Least Squares   F-statistic:                     102.5
Date:                Tue, 06 Oct 2026   Prob (F-statistic):           1.33e-19
Time:                        16:55:23   Log-Likelihood:                -122.11
No. Observations:                  60   AIC:                             250.2
Df Residuals:                      57   BIC:                             256.5
Df Model:                           2
Covariance Type:            nonrobust
==============================================================================
                 coef    std err          t      P>|t|      [0.025      0.975]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
const          0.3000      0.249      1.204      0.234      -0.199       0.799
mkt            0.7500      0.058     12.905      0.000       0.634       0.866
mom            0.1500      0.084      1.788      0.079      -0.018       0.318
==============================================================================
Omnibus:                        6.322   Durbin-Watson:                   1.606
Prob(Omnibus):                  0.042   Jarque-Bera (JB):                7.625
Skew:                          -0.350   Prob(JB):                       0.0221
Kurtosis:                       4.600   Cond. No.                         4.77
==============================================================================

Notes:
[1] Standard Errors assume that the covariance matrix of the errors is correctly specified.
```

"nonrobust" is statsmodels' literal label; lessons show it as printed (open item 8). The `summary-table` widget shows any cell at full precision on tap; the values items compute from are:

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| SSE, SST, SSR | 205.80, 945.62, 739.82 |
| $s^2 = \text{SSE}/57$, $s$ | 3.6105, 1.9001 |
| $R^2$, adjusted $R^2$, $F$ | 0.782365, 0.774729, 102.453 |
| Log-likelihood, AIC, BIC | $-122.1131$, 250.2262, 256.5093 |
| std err (const, mkt, mom) | 0.249183, 0.058116, 0.083900 |
| $t$ (const, mkt, mom); $P > \lvert t\rvert$ | 1.2039, 12.9052, 1.7878; 0.2336, $<10^{-17}$, 0.0791 |
| Newey-West std err, 4 lags, no small-sample factor (const, mkt, mom) | 0.2794, 0.0565, 0.0780; $t$ for mom 1.923, $p = 0.059$ with $t_{57}$, or 0.054 with the normal, which `fit(cov_type="HAC")` uses by default |
| Residual lag-1 autocorrelation; $1 - \text{DW}/2$ | 0.179; 0.197 |
| Test of $\beta_{\text{mkt}} = 1$ | $t = (0.75 - 1)/0.058116 = -4.30$ on 57 df, $p = 0.00007$ |

**Specimen Q19.** "A fund's monthly excess returns were regressed on the market and momentum factors over 60 months; the summary is shown. For every field, say what it is, how it is computed, and what it says here. Then say which printed numbers you would not trust as they stand, and what you would do. Write one line per field or group of fields. (10 marks, about 15 minutes.)"

*Model answer.*

1. **Labels.** Dep. Variable, Model, Method, Date and Time describe the run: ordinary least squares on `fund`. They carry no statistics.
2. **Counts.** No. Observations $n = 60$. Df Residuals $= n - p = 57$ with $p = 3$ coefficients. Df Model $= p - 1 = 2$: statsmodels does not count the constant.
3. **Covariance Type** nonrobust: every std err, $t$, $P > \lvert t\rvert$ and interval below uses the classical $s^2(X^\top X)^{-1}$, which assumes uncorrelated errors of constant variance. Note [1] says the same.
4. **R-squared** $= 1 - \text{SSE}/\text{SST} = 1 - 205.80/945.62 = 0.782$: the share of the fund's month-to-month variation around its mean that the two factors account for. **Adj. R-squared** $= 1 - (1 - R^2)\frac{n - 1}{n - p} = 0.775$ charges for the extra coefficient.
5. **F-statistic** $= \frac{R^2/(p - 1)}{(1 - R^2)/(n - p)} = 102.5$ tests that both slopes are zero; **Prob (F-statistic)** $1.33 \times 10^{-19}$ is its p-value on $(2, 57)$ degrees of freedom. Unsurprising for a fund with market exposure, and silent about alpha.
6. **Log-Likelihood** $= -\frac n2\left(\ln 2\pi + \ln\frac{\text{SSE}}{n} + 1\right) = -122.11$, the normal log-likelihood at the MLE. **AIC** $= -2\ell + 2p = 250.2$ and **BIC** $= -2\ell + p\ln n = 256.5$, with $p = 3$ coefficients and $\sigma^2$ not counted (statsmodels' convention). Both mean something only in comparison with another model of the same `fund` over the same months.
7. **coef.** Least-squares estimates, each a partial effect with the other factor held fixed: const 0.30 is the monthly alpha in percent (3.6% a year), mkt 0.75 the market beta, mom 0.15 the momentum loading.
8. **std err** $= s\sqrt{[(X^\top X)^{-1}]_{jj}}$ with $s^2 = \text{SSE}/57 = 3.61$; **t** $=$ coef / std err; **P>|t|** is the two-sided tail of $t_{57}$ beyond $\lvert t\rvert$.
9. **Reading them.** Alpha: $t = 1.20$, $p = 0.234$, no evidence of skill. Beta: $t = 12.9$, so clearly positive, and the question that matters, $\beta = 1$, gives $t = (0.75 - 1)/0.058 = -4.3$: the fund carries less market risk than the market. Momentum: $p = 0.079$, not significant at 5%; this is not "a 92% chance momentum matters", because a p-value is a statement about the data under $\beta = 0$, not a probability for $\beta$.
10. **[0.025, 0.975]** $=$ coef $\pm t_{57, 0.975}\times$ std err with $t_{57, 0.975} = 2.002$, not 1.96: for mom $[-0.018, 0.318]$. An interval contains 0 exactly when $P > \lvert t\rvert$ exceeds 0.05.
11. **Omnibus** 6.322 is D'Agostino and Pearson's test, which combines a test of the residuals' skewness and one of their kurtosis; **Prob(Omnibus)** 0.042 rejects normal errors at 5%.
12. **Skew** $-0.350$ and **Kurtosis** 4.600, which is Pearson's kurtosis (normal $= 3$, excess 1.6): a longer left tail and fatter tails than normal, typical of monthly fund returns.
13. **Jarque-Bera** $= \frac n6\left(S^2 + \frac{(K - 3)^2}{4}\right) = 10(0.1225 + 0.64) = 7.625$, referred to $\chi^2_2$: **Prob(JB)** $= e^{-7.625/2} = 0.022$, agreeing with Omnibus. Consequence: the coefficients are still unbiased; with $n = 60$ the $t$-based p-values are approximations rather than exact.
14. **Durbin-Watson** $= \frac{\sum(e_t - e_{t-1})^2}{\sum e_t^2} = 1.606 \approx 2(1 - r_1)$, so $r_1 \approx 0.2$: mild positive autocorrelation in the residuals, which makes classical standard errors too small, most of all the constant's. DW looks at lag 1 only.
15. **Cond. No.** 4.77 is the condition number of $X$ in these units: no numerical or collinearity problem (the factors correlate at 0.317, VIF 1.11). It depends on units, so it is a warning light, not a test.
16. **Judgement.** Distrust, as printed, the standard errors and p-values (lines 13 and 14) and the reading of $R^2$ as skill (it measures market exposure). Refit with Newey-West errors (4 lags): std errs 0.279, 0.057, 0.078; the alpha's $t$ falls to 1.07 and mom's rises to 1.92 ($p = 0.059$), so no conclusion at 5% changes. Report alpha as "0.30% a month, not distinguishable from zero", and test $\beta = 1$ rather than $\beta = 0$.

*Rubric (10).* Each point is earned only when every field it names is right.

| Points | Evidence (model lines) |
|---|---|
| 1 | Counts and degrees of freedom, with Df Model excluding the constant (2) |
| 1 | Covariance type and note [1] tied to the classical assumptions (3) |
| 1 | $R^2$ and adjusted $R^2$, each with its formula (4) |
| 1 | $F$ and its p-value: what is tested, on which degrees of freedom (5) |
| 1 | Log-likelihood, AIC and BIC, with statsmodels' count of $p$ and the comparison-only caveat (6) |
| 1 | Coefficients read as partial effects in units (7) |
| 1 | std err, $t$ and $P > \lvert t\rvert$ computed and read correctly, including the right p-value sentence and a test against a value other than 0 (8, 9) |
| 1 | Intervals with $t_{57}$ (10) |
| 1 | Diagnostics: Omnibus, JB, skew, Pearson kurtosis, Durbin-Watson and condition number, each read correctly (11 to 15) |
| 1 | Judgement: which numbers to distrust, and a named remedy (16) |

Bertie's suggested marks quote the learner's line for each point (AI-TUTOR.md 2.7, tier `complex`). Common losses the marking prompt lists: Df Model counted with the constant; kurtosis read as excess; "a 92% chance"; 1.96 for the interval; a Durbin-Watson near 2 called bad; AIC read in isolation.

**The held-out pool** (`pool.stats.q19`, six fixtures built by the same recipe with their own targets; each has a model answer of the same shape and the same rubric):

| Fixture | What it adds to the explanation |
|---|---|
| `fix.stats.q19-fan` | Fan-shaped errors: HC3 standard errors differ from the classical ones by about 40% on one slope |
| `fix.stats.q19-collinear` | Two near-duplicate factors: Cond. No. above $10^3$ and statsmodels' second note; individually insignificant slopes with a large $F$ |
| `fix.stats.q19-no-constant` | No constant: uncentred $R^2$, Df Model equal to $p$, and the R-squared (uncentered) label |
| `fix.stats.q19-levels` | Price on price over four years: $R^2 = 0.97$, Durbin-Watson 0.2, $t$ above 100 (homework RG5 example A, examined) |
| `fix.stats.q19-heavy` | Kurtosis 9 and one crash month: JB in the hundreds, and the crash month's influence on beta |
| `fix.stats.q19-small` | $n = 15$ with a regime dummy and an interaction: $t_{11}$ intervals, and a main effect that is not an average effect |

### 4.3 The 1.6 exit, rebuilt in the app

**`1.6.exit`: "Fit a linear regression in NumPy, match the statsmodels output, and explain every number in its summary table."**

| Part | Instrument | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| Brief | Shown in lesson 69 and in the drill | The conventions are fixed so answers are checkable: the design matrix carries the constant as a named column; statsmodels 0.15.0 is the reference (open item 6); "every number" means every field of `summary()`: the header block, the coefficient block, the diagnostic block and its notes |
| Gate 1: paper | `exm.stats` at 70% (56 of 80) with `slotMin` 0.7 on Q19 | Q19 is the "explain" half (section 4.2). A sitting that passes the paper with Q19 below 7 of 10 passes the topic exam; the exit then waits for a 15-minute exit-item sitting of Q19 from the pool (decision E3) |
| Gate 2: capstone | `drl.stats.exit-ols` (`web`, 4 hours; in the Artifact the screen hands off to the web app and returns by result code) | Functions: `fit(X, y)` returns coefficients computed through `np.linalg.qr`; `summary_fields(X, y, names)` returns a dictionary keyed by statsmodels' printed labels (`"R-squared"`, `"Df Model"`, `"std err"`, `"Cond. No."` and so on), coefficient fields per name; `notes(fields)` returns the notes statsmodels would print. NumPy and SciPy only (`scipy.stats.normaltest` is allowed for Omnibus, as in homework RG5) |
| Hidden tests | 9 | (1) every field of `fix.stats.fund-60` against the stored statsmodels output, to relative $10^{-8}$ where statsmodels exposes the value as an attribute and to printed precision otherwise; (2) to (5) the same on four more fixtures: no constant (uncentred $R^2$, Df Model $= p$, AIC with $k = p$), near-collinear (Cond. No. above $10^3$ and the second note's text), fan-shaped errors, and $n = 12$ with $p = 4$; (6) a constant column in any position is detected as statsmodels' `k_constant` detects it; (7) a rank-deficient $X$ raises `ValueError` (statsmodels uses a pseudo-inverse and does not raise; the drill is stricter on purpose); (8) `"statsmodels" not in sys.modules` after the learner's module is imported; (9) coefficients on a fixture with condition number $10^7$ within relative $10^{-6}$ of the QR reference, which the normal-equations inverse misses [verify in CI] |
| Known-wrong solutions | 5 | $s^2$ with divisor $n$ (fails 1 on std err); excess kurtosis (fails 1 on Kurtosis and JB); AIC counting $\sigma^2$ (fails 1 on AIC); Df Model counting the constant (fails 1); 1.96 in place of $t_{n-p}$ (fails 1 on the interval) |
| Optional local run | Repository task, not gated | The learner commits the capstone module with `pytest` tests that compare it with statsmodels on their own machine (decision S8) |
| Help | Bertie | Locked until the first submission of gate 2, as for homework; away for the paper |
| Pass | Both gates | `estHours` 9.5: paper 1.5, capstone 4, practice paper 1.5, reviewing both papers 1.0, local run 1.5 (section 1.12 request to ASSESSMENT.md) |

```ts
// exit data for 1.6, using ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart
{ id: '1.6.exit',
  text: 'Fit a linear regression in NumPy, match the statsmodels output, and explain every number in its summary table',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.stats', pass: 0.7, slotMin: { q19: 0.7 } },
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.stats.exit-ols'] } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 9.5 }
```

**Rehearsal.** Lesson 69 is the exit in miniature; homework RG5 example B recomputes blanked cells of the same table; the RG2, RG3 and RG5 drills build the pieces the capstone assembles. Neither gate replaces the other: the paper closes the topic's knowledge without notes, the capstone proves the numbers in code.

### 4.4 `exm.ts`: 2.2 Time series

**Duration** 75 minutes (summed item estimates 50 minutes). **Marks** 60. **Items** 15 in four untimed sections with a suggested split. **Pass** 42 of 60; the `2.2.exit` bar is 7 of 10 on Q15 (section 4.6). **Calculator** scientific. **Aids** `card.stats.tables` with its time-series rows (decision E2). **Runs on** both targets.
**Format mix by marks:** numeric 28 (46.7%), multi-select 6 (10%), sort 4 (6.7%), match 3 (5%), single choice 3 (5%), long-form 16 (26.7%).
**Difficulty mix by marks:** d2 12 (20%), d3 30 (50%), d4 12 (20%), d5 6 (10%).

| # | Section | Level | Skill | Format | Difficulty | Marks | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A Returns and dependence (18 min) | SM1 | Simple and log returns over several days from prices; annual volatility from daily by $\sqrt{252}$ | Numeric (2 parts) | 2 | 4 | `gen.ts.log-return`, `gen.ts.sqrt-h` |
| 2 | A | SM1 | Sort six series (a price, its log return, a trending volume, a seasonal series, an AR(1) spread, a GARCH return) into stationary or not, each with the transform that makes it stationary | Sort | 2 | 4 | `gen.ts.classify`, `gen.ts.transform` |
| 3 | A | SM2 | $r_k$ from a short series with the full-sample mean; Ljung-Box $Q$ from given autocorrelations, and its decision | Numeric (2 parts) | 3 | 4 | `gen.ts.acf-value`, `gen.ts.ljung-box` |
| 4 | A | SM2 | Effective sample size of an AR(1) series and the standard error of its mean | Numeric | 3 | 3 | `gen.ts.eff-n` |
| 5 | B ARMA and forecasting (20 min) | SM3 | AR(1) mean, variance and half-life; whether a given AR(2) is stationary | Numeric (3), single choice (1) | 2 | 4 | `gen.ts.half-life`, `gen.ts.region` |
| 6 | B | SM3 | Match four correlograms (ACF and PACF bars) to AR(1), AR(2), MA(1) and ARMA(1, 1) | Match | 3 | 3 | `gen.ts.identify` |
