**Totals.** Marks 60. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 4 + 4 = 12$ (Q1, Q2, Q5); d3 $4 + 3 + 3 + 4 + 3 + 3 + 10 = 30$ (Q3, Q4, Q6, Q8, Q9, Q12, Q15); d4 $6 + 3 + 3 = 12$ (Q7, Q10, Q11); d5 $3 + 3 = 6$ (Q13, Q14). Generator slots carry 41 marks (68%). Q15 is d3 although it is the exit item: the idea is taught from lesson 1, rehearsed in lessons 35 and 43 and homework CO1 and RG5, and its bar (7 of 10) is where the difficulty sits.

**Coverage.** Every form tests all 8 levels. **Marks by course:** SM 32 (53%), VL 9 (15%), CO 9 plus Q15's 10 (32%). Against the lesson shares (SM 49%, VL 23%, CO 28%), VL is light on purpose: the project `prj.ts.vol-forecast` (4.5 hours, fitted on 3,000 days, scored and backtested) examines volatility in more depth than three timed items can, while the paper weights what the exit names.

**Worked example of one slot (Q13, d5, form A).** "Two futures on related commodities, 750 daily closes, both I(1) by ADF and KPSS. Step 1 of Engle-Granger gives $\hat\beta = 0.82$; the residual ADF statistic is $-3.62$; the spread's AR(1) coefficient is 0.93. MacKinnon's two-series values at $T = 750$ are $-3.91$ (1%) and $-3.34$ (5%). (a) The spread's half-life in days. (b) The decision."

- *Answer (a), 2 marks.* $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln 0.93} = 9.55$ days; accepted 9.5 to 9.6. Diagnosed: 14.3 ($\frac{1}{1 - 0.93}$, the mean lag, `mc.ts.half-life-is-mean-lag` [new]); 0.07 (slip).
- *Answer (b), 1 mark, single choice.* Correct: "Reject 'no cointegration' at 5% but not at 1%." Distractors: "Reject at 1% too, because $-3.62$ is below the Dickey-Fuller 1% value $-3.43$" (`mc.ts.df-critical-values-for-residuals`); "Do not reject at 5%, because $-3.62$ is above $-3.91$" (slip: the 1% value read as the 5% one); "Reject, so trade the spread with $\hat\beta = 0.82$ from all 750 days" (`mc.ts.full-sample-beta-in-backtest`).

### 4.5 Q15: why returns, not prices

**Specimen Q15** (`pool.ts.q15`, framing A). "A colleague regresses the daily close of share A on the daily close of share B over four years and finds $R^2 = 0.97$ and $t = 182$. He also fits an AR(1) to A's price, gets $\hat\varphi = 0.998$, and calls the price 'highly predictable'. Explain why models are fitted to returns and not prices, and say what he should do instead. (10 marks, about 15 minutes.)"

*Model answer.*

1. **Prices wander.** A share price is close to a random walk: $P_t = P_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t$ in logs, so $\text{Var}(P_t - P_0) = t\sigma^2$ grows without bound and there is no fixed mean to return to (lesson 3). Returns, the differences, hover around a stable mean with a stable spread (lessons 1 and 2).
2. **Estimation needs stationarity.** Sample means, variances and correlations converge to fixed population values, and the CLT gives standard errors, only for stationary (and ergodic) series. For a random walk the sample mean and variance depend on the path and grow with the sample, so there is nothing for them to estimate.
3. **The usual tables break at a unit root.** In $\Delta y_t = c + \gamma y_{t-1} + e_t$ with $\gamma = 0$, the $t$-ratio is not $t$-distributed: its 5% point is $-2.86$, not $-1.645$, and the $-1.645$ rule "rejects" a true unit root 46% of the time (lesson 33).
4. **Regressions of price on price are spurious.** Two independent random walks give $\lvert t\rvert > 1.96$ about 76% of the time at $T = 100$, and $t$ grows with $T$ (lesson 35). The symptom is $R^2$ high with Durbin-Watson near 0 (Granger and Newbold): $R^2 = 0.97$ and $t = 182$ say only that both series trend.
5. **"Predictable" prices are persistence, not skill.** $\hat\varphi = 0.998$ is what a random walk produces, with a downward bias; its forecast is about yesterday's price, which is the naive benchmark (lesson 17). Predictability is a claim about returns: does anything beat $\hat P_{t+1} = P_t$?
6. **Returns are what is earned and compared.** P&L, Sharpe ratios, betas and VaR are defined on returns; returns are scale-free, so a 1-point move means the same at 10 and at 1,000 only after dividing by the price; log returns add over time.
7. **Risk scales on returns.** Volatility, the $\sqrt h$ rule and GARCH are statements about returns; a price's "variance" has no fixed value to report.
8. **The exception is cointegration.** If some combination $P^A_t - \beta P^B_t$ is stationary, the levels regression is meaningful and $\hat\beta$ is even superconsistent; but this must be tested on the residuals with Engle-Granger critical values ($-3.34$ at 5% for two series), not Dickey-Fuller's (lesson 39). An error-correction model then uses returns and the lagged spread together (lesson 40).
9. **What to do.** Test each price for a unit root (ADF and KPSS); regress returns on returns for a beta; test the pair for cointegration with the right tables before reading anything from a levels regression; evaluate any forecast against the random walk out of sample.
10. **Returns are not the end of the story.** Returns are close to uncorrelated but not independent: volatility clusters (lesson 22), so return regressions need HAC errors or a volatility model; and differencing a cointegrated pair throws away the long-run relation, which is why the test in line 8 comes first.

*Rubric (10), one point per line:* random-walk behaviour of prices with the variance growing in $t$ (1); why estimation needs stationarity (2); the unit-root distribution with a number (3); spurious regression with its symptom, tied to the colleague's numbers (4); persistence against predictability, with the naive benchmark (5); returns as the economic quantity (6); risk measures on returns (7); cointegration as the exception, with the right critical values (8); a concrete remedy (9); the caveat on volatility clustering or lost long-run information (10). Bertie's marking prompt lists the common losses: "prices are not normal" offered as the reason (it is not the reason); cointegration omitted; no remedy.

**The held-out pool** (`pool.ts.q15`): (A) the specimen; (B) an ARMA fitted to an index level and used to forecast a month ahead; (C) a hedge ratio set from the correlation of two price series (0.95) whose returns correlate at 0.3; (D) a portfolio manager's question, "if prices are so persistent, why can't you forecast them?"; (E) a gradient-boosted model trained on price levels with a shuffled split, reporting $R^2 = 0.99$; (F) a pairs desk regressing one price on another, where the levels regression is right only after a cointegration test. Each has its own model answer; the rubric's ten points are the same.

### 4.6 The 2.2 exit, rebuilt in the app

**`2.2.exit`: "Test a pair of price series for cointegration, and explain why models are fitted to returns and not prices."**

| Part | Instrument | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| Brief | Shown in lesson 43 and in the drill | Conventions: log prices; ADF with a constant and lags chosen by AIC for each series; Engle-Granger with $y$ regressed on a constant and $x$, residual ADF without a constant, MacKinnon's two-series critical value at the sample's $T$; both orderings reported; statsmodels 0.15.0 is the reference (open item 6) |
| Gate 1: paper | `exm.ts` at 70% (42 of 60) with `slotMin` 0.7 on Q15 | Q15 is the "explain" half (section 4.5); a missed Q15 bar is retaken as a 15-minute exit-item sitting |
| Gate 2: capstone | `drl.ts.exit-coint` (`web`, 2.5 hours; handoff from the Artifact) | Functions: `integration_order(p, max_d=2)` returns 0, 1 or 2 by ADF on levels, then differences; `engle_granger(y, x)` returns $\hat\beta$, the statistic, the 5% critical value and the decision; `half_life(spread)` from the spread's AR(1) coefficient; `pair_report(y, x, formation)` estimates $\hat\beta$, the spread's mean and SD and the half-life on the first `formation` observations only, and returns a dictionary with both orderings and a one-sentence verdict. The learner may call `adfuller` and `mackinnoncrit`, not `coint` |
| Hidden tests | 8 | (1) on 8 generated pairs at $T = 750$ (3 cointegrated with spread AR(1) coefficients 0.85, 0.90 and 0.95; 3 independent random walks; 2 with return correlation 0.9 and no cointegration) and on the CO2 drill's planted pair of independent walks whose statistic is $-3.0$, every decision is right; (2) each statistic matches `statsmodels.tsa.stattools.coint(y, x)` to $10^{-6}$; (3) every critical value matches `mackinnoncrit(N=2, regression="c", nobs=T - 1)` at 5%, the value `coint` itself reports (statsmodels 0.15.0's `coint` passes `nobs - 1` [verified]); (4) `integration_order` returns 1 for all 16 series and 0 for a planted stationary one; (5) half-lives match $\frac{\ln 0.5}{\ln\hat\varphi}$ from an OLS AR(1) to $10^{-8}$, and `half_life` raises for $\hat\varphi \ge 1$; (6) a corrupted-future test: changing observations after `formation` leaves $\hat\beta$, the bands and the half-life in the report unchanged; (7) both orderings appear in the report; (8) `coint` patched to raise is never called |
| Generator | `gen.ts.exit-pair` | Pairs are admitted only when the Engle-Granger statistic lies at least 1.0 from the 5% critical value (section 3.0), so no decision is borderline; the generator records each pair's truth |
| Known-wrong solutions | 3 | Dickey-Fuller's $-2.86$ as the residual critical value (fails 1 on the planted pair, the one pair exempt from the margin rule); a full-sample $\hat\beta$ in `pair_report` (fails 6); a residual ADF with a constant (fails 2) |
| Help | Bertie | Locked until the first submission of gate 2 |
| Pass | Both gates | `estHours` 5.0: paper 1.25, capstone 2.5, practice paper 1.25 |

```ts
// exit data for 2.2
{ id: '2.2.exit',
  text: 'Test a pair of price series for cointegration, and explain why models are fitted to returns and not prices',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.ts', pass: 0.7, slotMin: { q15: 0.7 } },
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.ts.exit-coint'] } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 5.0 }
```

**Real pairs.** An optional extension lets the learner run the capstone's functions on a pair from their own CSVs in the web app (decision T8); it is ungated because no generator knows the truth of a real pair.

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **One primary resource per item** (curriculum rule 1). 1.6 reads **DeGroot and Schervish, 4th edition**, with **MIT 18.650 (Fall 2016, Rigollet)** as the lecture parallel; **OpenIntro Statistics, 4th edition** is the curriculum's "if it is too steep" route: read the OpenIntro row *instead of* the DeGroot row for that level, never as well, inside the same hours. 2.2 reads **FPP, the Pythonic Way** for the forecasting half and **Tsay, 3rd edition** for the financial half, with **MIT 18.S096 (Fall 2013)** as the lecture parallel. Everything else is a targeted second explanation, extra problems, or stretch.
- **Problems over reading** (rule 2). Every row says what to do. Rows carry hours; those hours become the level's `companion` entries (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.2) and sum to section 1's external budgets: **1.6, 28.0 hours** (watching 15.0, reading 5.0, problems 8.0) and **2.2, 11.0 hours** (watching 5.0, reading 3.0, problems 3.0). Rows at 0 h are conditional ("if the quiz is below 3 of 4"), alternatives or stretch: optional, logged as spent time, never part of the estimate.
- **Lectures** are budgeted at 1.35 hours for an 18.650 lecture (about 80 minutes recorded, with pauses) and 1.25 to 1.5 hours for an 18.S096 lecture. Watch the lecture before the level's first lesson or after its last; the rows say which.
- **Reading is targeted.** 0.25 hours a level reads the named section's definitions, one worked example and the summary, not the chapter. Lessons carry the explanation; the book is the second voice and the source of exercises.
- **Exercises.** Attempt before checking; the DeGroot and Schervish student solutions manual covers odd-numbered exercises [check]; MIT 18.05 (Spring 2014) problem sets and exams come with solutions (canon-gap.md 6); 18.650's problem sets are posted, their solutions [unverified]; Tsay's exercises use the author's data sets.
- **[check]** marks a chapter, section, lecture or problem number recalled but not confirmed here (open item 9; ocw.mit.edu and otexts.com were unreachable from this environment). **Confirmed by search on 6 October 2026:** 18.650 Fall 2016 lecture 11 is "Parametric Hypothesis Testing (cont.) and Testing Goodness of Fit", lecture 13 is "Regression" and lectures 14 and 15 continue regression; 18.S096 Fall 2013 lecture 6 is "Regression Analysis", 8 "Time Series Analysis I", 9 "Volatility Modeling" and 11 "Time Series Analysis II" (vector autoregressions), with case studies; FPP, the Pythonic Way, carries FPP3's content in Python with two added chapters (neural networks and foundation models). A second search pass on 6 October 2026 (the verification log) confirmed 18.650 lectures 1 to 8 and 17 by title, lecture 6's Fisher information, 18.S096 lectures 7 and 12, DeGroot §7.5, §8.8, §9.5, §9.6, §10.6, §11.5 and §12.6, OpenIntro §5.1, §7.2, §8.4 and §9.5, FPP3's chapter numbers, and Tsay §2.7, §3.5, §7.2, §8.6 and §8.8. Other DeGroot section numbers follow section 2.8's table [check].

## 6. Verification approach

Every auto-marked item has two independent implementations of its answer (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 6.2): the TypeScript generator or the author's declared answer that builds it, using the engine's `ols`, `tests` and `ts` services, and a Python verifier written from the generator's plain-language `semantics` without reading the TypeScript, run in CI under `tools/zqverify`. CI replays every listed distractor through the runtime grader and requires it to be marked wrong with its intended diagnosis. Statistics and time series add six rules of their own [our choice]:

1. **statsmodels is the third implementation wherever statsmodels prints the number.** Regression tables, HC and HAC errors, `acf`, `pacf`, Ljung-Box, `adfuller`, `kpss`, `coint`, `ARIMA` and `Logit` results are compared with statsmodels 0.15.0, the pinned version (open item 6), to relative $10^{-9}$ where it exposes the value and to printed precision otherwise. A version bump that changes any printed digit fails CI until the fixtures are regenerated and the owner signs the diff.
2. **Exact first.** Binomial tails, permutation p-values, BH and Holm sets, grid posteriors and Beta-binomial predictives are exact rationals (`fractions`); normal, $t$, $\chi^2$ and $F$ quantiles are recomputed with mpmath at 30 digits and compared with SciPy.
3. **Simulation checks claims, never answers.** Every sampling-distribution claim in the copy, whether exact (the 0.122 of lesson 5, the 87.8% coverage of lesson 22) or known only by simulation (the 46% of lesson 33, the 76% of lesson 35), is re-simulated in CI with a fixed seed and at least $2 \times 10^5$ replications and must lie within 4 standard errors of the quoted value; claims of the second kind store the quoted value with its standard error beside the copy.
4. **No borderline classifications.** Items that ask "stationary?", "cointegrated?", "significant?" or "which model?" admit an instance only when its statistic lies at least 1.0 from the critical value (section 3.0), or, for p-values, outside $[0.03, 0.08]$ around a 5% decision [our choice]; the one deliberate exception (the planted $-3.0$ pair in the CO2 drill and the exit capstone) is listed by id.
5. **Critical values have two sources.** Dickey-Fuller, Engle-Granger and KPSS values come from statsmodels (`mackinnoncrit`, `kpss`) and are re-simulated in CI (40,000 replications per case and sample size, within 0.05); the tables card is checked entry by entry against both.
6. **Fixtures are code.** Each fixture (`fix.stats.fund-60`, the Q19 pool, the CO2 and exit pairs, the Lab series) is a CSV, its generator recipe and seed, and statsmodels' stored output. CI regenerates the CSV from the recipe and checks it value by value at $10^{-10}$, refits it, and compares every stored field. A number quoted in prose (section 4.2's table, homework RG2 to RG5) is bound to the fixture's stored output by the copy-binding of CONTENT-FORMAT.md 6.8, so an edit to either side fails CI.

| Family | Items | CI: second implementation | Runtime check | Traps the check must catch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Standard errors and sample sizes | ES1, SM2 lesson 10, section 7 | Closed form in exact arithmetic where inputs are rational; required-$n$ answers checked by testing $n - 1$ and $n$ against the target exactly, so a floating-point ceiling cannot pass (homework ES1 test 6); equicorrelated variances by enumerating the covariance matrix for $n \le 20$ | `numeric` with the item's tolerance | SD for SE and the reverse; $\sigma/n$; $\lceil 225.00000000000006\rceil = 226$ |
| Bias, variance and MSE | ES2 | SymPy expectations under the stated model, against a seeded simulation of $10^6$ draws within 4 standard errors; MSE crossovers by SymPy `solve` | `numeric`, `expression` | Bias not squared; MSE evaluated at the wrong $\theta$ |
| Likelihood and MLE | ES3 | SymPy score equation and second derivative; boundary MLEs by evaluating the likelihood on both sides of the claimed maximum; numerical MLEs from 20 random starts against `scipy.stats.<dist>.fit` | `expression` probed on 8 seeded data sets; `numeric` | A stationary point that is a minimum; a boundary maximum missed; the censored observations dropped |
| Information, asymptotics, delta method | ES4 | Fisher information two ways in SymPy, $-E[\ell'']$ and $\text{Var}(\ell')$; delta-method standard errors against simulation at $n = 10^4$ (within 2%) | `numeric` | Total and per-observation information confused; an SE annualised by 252 |
| Intervals | IT1 | Endpoints from mpmath quantiles; exact coverage of Wald and Wilson by summing binomial probabilities; bootstrap items graded only on fixed data with a fixed seed, against a second resampler written separately | `interval` with endpoint tolerance | $z$ for $t$ at small $n$; Wald at 0 or 1; an interval for $\sigma$ symmetric about $s$ |
| Tests, p-values and power | IT2 | p-values from exact binomial sums or mpmath tails; power and sample size by closed form against $2 \times 10^5$ simulated tests | `numeric`, `choice` | One-sided for two-sided; $z_{1-\alpha}$ in a two-sided power |
| The testing toolkit | IT3 | $\chi^2$, KS and JB against `scipy.stats` (`chisquare`, `chi2_contingency`, `kstest`, `jarque_bera`); permutation p-values by full enumeration with `itertools`; LR statistics by SymPy; Neyman-Pearson regions by brute force over every region of size at most $\alpha$ on small outcome spaces | `numeric`, `choice`, `sort` | Proportions for counts; degrees of freedom not reduced for estimated parameters; a Monte Carlo p-value of 0 |
| Multiple testing | IT4, `exm.stats` Q11, Q12 | Holm and BH sets by the textbook procedures and by a brute-force reading of their definitions (all subsets for $m \le 12$); $E[\max]$ of $N$ normals by quadrature against $10^6$ simulated maxima | `set`, `numeric` | BH stopped at the first p-value above its line; Holm continued past its first failure |
| Regression coefficients and tables | RG1, RG2, RG5, `exm.stats` Q13, Q14, Q19, `drl.stats.exit-ols` | Three implementations: the engine's `ols` service, a NumPy QR reference, and statsmodels `OLS(...).fit()`; every field of `summary()` compared (rule 1) | `numeric`, `interval` | $s^2$ on divisor $n$; Df Model counting the constant; AIC counting $\sigma^2$; 1.96 for $t_{n-p}$; excess for Pearson kurtosis; the uncentred $R^2$ |
| Assumption failures | RG3, `exm.stats` Q15, Q16 | HC0 to HC3 and HAC against statsmodels `cov_type` (HAC without the small-sample factor, statsmodels' default [verified]); hat values and Cook's distance against `OLSInfluence`; DW against `durbin_watson`; coverage claims of lessons 55 and 56 by $10^5$ simulated fits | `numeric`, `match` | Bartlett weights $1 - l/L$; HC errors changing the coefficients; DW read at lag 2 |
| Multiple regression in practice | RG4 | VIF against `variance_inflation_factor`; OVB items built so the short slope is $\beta_1 + \beta_2\delta$ exactly in the sample; logistic fits against `Logit` to $10^{-8}$ | `numeric`, `expression` | VIFs from pairwise correlations; a log-level coefficient read as a percentage; a log-odds coefficient read as a probability change |
| Bayesian inference | BY1, BY2, `exm.stats` Q17, Q18 | Grid and conjugate posteriors in exact fractions; Beta and normal quantiles by mpmath; Bayes factors by quadrature against closed forms; Metropolis drills against the grid posterior with tolerances CI records from 200 seeded chains | `numeric`, `distribution` (D items; total variation stated per item) | No renormalisation; rejected proposals dropped; the prior counted twice |
| Returns and stationarity | SM1, `exm.ts` Q1, Q2 | Returns and aggregation recomputed in exact decimals; $\sqrt h$ and variance-ratio items by closed form; classification items labelled by the generator under rule 4 | `numeric`, `sort` | Simple returns added over time; 252 for $\sqrt{252}$; a trending stationary series called a random walk |
| Autocorrelation | SM2, `exm.ts` Q3, Q4 | `acf` (`adjusted=False`), `pacf` and `acorr_ljungbox` against a direct NumPy implementation; effective sample sizes by the exact finite-$T$ sum and by its limit | `numeric`, `distribution` | Separate means for the shifted pieces; fitted parameters not subtracted from Ljung-Box's degrees of freedom |
| ARMA theory and fitting | SM3, `exm.ts` Q5 to Q7 | Moments, ACFs and stationarity regions by SymPy; theoretical ACFs against `ArmaProcess.acf`; roots by NumPy against SymPy; fits against `ARIMA` | `numeric`, `point`, `match` | $\lvert\varphi_1\rvert < 1$ and $\lvert\varphi_2\rvert < 1$ checked separately; the non-invertible MA root |
| Forecasting and evaluation | SM4, `exm.ts` Q8 | Closed-form forecasts and error variances against `ARIMA(...).get_forecast` and simulation; rolling-origin answers recomputed by a second implementation fed corrupted future values, which must change nothing | `numeric`, `order` | Intervals that ignore $h$; scoring on the training period |
| Volatility | VL1, VL2, `exm.ts` Q9 to Q11 | EWMA and GARCH recursions by two implementations; long-run variance, half-life and term-structure answers by closed form against iteration; GARCH MLEs against a reference optimiser from 20 starts; Kupiec statistics and binomial tails exact | `numeric`, `point` | $r_t$ inside $\sigma_t^2$ (look-ahead); $\sqrt h$ times today's volatility; variance half-life read as volatility half-life |
| Unit roots and cointegration | CO1, CO2, `exm.ts` Q12 to Q14, `drl.ts.exit-coint` | `adfuller`, `kpss` and `coint` against the drills' NumPy implementations; critical values by rule 5; generated pairs by rule 4, with each pair's truth stored | `numeric`, `choice`, `multi` | Dickey-Fuller values for residuals; failing to reject read as proof; a full-sample $\hat\beta$ |
| Simulation screens | every course | Never graded (decision 3). CI runs each screen's seeded simulation and requires the displayed result within 4 standard errors of the exact value, so no seed shows a misleading run | Predictions graded before the run | A seed whose run favours a wrong answer |
| Long-form items | every set; `exm.stats` Q19, Q20; `exm.ts` Q7, Q15 | SymPy checks each model-answer line (each equation equivalent to the previous under the stated assumptions); every number recomputed; every rubric point names lines that exist; Q19's and Q15's pools checked against their fixtures' stored output | Self-marking against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote (AI-TUTOR.md 2.7, tier `complex`) | Rubric points no line evidences; a model line that quotes a number the fixture does not produce |
| Code drills | every set, the two Labs, the capstones | `zqtest` suites against `reference.py` on CPython 3.11 and Pyodide's CPython, and on MicroPython for `micropython-ok` drills; every `wrong/*.py` must fail the test it names; `web` drills on the pinned NumPy, SciPy and statsmodels | Visible examples on Run; hidden tests at submission; the first failing test mapped to a diagnosis | Unseeded randomness; float equality where a tolerance is meant; statsmodels imported where it is forbidden |
| Exams | `exm.stats`, `exm.ts` | ASSESSMENT.md 3.2 on every form: equal marks, the 20/50/20/10 mix within 5 points, at least 80% of levels, at least 50% of marks from generators, reserve capacity for six fresh sittings; `card.stats.tables` checked against mpmath and statsmodels | Exam engine (ASSESSMENT.md 3) | A form off the mix; a wrong table entry; a pool item seen in homework |

**What verification cannot prove** (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 6.13): that a hook surprises, that a trap catches the misconception it targets, that a rubric rewards understanding rather than vocabulary, or that a fixture "looks like" real data. The last matters more here than elsewhere: tidy-by-construction fixtures can drift into shapes no market produces (a 21% monthly market return appeared in a candidate for `fix.stats.fund-60` and was rejected by choosing another seed). Every fixture therefore carries a plausibility line in its metadata (ranges, kurtosis, autocorrelation) that the owner signs off, and every Lab drill offers the learner's own CSV as a second data set on the web app. The rest is checked by the owner's review of each level before release and by item statistics afterwards: a trap fewer than 30% of learners fall into, or a distractor nobody picks, is flagged for rewriting.

---

## 7. Sample lesson in full: "The standard error and the square-root law"

**Why this lesson** (decision 9). It is the root of both items' dependency graphs: every interval, test statistic and "std err" column in 1.6, and the $\pm 1.96/\sqrt T$ bands, long-run variance and Newey-West errors of 2.2, are this lesson's $\sigma/\sqrt n$ generalised. It carries the subject's most expensive trap, standard deviation and standard error swapped in either direction, and its most important quant fact: a year of returns cannot pin down a mean.

### 7.1 Lesson header

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Id | `lsn.estimators.standard-error` (course ES, level ES1, lesson 2 of 1.6) |
| Length | 12 screens (1 title, 1 summary, 1 explore, 9 tasks) and a quiz of 5 items; about 11 minutes of screens and 4 of quiz |
| Requires | 1.6 lesson 1 (parameter, estimator, estimate); 1.5 lessons 94 (covariance), 96 (variance of sums), 116 (the weak law) and 117 (counts spread like $\sqrt n$); 1.1 lesson 63 (`ddof`); 0.3 level C5 (the standard deviation) |
| Introduces | `skl.stats.standard-error-mean`, `skl.stats.sample-size-for-se`, `skl.stats.estimated-se`, `skl.stats.se-correlated-draws`; terms "standard error" and "square-root law" (embargoed for Bertie until screen 4), "effective sample size" (embargoed until screen 10's Check) |
| Frame (why it exists) | Every number a desk reports is an estimate, and an estimate without an error bar cannot be acted on. The standard error is that error bar for the commonest estimate of all, the average, and it is the unit in which every later test and interval is measured |
| Insight (the aha line) | Averaging $n$ independent draws divides the variance by $n$, so the typical error falls only by $\sqrt n$: precision is bought at a square-root rate, and correlation between draws spends it |
| Misconceptions | `mc.stats.se-vs-sd` (the bait of screen 2), `mc.stats.se-for-individual-spread` [new] (the bait of screen 9), `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` [new], `mc.stats.averaging-removes-noise` [new], `mc.stats.sum-is-n-copies` [new], `mc.stats.sd-adds` [new], `mc.stats.forget-to-square` [new], `mc.stats.borrowed-sigma` [new], `mc.stats.divisor-n-for-s` [new], `mc.stats.numpy-ddof-default` [new], `mc.stats.estimate-is-parameter` [new; lesson 1's trap, given an id here], `mc.stats.correlated-draws-count-fully` [new], `mc.stats.annualise-mean-se-by-sqrt` [new] |
| Worked example with a decision point | Screen 7: a new client's nine orders; which spread belongs in the standard error (the broker-wide 40 lots, or the client's own $s = 30$), then $s/\sqrt 9 = 10$ |
| Derivation | Screen 5 (`derive` with `leap`): $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \sigma^2/n$, where the naive "divide the spread by $n$" fails against screens 3 and 4's measurements |
| Trap and edge cases | Traps: screen 2 (SD where the SE belongs) and screen 9 (SE where the SD belongs). Edges: screen 8 (NumPy's default divisor), screen 10 (correlated draws: $\rho = 1$ is worth one draw; $\rho = 0.2$ caps any batch at 5) |
| Transfer | Screen 11: the standard error of a year's mean return |
| Sources | DeGroot §8.1 [check]; OpenIntro §5.1 (confirmed); MIT 18.650 lecture 1 (confirmed) |
| Lesson-wide tutor guidance | Do not say "standard error", "SE", "square root" or "$\sqrt n$" before screen 4; never state 20, 10, 5, 100 or 9.43 before the Check of the screen that asks for them; prefer "what happened to the spread when the orders went from 1 to 4?" and "is this a statement about one order or about the average?" to statements; never call a p-value or an interval a probability for the parameter (section 1.12's tutor rule) |

The population of order sizes is drawn by `sampling-lab` as a right-skewed curve: a Gamma distribution with shape 6.25 and scale 16 (mean 100 lots, SD 40, skewness 0.8), labelled "order size, lots". Screens use "lots" throughout; every colour cue also has a text label (design research 11).

### 7.2 Screens

---

**Screen 1. Title** (`title`)

- **Heading:** The standard error and the square-root law
- **Prose:** "An average is calmer than the numbers it averages. How much calmer decides how far any estimate can be trusted."
- **Art:** Bertie holding a fan of four order tickets, hair streaming sideways as if in a breeze.
- **Action:** Continue.

---

**Screen 2. The hook** (`problem`, id `hook`, `predictFirst`)

- **Prose:** "A broker's client orders vary in size with a standard deviation of 40 lots around their long-run average. You will see 4 orders today and average them."
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab` in sampling mode with the draw buttons locked until the answer is checked. The top panel shows the population curve with the true mean marked by a dashed line and a bracket of 40 lots drawn either side of it, labelled "one order: SD 40". The middle panel shows four empty order slots.
- **Question:** "Typically, how far from the true mean will the average of the 4 orders land?"
- **Answer input:** `choice`, four cards.
- **Correct:** 20 lots.

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. 40 lots | no | `mc.stats.se-vs-sd` | "40 lots is how far one order typically lands. In an average of four, large and small orders partly cancel. The next screen measures how much." |
| B. 20 lots | yes | | "Yes. Keep your reason in mind; the next screen tests it on thousands of averages." |
| C. 10 lots | no | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "That divides the spread by 4, as if every error were cancelled by another. Only some are. Measure it on the next screen." |
| D. 5 lots | no | `mc.stats.averaging-removes-noise` | "That would mean four orders remove nearly all the noise. Averages help, but far less than that." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "If the 4 orders were copies of one order, how far would their average land? Could averaging independent orders ever be worse than that?" Point: highlights the 40-lot bracket. Step: "Averaging cannot make the typical error larger than 40, and 10 would need every error to be cancelled by another. Choose between the two that remain."
- **Why?** Shown: "Averages of independent draws land closer to the truth than single draws, by a factor that grows more slowly than the number of draws." Then: "On the next screen you draw thousands of averages and measure the factor."
- **Tutor.** Focus: a prediction made before any data. Notes: most learners choose A (the data's spread) or C (divide by $n$); both are the misconceptions this lesson exists for. Reps: picture. Avoid: "standard error", "square root", "√", and the numbers 20 and 2 as a factor.

---

**Screen 3. Draw the averages** (`explore`, id `draw`)

- **Prose:** "Draw samples of 4 orders. Each sample's average drops into the bottom panel. Then change the number of orders and draw again."
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab`, sampling mode, the order-size population, statistic `mean`. Chips for $n$ = 1, 4, 16 and 64 (start 4); buttons Draw 1, Draw 100, Draw 1,000. The bottom panel is the histogram of sample averages with the truth line at 100 and a live readout, "SD of the averages". The learner's screen 2 choice is drawn as a dashed bracket either side of 100. Changing $n$ clears the bottom panel; the readout for each $n$ already drawn stays listed beside it, one line per $n$ with its measured SD.
- **Continue when:** at least 1,000 averages drawn at $n = 4$ and at least 1,000 at one other $n$.
- **Graded:** nothing (decision 3).
- **Tutor.** Focus: measuring the spread of averages at several $n$. Notes: if the learner draws only at $n = 4$, ask "what do you expect at $n = 16$?" before suggesting a chip; when they have two readouts, ask what happened to the spread when the orders were multiplied by 4. Reps: simulation, picture. Avoid: the formula, and the word "standard error".

---

**Screen 4. Predict at 16** (`ladder`, id `predict16`, constraint "Predict before drawing, at n = 16")

- **Prose:** "The spread of the averages has a name: the **standard error of the mean**, SE for short. For one order it is 40 lots. For averages of 4 you measured about 20."
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab` with $n$ fixed at 16 and the bottom panel empty; a draggable prediction bracket centred on 100 whose half-width is also typed in a `numeric-input`. The draw buttons unlock after Check, and the learner's screen 3 readouts are pinned beside the panel.
- **Question:** "Set the bracket to one standard error either side of 100 for averages of 16 orders."
- **Answer input:** the bracket's half-width.
- **Correct:** 10 lots (accepted 9.5 to 10.5).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 2.5 | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "40 divided by 16 assumes every error cancels. From 1 order to 4, the spread only halved." |
| 5 | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "From 4 to 16 is 4 times the orders. Last time, 4 times the orders took the spread from 40 to 20: it halved, it did not quarter." |
| 20 | slip | "More orders, more cancelling: the bracket must be narrower than at 4 orders." |
| 13 to 15 | slip | "From 4 orders to 16 the number quadrupled. What did quadrupling do between 1 and 4?" |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Use your readouts: what happened to the spread when the orders went from 1 to 4?" |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "From 1 order to 4 the spread went from 40 to about 20. And 16 is 4 times 4." Point: highlights the two pinned readouts. Step: "Quadrupling the orders halved the spread once (40 to 20); quadrupling them again halves it again: 10."
- **Why?** Yours: "You set [x]; the 1,000 averages you then drew have SD [measured], against the exact 10." Then: "Each time the number of orders is multiplied by 4, the standard error halves. The next screen finds out why."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the pattern "four times the data, half the error". Notes: learners anchored on dividing by $n$ set 2.5 or 5; ask what 1 to 4 did. Reps: simulation, count. Avoid: "square root" before Check.

---

**Screen 5. Why a square root?** (`derive`, id `derive`)

- **Prose:** "Write the average as $\bar X = \frac1n(X_1 + \dots + X_n)$, where the orders $X_1, \dots, X_n$ are independent and each has variance $\sigma^2$."
- **Leap.** Naive: "Averaging $n$ orders divides their spread by $n$: $\text{SD}(\bar X) = \sigma/n$." Fails: "You measured about 20 at $n = 4$, not 10. Standard deviations do not add, so they cannot be divided out like this." Points to: "Variances of independent variables add (1.5 lesson 96). Work with the variance first, then take its square root."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in fill mode:
  1. $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{1}{n^2}\,\text{Var}(X_1 + \dots + X_n)$ (a constant comes out of a variance squared)
  2. [missing line]
  3. $\phantom{\text{Var}(\bar X)} = \frac{1}{n^2}\cdot n\sigma^2 = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}$
  4. $\text{SE}(\bar X) = \sqrt{\text{Var}(\bar X)} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}$
- **Question:** "Choose the missing line 2."
- **Answer input:** `choice`, four lines.
- **Correct:** A.

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. $= \frac{1}{n^2}\left(\text{Var}(X_1) + \dots + \text{Var}(X_n)\right)$, because independent orders have zero covariance | yes | | "Yes. The variance of a sum is the sum of the variances plus every covariance, and independence makes each covariance 0." |
| B. $= \frac{1}{n^2}\,n^2\sigma^2$, because the sum of $n$ orders is $n$ times as big | no | `mc.stats.sum-is-n-copies` | "That is the variance of $nX_1$: one order copied $n$ times. Independent orders are not copies, so their errors partly cancel." |
| C. $= \frac{1}{n^2}\left(\text{SD}(X_1) + \dots + \text{SD}(X_n)\right)^2$ | no | `mc.stats.sd-adds` | "Adding standard deviations, even squared afterwards, gives $\sigma^2$ here: the copies case again. Variances add; standard deviations do not." |
| D. $= \frac{1}{n^2}\,\sigma^2$, because each order has variance $\sigma^2$ | no | slip | "That counts one order. The sum has $n$ of them." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "What is the variance of a sum of two independent variables?" Point: highlights the sum inside line 1. Step: "$\text{Var}(X_1 + \dots + X_n) = \sum_i\text{Var}(X_i) + \sum_{i \ne j}\text{Cov}(X_i, X_j)$, and independence makes every covariance 0, leaving $n\sigma^2$."
- **Why?** Shown: "The variance falls like $\frac1n$, so the spread, its square root, falls like $\frac{1}{\sqrt n}$: 4 times the data halves the error, 100 times the data divides it by 10." Then: "Independence was used in exactly one place, line 2. Screen 10 takes it away."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the one step where independence enters. Notes: a learner who picks B is imagining copies; ask "can a large independent order be offset by a small one?" Reps: algebra, picture. Avoid: nothing further.

---

**Screen 6. The square-root law** (`generalise`, id `rule`)

- **Rule** (prose): "You found that averaging $n$ independent draws shrinks the spread by $\sqrt n$. To halve an error you need four times the data; to divide it by 10, a hundred times."
- **TeX:** $\text{SE}(\bar X) = \dfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}$, and for a target standard error $e$: $n = \left\lceil(\sigma/e)^2\right\rceil$.
- **Widget:** `plot-axes` with the curve $40/\sqrt n$ for $n$ from 1 to 400, a draggable point on it showing $(n, \text{SE})$, and a `numeric-input`.
- **Question:** "The broker wants the client's average order size known to a standard error of 4 lots. How many independent orders are needed?"
- **Correct:** 100.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 10 | `mc.stats.forget-to-square` | "With 10 orders the SE is $40/\sqrt{10} = 12.6$ lots. Solve $40/\sqrt n = 4$: that gives $\sqrt n = 10$, so $n$ is 10 squared." |
| 400 | slip | "$40/\sqrt{400} = 2$ lots: twice as precise as asked, at four times the cost." |
| 160 | slip | "That multiplies 40 by 4. Solve $40/\sqrt n = 4$ instead." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Set $40/\sqrt n = 4$ and solve for $\sqrt n$ first." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Set $40/\sqrt n = 4$." Point: highlights SE = 4 on the curve. Step: "$\sqrt n = 40/4 = 10$, so $n = 100$."
- **Why?** Yours: "$40/\sqrt{100} = 4$ lots." Then: "Precision is expensive: each extra decimal place costs 100 times the data. Computed in floating point, $(\sigma/e)^2$ can land a hair above a whole number (homework ES1's drill tests $0.9/0.06$), so round with care before taking the ceiling."
- **Tutor.** Focus: inverting the law to size a sample. Notes: learners stop at $\sqrt n = 10$. Reps: algebra, picture. Avoid: nothing.

---

**Screen 7. A worked example: a new client** (`problem`, id `estimated`; the decision point)

- **Prose:** "A new client has sent nine orders: 120, 60, 150, 100, 80, 130, 70, 110 and 80 lots. The broker's 40-lot figure describes all clients over many years. This client may spread differently, and his true mean is unknown."
- **Widget:** `data-table` with the nine orders and a deviations column that fills as the learner computes the mean; three result rows, "mean", "sample SD $s$ (divisor $n - 1$; lesson 3 proves why)" and "estimated SE". Above the table, three method cards for part (a).
- **Question:** "(a) Which spread belongs in the standard error of this client's mean order? (b) Compute the estimated standard error."
- **Answer input:** (a) `choice`; (b) `numeric-input`.
- **Correct:** (a) the client's own sample SD; (b) 10 lots (mean 100, $s = 30$).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| (a) "The broker-wide 40 lots" | `mc.stats.borrowed-sigma` | "40 describes other clients. This client's own orders estimate his spread; that is the spread his average inherits." |
| (a) "The range, $150 - 60 = 90$" | slip | "The range is not a standard deviation, and it grows as you see more orders." |
| (b) 30 | `mc.stats.se-vs-sd` | "30 is the spread of single orders. The average of nine is $\sqrt 9 = 3$ times calmer." |
| (b) 3.33 | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "That divides by 9. The square-root law divides by $\sqrt 9 = 3$." |
| (b) 9.43 | `mc.stats.divisor-n-for-s` | "That uses divisor $n$ for the spread ($\sqrt{7200/9} = 28.28$). Use $s$ with $n - 1$, as pandas and statsmodels do." |
| (b) 13.3 | `mc.stats.borrowed-sigma` | "That is $40/3$, the broker-wide spread. Use this client's $s$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Find the mean first, then each order's deviation from it." Point: highlights the deviations column: 20, $-40$, 50, 0, $-20$, 30, $-30$, 10, $-20$. Step: "The squared deviations sum to 7,200; $s^2 = 7200/8 = 900$, so $s = 30$; SE $= 30/\sqrt 9 = 10$."
- **Why?** Yours: "Mean 100 lots, $s = 30$, SE $= 10$: the client's true mean order is known to about $\pm 10$ lots." Then: "The decision was which spread to use. Using $s$ for $\sigma$ adds a second source of noise, which matters at small $n$: lesson 5 measures it and lesson 22 repairs it with the $t$ distribution."
- **Tutor.** Focus: estimating $\sigma$ with $s$ and then dividing by $\sqrt n$. Notes: some learners divide the variance 900 by 3; ask whether the result has units of lots or lots squared. Reps: count, algebra. Avoid: the $t$ distribution beyond naming it.

---

**Screen 8. What NumPy prints** (`problem`, id `ddof`; edge case)

- **Prose:** "A colleague computes the same standard error in NumPy."
- **Widget:** `predict-output` showing the code below; it runs only after Check.

```python
import numpy as np
x = np.array([120, 60, 150, 100, 80, 130, 70, 110, 80])
print(round(x.std() / np.sqrt(len(x)), 2))
```

- **Question:** "What does it print?"
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input`.
- **Correct:** 9.43.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 10.0 or 10 | `mc.stats.numpy-ddof-default` | "NumPy's `std` divides by $n$ unless told `ddof=1`, so it returns 28.28, not 30 (1.1 lesson 63)." |
| 28.28 or 30.0 | `mc.stats.se-vs-sd` | "The code divides by `np.sqrt(len(x))`, which is 3, before printing." |
| 3.14 or 3.33 | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "`np.sqrt(len(x))` is 3, not 9." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Work out `x.std()` first: which divisor does NumPy use by default?" |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Which divisor does `np.std` use when `ddof` is not given?" Point: highlights `x.std()`. Step: "`x.std()` divides by 9: $\sqrt{7200/9} = 28.28$; divided by 3 that is 9.43. `x.std(ddof=1)` would give 30 and an SE of 10."
- **Why?** Shown: "The two answers differ by the factor $\sqrt{8/9} = 0.943$: 6% here, fading as $n$ grows. pandas and statsmodels use $n - 1$; homework RG2's drill catches this same bug in a regression's standard errors." Then: "When you hand-check code, check its divisor first."
- **Tutor.** Notes: the learner may not run the code before answering; after Check the cell runs and shows both `x.std()` and `x.std(ddof=1)`. Reps: code. Avoid: nothing.

---

**Screen 9. The reverse mistake** (`trap`, id `reverse`, trap `mc.stats.se-for-individual-spread`, bait option A)

- **Prose:** "The broker's report on the new client reads: 'His average order is 100 lots with a standard error of 10. So his next order will almost certainly be between 80 and 120 lots.'"
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab` in population view: the nine orders as dots on a line, the band from 80 to 120 shaded and labelled "100 ± 2 SE", and a faint curve for single orders with mean 100 and SD 30.
- **Question:** "Is the report's second sentence right?"
- **Answer input:** `choice`.

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. Yes: 100 plus or minus 2 standard errors covers about 95% (the bait) | no | `mc.stats.se-for-individual-spread` | "The standard error measures how far the average lands from the true mean. A single order spreads with the SD, 30 lots: three times wider." |
| B. No: single orders spread with SD 30, so 80 to 120 holds only some of them; it is a range for the mean, not for one order | yes | | "Yes. Five of the nine orders lie in the band, counting its ends, and if his orders were roughly normal with SD 30 the band would hold about half of them: it is only $\pm 0.67$ SD wide." |
| C. No: it should be 90 to 110, one standard error either side | no | `mc.stats.se-for-individual-spread` | "Still a statement about the average. One order needs the SD." |
| D. Yes, because his nine orders average exactly 100 | no | `mc.stats.estimate-is-parameter` | "100 is an estimate of his true mean, known only to about $\pm 10$. One order varies far more than that." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Is the sentence about his average, or about one order?" Point: highlights the four dots outside the band (60, 70, 130, 150). Step: "One order spreads with $s = 30$. The band is $\pm 20$, only 0.67 SD, which holds about half of a normal population."
- **Why?** Shown: "SD describes the data; SE describes an estimate. They answer different questions and differ by the factor $\sqrt n$." Then: "Screen 2's trap put the SD where the SE belonged; this one puts the SE where the SD belongs. Reports make both mistakes, in both directions."
- **Tutor.** Notes: most learners choose A; the screen exists to be failed once. Reps: picture. Avoid: nothing.

---

**Screen 10. When orders move together** (`ladder`, id `correlated`, constraint "The draws are correlated"; edge case)

- **Prose:** "This client splits large trades into several orders, so his orders on the same day are correlated: any two have correlation $\rho = 0.2$. In screen 5's line 2 the covariances now stay: each of the $n(n - 1)$ ordered pairs adds $\rho\sigma^2$."
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab` with the population option `correlated: { rho }` [mode to add]: equicorrelated draws built from one shared normal factor, so the population switches to a normal curve with the same mean 100 and SD 40, labelled "normal, same mean and SD". $n$ is fixed at 16, a $\rho$ slider from 0 to 1 is locked at 0.2 until Check, and the independent answer (10) is drawn as a grey bracket. The formula $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{1}{n^2}\left(n\sigma^2 + n(n - 1)\rho\sigma^2\right)$ is shown under the panel.
- **Question:** "(a) Predict the standard error of the average of 16 such orders. (b) How many independent orders is the batch worth?"
- **Answer input:** two `numeric-input` cells.
- **Correct:** (a) 20 lots; (b) 4 orders.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| (a) 10 | `mc.stats.correlated-draws-count-fully` | "10 is the independent answer. Correlated orders repeat each other's errors, so fewer of them cancel." |
| (a) 40 | slip | "That is $\rho = 1$: every order a copy of the first. At $\rho = 0.2$ some cancelling remains." |
| (a) 17.9 | slip | "17.9 is the floor for a very large batch. For 16 orders compute $1 + 15\rho$ first." |
| (b) 16 | `mc.stats.correlated-draws-count-fully` | "16 orders count as 16 only when they are independent. Which $n$ of independent orders gives an SE of 20?" |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Compute $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\left(1 + (n - 1)\rho\right)$, then its square root." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "How many ordered pairs do 16 orders make, and what does each add to the variance of the sum?" Point: highlights the covariance term in the formula. Step: "$\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{1600}{16}(1 + 15 \times 0.2) = 100 \times 4 = 400$, so SE $= 20$: what 4 independent orders give ($40/\sqrt 4$)."
- **Why?** Yours: "SE 20 lots: the 16 orders are worth 4 independent ones, their **effective sample size**." Then: "Now move the slider. At $\rho = 1$ the batch is worth one order (SE 40). At $\rho = 0.2$ no batch, however large, is worth more than $1/\rho = 5$ independent orders, so the SE never falls below $40\sqrt{0.2} = 17.9$. Autocorrelated returns do the same to the error of a mean or a Sharpe ratio (2.2 lesson 10)."
- **Tutor.** Focus: independence is an assumption with a price. Notes: after Check, invite the learner to slide $\rho$ to 0 and to 1 and say what each end means. Reps: algebra, simulation. Avoid: "effective sample size" before Check.

---

**Screen 11. Transfer: a year of returns** (`problem`, id `returns`, context quant)

- **Prose:** "A strategy's 252 daily returns average 0.04%, about 10% a year. Daily returns have an SD of 1%. Treat the days as independent."
- **Widget:** `sampling-lab` with the population "daily return, %" (SD 1, mean hidden), $n$ fixed at 252, and a conversion strip beneath: "annual mean $= 252 \times$ daily mean".
- **Question:** "(a) What is the standard error of the mean daily return? (b) What is the standard error of the annualised mean return, $252$ times the daily mean?"
- **Answer input:** two `numeric-input` cells, in percent.
- **Correct:** (a) 0.063% (accepted 0.062 to 0.064); (b) 15.9% (accepted 15.8 to 16.0).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| (a) 1 | `mc.stats.se-vs-sd` | "1% is one day's spread. The average of 252 days is calmer by $\sqrt{252}$." |
| (a) 0.004 | `mc.stats.se-divides-by-n` | "Divide by $\sqrt{252} \approx 15.9$, not by 252." |
| (b) 1.0 | `mc.stats.annualise-mean-se-by-sqrt` | "The annual mean is 252 times the daily mean, so its SE is 252 times 0.063%. Multiplying by $\sqrt{252}$ is the rule for a volatility, not for a mean." |
| (b) 0.063 | slip | "That is the daily figure; part (b) asks about the annualised mean." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "(a) is $\sigma/\sqrt n$; (b) is 252 times (a)." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "(a) is $\sigma/\sqrt n$ with $\sigma = 1\%$ and $n = 252$." Point: highlights "× 252" on the conversion strip. Step: "(a) $1/\sqrt{252} = 0.063\%$. (b) $252 \times 0.063\% = 15.9\%$, which equals $\sqrt{252} \times 1\%$, the annual volatility."
- **Why?** Yours: "The year's average return is about 10% with a standard error of 15.9%: only 0.63 standard errors from zero." Then: "A year of returns cannot tell a 10% strategy from a 0% one. Pinning the annual mean to $\pm 2\%$ would take $(15.9/2)^2 = 63$ years of data. This is why desks distrust sample means of returns (lesson 76 starts from a prior instead), and why lesson 19 asks the same question of a Sharpe ratio."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the standard error of a mean return, and why it is large. Notes: learners who annualise by $\sqrt{252}$ in (b) have met volatility scaling in 1.1; separate "the mean scales by 252, its SE by $252/\sqrt{252}$". Reps: algebra. Avoid: nothing.

---

**Screen 12. Summary** (`summary`)

- **Three ideas.**
  1. The standard deviation is the spread of the data; the standard error is the spread of an estimate across samples. For the mean of $n$ independent draws, $\text{SE} = \sigma/\sqrt n$, estimated by $s/\sqrt n$ with $s$ on divisor $n - 1$.
  2. The square-root law: four times the data halves the error, so a target standard error $e$ needs $n = \lceil(\sigma/e)^2\rceil$ draws.
  3. Independence was used once, to drop the covariances. Correlated draws are worth fewer independent ones: $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\left(1 + (n - 1)\rho\right)$ for equicorrelated draws, and no batch is worth more than $1/\rho$.
- **Formula card (saved to the Notebook; `derivedIn: derive` and `correlated`).** $\text{SE}(\bar X) = \sigma/\sqrt n$; estimated $s/\sqrt n$; $n = \lceil(\sigma/e)^2\rceil$; equicorrelated $\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}(1 + (n - 1)\rho)$; annualised mean return SE $= \sqrt{252}\,\sigma_{\text{daily}}/\sqrt{\text{years}}$.
- **Common trap.** "SD and SE swapped, in either direction."

---

### 7.3 Lesson quiz (5 items, 1 mark each, difficulty 2 to 3, one attempt each, no hints, Why? after each)

| # | Item | Type | Difficulty | Correct | Diagnosed wrong answers | Why? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Slippage on a desk's trades has an SD of 8 basis points. What is the standard error of the mean slippage over 64 independent trades? | N | 2 | 1 bp | 8 (`mc.stats.se-vs-sd`); 0.125 (`mc.stats.se-divides-by-n`); 2.83 (slip: $8/\sqrt 8$) | "$8/\sqrt{64} = 8/8 = 1$ bp." |
| Q2 | Daily P&L has an SD of £2,000. How many independent days give a standard error of the mean of at most £250? | N | 2 | 64 | 8 (`mc.stats.forget-to-square`); 16 (slip: $2 \times 8$ in place of $8^2$) | "$\sqrt n = 2000/250 = 8$, so $n = 8^2 = 64$." |
| Q3 | A researcher doubles the number of independent observations. What happens to the standard error of the mean? | C | 3 | It is multiplied by $1/\sqrt 2 \approx 0.71$, a fall of about 29% | "It halves" (`mc.stats.se-divides-by-n`); "It stays the same, because the data's spread does not change" (`mc.stats.se-vs-sd`); "It falls by a quarter" (slip) | "SE is proportional to $1/\sqrt n$; doubling $n$ divides it by $\sqrt 2$. Halving it takes four times the data." |
| Q4 | Five executions of the same order type took 96, 104, 98, 102 and 100 milliseconds. Estimate the standard error of their mean | N | 3 | 1.41 ms ($\sqrt 2$; accepted 1.40 to 1.42) | 3.16 (`mc.stats.se-vs-sd`); 1.26 (`mc.stats.divisor-n-for-s`); 0.63 (`mc.stats.se-divides-by-n`) | "Deviations $-4, 4, -2, 2, 0$; squares sum to 40; $s^2 = 40/4 = 10$; SE $= \sqrt{10}/\sqrt 5 = \sqrt 2 = 1.41$." |
| Q5 | Twenty-five analysts' forecasts each have error SD 10%, and any two analysts' errors correlate at 0.5 because they read the same news. What is the standard error of their average forecast? | N | 3 | 7.21% (accepted 7.1 to 7.3) | 2 (`mc.stats.correlated-draws-count-fully`); 10 (slip: $\rho = 1$) | "$\text{Var} = \frac{100}{25}(1 + 24 \times 0.5) = 4 \times 13 = 52$, so SE $= 7.21\%$: the 25 forecasts are worth $25/13 = 1.9$ independent ones." |

The quiz creates the FSRS cards for the four introduced skills (ASSESSMENT.md 5.3): Q1 and Q3 `skl.stats.standard-error-mean`, Q2 `skl.stats.sample-size-for-se`, Q4 `skl.stats.estimated-se`, Q5 `skl.stats.se-correlated-draws`. 4 of 5 or better on the first completion marks the lesson Mastered; any wrong item joins the Redo queue as a fresh variant at the same level from `gen.stats.predict-se`, `gen.stats.required-n`, `gen.stats.estimated-se` [new] or `gen.stats.se-correlated` [new]. Choice items: 1 of 5.

### 7.4 Verification for this lesson

Recomputed on 6 October 2026 [verified]: the hook and ladder values $40/\sqrt 4 = 20$, $40/\sqrt{16} = 10$, $40/\sqrt{64} = 5$; the Gamma population's mean 100, SD 40 and skewness 0.8 (shape 6.25, scale 16); $n = 100$ for an SE of 4; the nine orders' mean 100, $s = 30$, SE 10, divisor-$n$ SD 28.28 and the printed 9.43; $\sqrt{8/9} = 0.943$; $P(\lvert Z\rvert < 2/3) = 0.495$ and five of nine orders in $[80, 120]$; the equicorrelated SE 20, effective size 4, the limit $1/\rho = 5$ and floor $40\sqrt{0.2} = 17.89$; $1/\sqrt{252} = 0.0630\%$, $252 \times 0.0630\% = 15.87\%$, $0.04/0.0630 = 0.635$, $(15.87/2)^2 = 63.0$ years; quiz answers 1, 64, $1/\sqrt 2 = 0.707$, $\sqrt 2 = 1.414$ (divisor $n$: 1.265; $s/n$: 0.632), $\sqrt{52} = 7.211$ and $25/13 = 1.92$. CI adds: screen 3's and 4's seeded runs within 4 standard errors of 20 and 10 at 1,000 averages; screen 10's equicorrelated draws reproduce $\rho = 0.2$ within 4 standard errors and the SE of 20; every number in the tutor fields graded against each screen's check to prove none leaks an answer (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.10); every distractor replayed through the grader with its diagnosis.

### 7.5 What this lesson asks of the widget library

- `sampling-lab`: `predictFirst` on a `choice` (screen 2) and on the prediction bracket (screen 4), both already in the catalogue; per-$n$ readouts that persist when $n$ changes (screen 3); the population option `correlated: { rho }` with a shared normal factor and its label (screen 10), already requested in section 1.12; a population view that draws fixed data as dots with a shaded band (screen 9).
- `derivation-steps` in fill mode with a `leap` block (screen 5) and `predict-output` that runs only after Check (screen 8): both exist in the programming and Stage 0 catalogues.
- `data-table` with a deviations column that fills from the learner's mean (screen 7) [mode to add].
- No widget computes or displays a graded number from a simulation; every graded answer on these screens is a prediction or a value from fixed data (decision 3).

Hours, lesson, item, mark and format totals were re-added. Citations were checked with 15 web searches; ocw.mit.edu, otexts.com, archive.org and the publishers' sites stayed blocked, so confirmations come from search results, not from opening the pages.

**Answers changed.**

| Where | Was | Now | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lesson 35 hook; sections 1.8, 3.7 (CO1 example A), 4.5 (Q15 line 4), 6 (rule 3) | $\lvert t\rvert > 1.96$ "77%" of the time; median $t = 4.7$ | 76%; median $\lvert t\rvert$ 4.5 | $2 \times 10^5$ simulated pairs of independent walks at $T = 100$ give 76.5% (SE 0.1%) and a median $\lvert t\rvert$ of 4.50; 77% sat outside rule 3's 4-SE band |
| Homework CO2 drill | 5% critical value "$-3.34$ to two decimals at $T = 500$" | $-3.35$ | `mackinnoncrit(N=2, regression="c", nobs=500)` gives $-3.348$; example B already said $-3.35$ (the section 8 erratum, now fixed) |
| Homework ES2 drill, test 1 | `trimmed_mean([1, 2, 3, 4, 100], 0.2) == 3.0` | the same values unsorted, `[100, 1, 2, 3, 4]` | On sorted input the known-wrong "trim before sorting" also returns 3.0, so the test could not catch it |
| Homework SM2 drill | `pacf2(0.8, 0.64) == 0` | within $10^{-12}$ of 0 | $0.8^2$ is $0.6400000000000001$ in floating point, so exact equality fails a correct solution |
